The fit of a LogisticRegression is followed by a numClasses request, which its training summary reads, as PySpark's does: the summary is the binary one of two classes or fewer, else the multinomial one.

    Native Pipeline and PipelineModel¶

    pyspark.ml.connect.Pipeline and pyspark.ml.connect.PipelineModel are PySpark's Pipeline and PipelineModel of pyspark.ml.pipeline, natively and in full, as PySpark 4.3.0 has them over Spark Connect:

    • Stages: the stages are any estimators and transformers: the native classes, pyspark.ml's, or the user's.
    • Fit: fit is Pipeline._fit. Up to the last estimator, a transformer transforms and an estimator fits, and its model transforms for the stages after it. The stages after the last estimator are kept as they are, in a PipelineModel of a new uid. As Pipeline._fit, it iterates the stages three times (to check their types, to find the last estimator, and to fit them), so stages of an iterator, such as a generator, which the first iteration consumes, fit a PipelineModel of no stages, as PySpark's.
    • Transform: PipelineModel.transform transforms by each stage in order.
    • Copy: Pipeline.copy keeps the uid and copies each stage with the extra params, while PipelineModel.copy is of a new uid.
    • Persistence: write, save, read and load are of PySpark's Python format:
    • the metadata of pyspark.ml.pipeline.Pipeline (or PipelineModel), of the stageUids;
    • each stage, saved by its own writer at stages/<index>_<uid>.

    As PySpark's writers, a save iterates the stages three times: to check that they write (validateStages), for their uids, and to save them. So the stages of a one-shot iterator (a generator), which the check consumes, are saved as no stages (stageUids of []), and a fit of the Pipeline after the save fits none, as PySpark's.

    PySpark loads what this client saves, and this client loads what PySpark saves, and a pipeline, a validator or a OneVsRest (and its model) that the JVM writer saved (of the org.apache.spark.ml class names, as Scala and classic PySpark's JavaMLWriter write them), also as a stage. A stage loads as the native class of pyspark.ml.connect of its name where one loads, else as PySpark's class. An overwrite deletes the path by the ML helper first, as RemoteMLWriter does.

    The vendored pyspark.ml.Pipeline and the validators of pyspark.ml.tuning fit, transform and save native stages, which are MLWritable (see "PySpark's types and class Params"). A fitted model is a stage of PipelineStage::model, which a save writes by the model's server-side object: a PipelineStage::transformer of a model fails at the save, as a transformer holds no such object. Their loads (pyspark.ml.PipelineModel.load, pyspark.ml.tuning.CrossValidatorModel.load) give PySpark's classes of pyspark.ml, as they give of PySpark's own stages: DefaultParamsReader resolves each stage of its JVM class. The loads of pyspark.ml.connect give the native classes. The native meta-algorithms take an object of a native class as writable, and any other object only if it is an MLWritable, as PySpark does.

    Native validators¶

    pyspark.ml.connect.tuning has PySpark's validators of pyspark.ml.tuning, natively and in full, as PySpark 4.3.0 has them over Spark Connect: CrossValidator, CrossValidatorModel, TrainValidationSplit and TrainValidationSplitModel. Its ParamGridBuilder is PySpark's own class.

    • Estimators and evaluators: any estimator and evaluator, of the native classes, of pyspark.ml, or of the user's. A Pipeline is an estimator too.
    • Fit: fit is the _fit of PySpark's class, on the client:
    • The splits are those of PySpark's: of CrossValidator, numFolds folds of a column <uid>_rand of rand(seed), or the folds of the numbers of foldCol; of TrainValidationSplit, the rows of rand(seed) below trainRatio train. A fold number out of [0, numFolds) fails the fit, and so does an empty fold, as PySpark raises them.
    • Each split is cached while its models are fitted and evaluated, as _cache_spark_dataset caches it.
    • The fits of the param maps run in a pool of parallelism threads, of the estimator's fitMultiple, as PySpark's _parallelFitTasks.
    • collectSubModels keeps the model of each fold and param map.
    • The metrics are PySpark's: the mean and standard deviation of numpy over the folds (avgMetrics, stdMetrics), or the metric of each param map (validationMetrics), and the best of them by isLargerBetter.
    • The best param map is fitted on the whole dataset, in a model of a new uid that gets the params of the validator, as _copyValues gives them.
    • Models: bestModel, the metrics (avgMetrics, stdMetrics, validationMetrics) and subModels, which are attributes of PySpark's instance, are native properties, assignable as PySpark's attributes: vars() lacks them, and del of one raises AttributeError: property has no deleter, where PySpark's deletes the attribute (and its next read raises the AttributeError of a missing attribute). So are PipelineModel's stages and OneVsRestModel's models, which the native members read. transform transforms by the best model. copy copies the best model and the sub-models, and a model of no sub-models fails as PySpark's assert does.
    • Copy: CrossValidator.copy and TrainValidationSplit.copy keep the uid and copy the estimator and the evaluator with the extra params. The param maps stay the same.
    • Persistence: of PySpark's Python format (_ValidatorSharedReadWrite):
    • the metadata of pyspark.ml.tuning.CrossValidator (or the others) and of its params, in PySpark's order. The param maps are of the JSON of each value, or the path of a value that is a stage (epm_<name><n>).
    • the estimator and the evaluator at estimator and evaluator, and a model's best model at bestModel. The sub-models are at subModels, of the writer's option persistSubModels (by default of whether the model has them).

    PySpark loads what this client saves, and this client loads what PySpark saves. A load of a model of a cross validation repeats one list of the sub-models for each fold, as PySpark's reader does. - Checks of a save: as PySpark's, a validator saves only if its estimator, the stages that the estimator nests, and its evaluator write, and each param of its param maps is a param of one of them. A nested validator is refused.

    The native Pipeline, PipelineModel and validators are subclasses of PySpark's classes of them (pyspark.ml.Pipeline, pyspark.ml.tuning.CrossValidator, ...), as every native class is. So isinstance checks of PySpark's classes hold of them, and PySpark's MetaAlgorithmReadWrite reads the nested stages of a native Pipeline.

    Native meta-algorithms (Rust API)¶

    spark_connect::ml has PySpark's meta-algorithms, of the estimators, transformers, models and evaluators of the crate (or the user's), as PySpark 4.3.0 runs them over Spark Connect, on the client:

    • Stages: a PipelineStage is an estimator, a transformer or a fitted model (PipelineStage::estimator(LinearSVC::new())), of any type that clones (EstimatorStage, TransformerStage, EvaluatorStage). A fitted model is given to PipelineStage::model, and a fitted model given to PipelineStage::transformer fails at the save (see "Native Pipeline and PipelineModel").
    • Param maps: a ParamMap holds values of params of stages, by the uid of a stage and the name of its param, as PySpark's Param.parent and Param.name. ParamGridBuilder builds PySpark's grids (add_grid, base_on, build, the last param varying fastest), and apply_param_map of an estimator, a transformer or an evaluator sets the values of its params, as a copy of a param map of PySpark does: each value is converted by the type converter of its param (Param.typeConverter, so an integer grid value of a double param is a double), and a param that the stage's class does not have is not set. A pipeline applies them to its stages, a OneVsRest to its classifier and a OneVsRestModel to its models, as their copy(extra) does, so that {lr.featuresCol: "f2"} of the classifier's uid sets the features of each model. The grid keeps the values as they were given, and a validator converts them when it applies or saves them, not when the grid is built as PySpark does, so a value that PySpark refuses at build (a TypeError) is refused at the fit or the save of the validator. So is a value that PySpark takes but cannot send, which its fit refuses: an int beyond a long (a whole float such as 1e20 of an int param), PySpark's VALUE_NOT_BETWEEN, and a list of ints of an element out of the range of the type of its first element, the AssertionError of its LiteralExpression. A list of ints is sent as PySpark sends it, of the type of its first element.
    • Pipeline and PipelineModel: fit (or fit_pipeline, of the typed model) is Pipeline._fit; PipelineModel transforms by each stage in order. copy keeps the pipeline's uid, and a pipeline model's copy has a new one.
    • CrossValidator and TrainValidationSplit: their fit (fit_validator, of the typed model) splits the dataset with k_fold or train_validation_split and caches each split, as _cache_spark_dataset does (of SPARKML_TEMP_DFS_PATH too). It fits a copy of the estimator of each param map in parallelism threads and evaluates each with the evaluator. The metrics are numpy's (the mean and the standard deviation of the population over the folds), and the best is numpy's argmax or argmin, of the first NaN of any. The best param map is fitted on the whole dataset. Their models have best_model, the metrics and sub_models (collect_sub_models), and transform by the best model. The evaluation of a param map that is not empty transforms by a copy of its model of the map (model.transform(validation, epm[index])), and of an empty one by the model itself. A model's apply_param_map is PySpark's copy(extra) of it: it applies the map to the best model, fails as PySpark's assert self.subModels is not None of a model of no sub-models (so the evaluation of a param map of a validator of no sub-models nested in a pipeline fails, as PySpark's does), copies the sub-models of no map, and sets the model's own params of the map (numFolds, foldCol, trainRatio and seed). A validator's apply_param_map sets its own params (numFolds, foldCol, trainRatio, seed, parallelism and collectSubModels) first, then its estimator's and evaluator's, as PySpark's copy(extra) does, so a validator nested in a grid takes the values of its own params from it. A fit of no fold (a numFolds of no fold with a fold column, or a negative one) is PySpark's TypeError. A validator of no seed has the default seed of its class: PySpark's default is hash(type(self).__name__), which is the same for each validator of a class in a process. This client draws one random seed per class and per process, so its value differs from PySpark's (as PySpark's differs between processes), and the validators of a class in a process split the same way. A model of a validator has the default seed of its own class, and the validator's seed when it is set.
    • Persistence: write() and load are of PySpark's Python format, so that PySpark loads what the Rust API saves and the Rust API loads what PySpark saves:
    • the metadata of pyspark.ml.pipeline.Pipeline (and the others) in PySpark's order of its keys, of NaN and Infinity floats as Python writes them;
    • each stage, saved by its own writer;
    • the validators' param maps, estimator, evaluator, best model and sub-models (persistSubModels).

    load_stage loads any saved stage, of its class: a meta-algorithm (of PySpark's Python format, or of the JVM writer's class names, a OneVsRest and its model too, whose readers check no class, as PySpark's do), or an estimator, transformer or model of the server's class (OperatorEstimator, OperatorTransformer, ObjectModel). The evaluator of a loaded validator is the evaluator of its class. A param map of a value that is a saved stage, which PySpark writes of a stage-valued param, does not load. A fitted model of an estimator has the JVM class that PySpark's _create_model names (the classes of the decision trees, random forests, gradient-boosted trees, factorization machines and perceptrons are their ...ClassificationModel and ...RegressionModel, and QuantileDiscretizer's model is a Bucketizer), and the params of the estimator that that class has, as PySpark's model copies them.

    pyspark.ml¶

    The DataFrame-based pyspark.ml API (pyspark.ml.classification, feature, regression, clustering, evaluation, tuning, stat, Pipeline, ...) is Apache Spark's own, vendored from v4.3.0: estimators fit, models transform, and pipelines save and load on the server as with PySpark Connect, over the Rust transport. These modules build their own spark.connect messages with pyspark.sql.connect.proto, which is the drop-in's own protobuf runtime in Rust (pyspark.sql.connect.proto._rust) in place of the generated modules of Apache Spark, whether or not protobuf is installed, so protobuf is not a dependency of pyspark-client-rust. The runtime has the classes, containers, descriptors and descriptor pool of upb, the implementation of a protobuf install, with their bytes, text, errors and presence (of protobuf 7, where protobuf 6's differ), the methods of protobuf's Any and Timestamp (a Timestamp field takes a datetime), and the modules of the protos with the names of the generated modules (pyspark.sql.connect.proto.base_pb2, ..., and example_plugins_pb2, of Spark's example plugin), whose files in the package are the source that type checkers find next to their stubs. Where protobuf imports, the classes are of the shape of upb's: of a metaclass MessageMeta (which refuses a subclass of a message class, gives the one class of a descriptor and gives the attributes of the nested enums, their values and the numbers of the fields), subclasses of protobuf's google.protobuf.message.Message (and of protobuf's methods of a well-known type, well_known_types.WKTBASES), of protobuf's descriptors of its default pool, to which the files of the protos are added, and of protobuf's EnumTypeWrapper; they pickle as protobuf's Message.__reduce__ pickles upb's (a message of a nested type of protobuf's _InternalConstructMessage, so that such a pickle loads where protobuf imports), and a parse that fails raises protobuf's DecodeError itself, of upb's message (Error parsing message with type 'spark.connect.Plan': Wire format was corrupt), as a serialization of a message that lacks a required field raises its EncodeError. As upb's, a message of more than 100 levels of messages below it fails where upb parses its bytes, of upb's Exceeded upb_DecodeOptions_MaxDepth and of what the parse keeps: in a parse, the load of a pickle, Any.Unpack, MergeFrom, copy.copy, a constructor given a message for a field or a repeated field (Plan(root=relation)), and append, extend and insert, while CopyFrom (of a message of protobuf's of the type too), ==, copy.deepcopy, the value of a map and the mappings of a constructor copy any depth, and the drop-in's own parses of the messages that PySpark builds in Python (Column._expr, a type of pyspark_types_to_proto_types, the requests that session hooks see) take any depth, while the responses of the server are read within the limit, as upb reads them (see What "drop-in" means). Once upb's SetAllowOversizeProtos(True) (api_implementation._c_module) is called, before the package imports or after (also of a reference to the function taken before, which a parse that reaches the limit of 100 levels asks upb of again, as upb tells of no other way), the limit is 65535 levels, as upb's; upb's parser, which recurses on the stack, ends the process past about 50,000 levels, where the runtime's grows its stack. A call of a reference taken before that sets it back to False is not seen once a parse found it set. Of protobuf's pure-Python implementation, the limit is that of its decoder.SetRecursionLimit (100 of none), as of its parsers, of upb's message (where its own parser's is Error parsing message: too many levels of nesting.), as of the runtime's other errors. A parse that fails keeps what upb's decoder keeps of its input stream: a value cut short by the end of its message is read of the bytes that follow it, and of zeros past the end of the data, and set before the parse fails, but an undeclared value of a closed enum of a field that is not packed read past the end of its message, which is not kept; an undeclared value of a closed enum is kept in the unknown fields of the bytes of its field as they are (of a packed field, of a tag of its own and the 64 bits of the value), a length of more than five bytes is corrupt, a field (of a group too) of the number 0 is an unknown field of a message type of no fields (google.protobuf.Empty), and an unknown group past the limit is corrupt (Wire format was corrupt), as upb's. A string or bytes member of a oneof whose bytes fail to parse after a member that holds a message is empty, where upb reads the memory of that message as the string, of the length of a string that the oneof held before, which no program can rely on: upb's SerializeToString writes that memory, a disclosure of the memory of the process, or ends the process (SIGSEGV) where the string is longer than the memory that follows the message (see "A parse cut short in a string of a oneof that held a message" of dev/parity/known-differences.md). protobuf's json_format and text_format print and parse its messages, and unknown_fields.UnknownFieldSet reads their unknown fields, which text_format prints with print_unknown_fields=True (see below). Without protobuf (which PySpark does not run without), the classes are of the runtime's own descriptors and pool, which Python code adds no file to, of the runtime's base of protobuf's methods of Message (_rust._MessageBase), a message of a nested type pickles of its class's qualified name, GetOptions() of a descriptor needs protobuf, and the errors are the runtime's DecodeError and EncodeError. The differences:

    • its types are of pyspark.sql.connect.proto, not of upb's google._upb._message: its metaclass is pyspark.sql.connect.proto._rust.MessageMeta, its classes subclass the runtime's Message (pyspark.sql.connect.proto._rust.Message, whose module is named pyspark.sql.connect.proto, which does not export it), its maps of scalars and of messages are its ScalarMapContainer and MessageMapContainer, of a base MapContainer, and its messages take weak references, which upb's refuse, as the runtime's unset messages and containers hold the message that they set weakly. The runtime's Message has the __reduce__, __getstate__ and __setstate__ of the results of protobuf's (of upb's pickles), which take less time than protobuf's Python ones, and a __copy__, which copies a message of its fields (of upb's bytes past the limit of a parse), where upb's copy.copy is protobuf's __reduce__, a parse of the bytes: so dir() of a class and of a message list __copy__, which upb's do not. The metaclass gives the attributes of a class (the wrappers of the nested enums, the numbers of their values and of the fields) when it makes the class, where upb's binds each when it is read, so that a read of an attribute of a class takes no more time than of a Python class: dir() and vars() of a class list them before they are read, and the AttributeError of a name that a class lacks is Python's (type object 'Join' has no attribute 'nope'), where upb's is of the name only (nope); as upb's, a name of the suffix _FIELD_NUMBER is only the number of a field, not a nested enum or a value of one of the name. The methods of its containers take their arguments as upb's do, positionally (RepeatedScalarContainer.append() takes no keyword arguments), with upb's errors, of the names of upb's classes of the maps too (ScalarMapContainer.MergeFrom() takes exactly one argument (0 given)). A dunder method of a map called directly with the wrong arguments (map.__eq__()) is an exception: its error is that of CPython's slot wrapper of the native type (expected 1 argument, got 0), where upb's __eq__ of a map is Mapping.__eq__, a Python function. So is a keyword argument of setdefault, insert, pop or __deepcopy__ (upb's METH_VARARGS methods) called through a bound method object (f = map.setdefault; f(key="k")): CPython's error of upb's names the method only (setdefault() takes no keyword arguments), as of a bound builtin method, and the runtime's method, which cannot tell that call from one of the map, names the class too. Before Python 3.12, while a trace or a profile function is set (coverage, a debugger), CPython calls each such method as a bound method, and the runtime's error then names the method only too;
    • where Python code that a method of a container runs (an __index__, an __eq__, an iterable) shortens the container, a read past its end raises IndexError and an insertion or a deletion takes the container as it is, where upb reads or writes past the end of its array, which can end the process;
    • a copy of a message into a message that it holds copies the message first, where upb's copy recurses until it ends the process (see "Copies into a message that the value holds" of dev/parity/known-differences.md): a CopyFrom into a message below its argument (an unset child of it too) or into a value of a map or a repeated field of it, and the constructor and MergeFrom of a map of messages, of a value that holds the map, each of what the value was before; and a repeated field of messages extended (or merged) by itself takes the messages that it has first, as a list's extend of itself, where upb's reads the field as it grows. A merge into a message that its value holds, the constructor (a second __init__ too), add, append and extend of a value that holds the message are upb's: upb reads the value as it is when it reads it (it serializes the argument of MergeFrom first), after the fields before it are set and the new message of add and append is appended;
    • the iteration order of a map is the order of insertion, where upb's is that of its hash table, which changes from one process to another (two processes of upb serialize the same Struct of other orders), so no program can rely on upb's. The serialization of a map writes its entries in the order of their keys of upb's deterministic serialization (SerializeToString(deterministic=True)), with deterministic or without it, where upb's serialization without it writes them in the order of its hash table; str() and repr() write them in upb's order of the keys of its text, and text_format in its own, as of upb's messages. upb's orders take the keys of a signed integer type as unsigned numbers, so that a negative key comes after the others;
    • the unset children of a message that Python read (r.limit of an empty Relation) are each detached by Clear (and the clear of ParseFromString), and each made the parsed message of its field by a merge that sets the field, where upb's skip some, of the slots of the addresses of its definitions of their fields in a hash table that its walks change as they go, which differ from one run of the same program to another, and with the modules imported before (see "The unset children that upb's Clear and merges skip" of dev/parity/known-differences.md). So no program can rely on upb's: after its Clear, a skipped child stays a child of the message, and a write to it sets it in the message again (of r = Relation(); s, s2 = r.limit, r.sql, r.Clear() and s2.query = "x" set upb's sql); after its merge, a skipped child is not the parsed message, and a write to it replaces that message;
    • it makes a Python object of each message that it merges into a message that holds fields, where upb makes one when the message is read. A parse into a message that holds no field (of FromString, ParseFromString, a MergeFromString of an empty message, and the drop-in's own parses, as of Column.to_plan) and a copy (of a message of the runtime given to a constructor, of CopyFrom, copy.copy and copy.deepcopy) hold what they read natively, of no Python object, and make the object of a message, a repeated field or a map of it when Python first reads it, as upb makes its objects: of all its levels at once, of a value of up to 256 messages, else of one level, of which a repeated message field makes the objects of its messages as Python reads them, as upb makes each, 64 at least at a time (of an index or an iteration; its other operations, but len(), make the rest first, and the bytes, the text, ==, the copies and DiscardUnknownFields of its message read the rest natively); what a parse or a copy holds natively, a copy of it shares, so proto.Expression(merge_action=action) copies an action of any size at once, and so does a copy of a parse that Python has not read. MergeFrom copies its argument so first, as upb serializes it first, and merges the copy, of which a field that the message does not hold takes the value as it is (so append and extend, which merge each value into a new message, make no object of it either). So the parse, the reads of what a parse or a copy holds natively, the copy of a message that Python read and the release of a message of many messages take several times as long as upb's (of CPU time, of a message of a million messages: 0.08 s for the parse, upb's 0.05 s, 0.41 s for the parse and a read of every message, upb's 0.18 s; 0.17 s for CopyFrom, copy.deepcopy and MergeFrom of a message that Python read, upb's 0.1 s (0.09 s of MergeFrom), none for a copy of a parse that it did not read, and 0.21 s for a MergeFrom into a message that has the repeated field already, whose objects it makes of the copy, upb's 0.09 s; see "The time of a parse" of dev/parity/known-differences.md). A parse holds the messages that Python has not read in slabs of up to 64 messages (of 4, 8, 16 and 32 first), each released once none of its messages is held any longer (the object of a message takes its fields); a message, a repeated field or a map that outlives the container, the map or the message that held it (one kept of a large parse whose other messages are released, or of a field cleared, replaced or merged into) moves the part of the parse that it holds unread, of up to 256 messages, out of the slabs, so that it keeps none of them alive, of a walk of its objects that passes by those that hold no part of a parse below them (those of a value read whole, and those that Python made): 2.6 µs for the pop of a message of 1,022 messages read whole, upb's 0.17 µs, whose messages share the arena of the parse. The runtime keeps up to 64 MiB of the slabs released for the parses that follow, as an allocator keeps memory that it was given back. The parse, the constructor (of lists and mappings too), the copies (CopyFrom, and copy.deepcopy of a message or of a repeated field of messages), the merges (MergeFrom), the extend of a repeated field of messages, the update and MergeFrom of a map, and a read of a repeated message field or a map of messages of a parse or a copy that makes the objects of 1,024 of its messages or more, let another thread take the GIL, and a signal interrupt them with the exception of its handler, every 1,024 steps of the operation (a message that it reads, makes or copies, an entry of a map, or a value of a repeated field that the constructor takes; the read, interrupted, leaves the field as it was), where upb holds the GIL until they end (but in the update and MergeFrom of a map and the constructor of a map of scalars, which run MutableMapping.update in Python, as the runtime's constructor runs it of a value that is not a dict), so another thread can use a message that one of them reads meanwhile. Of a parse, another thread and the handler of a signal see the message that it fills as it was until the parse ends: of FromString, ParseFromString (which clears the message first) and a MergeFromString of a message that holds no field, the message empty, as the parse reads the bytes into native values of its own, which the message takes when it ends (merged with what another thread set of the message meanwhile); of a MergeFromString of a message that holds fields, the message as the merge has set it so far, a field at a time. A parse that the exception of the handler of a signal interrupts leaves the message with what it read until then, as a parse that fails does. SerializeToString, == of two messages of the runtime and the release of a message hold the GIL until they end, as upb's do: 0.07 s, 0.06 s and 0.01 s for a million messages (0.03 s for the release of a copy), where upb's take 0.04 s, 0.11 s and 2 ms. The messages that Python reads of a parse or a copy (of copy.deepcopy too), those that a parse makes within a message that holds fields, and that of a read of an unset field are not tracked by the garbage collector (gc.is_tracked), as they make no reference cycles, so that a collection does not go through the messages of a large message; the message of a constructor, of FromString and of copy.copy, and that ParseFromString or CopyFrom fills, are tracked, as upb's;
    • the classes of Any and Timestamp are the runtime's own, of its modules (pyspark.sql.connect.proto._rust.any_pb2 and timestamp_pb2), where protobuf's modules (google.protobuf.any_pb2) keep protobuf's classes. Where protobuf imports, a message of one pickles as upb's, of protobuf's class (google.protobuf.any_pb2.Any), of the same bytes, so the pickle loads wherever protobuf does, as in the Python worker of the server that runs a UDF that holds one, and in the drop-in as a message of protobuf's class, which the runtime's fields take as they are, as one of PySpark's does; copy.copy keeps the runtime's class. So a message of such a pickle is of another class than the runtime's message that it was of (inherent: upb's methods take messages of their own class only, and the runtime's Any is a message of the runtime): it equals the runtime's, and the runtime's MergeFrom and CopyFrom take it, but its own refuse the runtime's, of upb's TypeError of a message of another class (pickle.loads(pickle.dumps(a)).MergeFrom(a), where a.MergeFrom(pickle.loads(pickle.dumps(a))) merges). Without protobuf, a pickle of protocol 4 or later names the class by the name that the module of the first proto of Spark Connect that imports its file binds it to, which is the class's own (pyspark.sql.connect.proto.base_pb2 and google_dot_protobuf_dot_any__pb2.Any, whose repr the class has), of no other global: it loads in PySpark as a message of protobuf's class (whose generated module binds protobuf's module there), where pyspark.sql.connect imports (which needs grpcio and the other dependencies of PySpark's Spark Connect, as PySpark's pickles of the package's messages do), and in the drop-in of the runtime's, with or without protobuf. One of an earlier protocol, which has no such dotted names, names the runtime's function pyspark.sql.connect.proto._rust._new_message, and loads only in the drop-in, as the class itself does of such a protocol, which pickle names as the attribute of the runtime's module of its file, which pickles by its name. A message of a nested type pickles of protobuf's _InternalConstructMessage where protobuf imports, as upb's, so a drop-in without protobuf does not load it (as it does not load PySpark's), and of its class's qualified name without protobuf (pyspark.sql.connect.proto.expressions_pb2 and Expression.Literal), which loads in PySpark and in the drop-in, with or without protobuf.

    Where protobuf is installed, the files of the protos of Spark Connect are added to its default descriptor pool, as the generated modules add them. The modules that protoc generates of other protos that import them (those of a plugin, such as Delta Lake's Connect client) then build, and json_format finds the types of an Any. Such a module, of a file of proto2, proto3 or editions that imports the protos of Spark Connect directly or through the files it imports, gets the classes of the runtime when protobuf's metaclass builds them: the drop-in wraps reflection.GeneratedProtocolMessageType (and the one of message_factory), which the builder of the modules of protoc 3.20 and later calls, as do the modules of an older protoc themselves, and message_factory.GetMessageClass, and the files are added to the runtime's pool. So their message fields take the messages of pyspark.sql.connect.proto as they are, as protobuf's messages of PySpark's plugins take PySpark's: Delta Lake 4.0's DeltaTable.delete, update and merge build their plans with CopyFrom of the package's messages, of the bytes, text and JSON of PySpark's, with either implementation of protobuf. The runtime reads the presence, defaults, packing, required fields, closed enums, groups and the strings that a parse does not check of proto2 and of the features of editions (of maps too), of upb's behaviors and errors of them. The module of a file of the package of such a file that it imports (of a package: files of no package are not of one) gets the runtime's classes too, also one that protobuf's builder built first (Delta Lake's delta/connect/proto/__init__.py imports base_pb2, of delta/connect/base.proto, of the package delta.connect, which does not import the protos, before the modules that do): the runtime takes over the module, found as the module of protobuf's class of a message of the file, else as a module of the top-level package of that module's name or of the plugin's module whose DESCRIPTOR is the file and that has the descriptors of its builder (_DELTATABLE, which a package that star-imports it has not), as a module of the name of protoc's path of the file (delta.connect.base_pb2) is imported of another (delta.connect.proto.base_pb2), and each name of a global of a module of its top-level package (delta) that is bound to one of protobuf's classes that they replace (the package's, of its star import), but a name bound to another object: the runtime reads the names of sys.modules once of a top-level package, and rebinds the globals of its modules once, of all the modules that it takes over at once. The classes are of the module that protobuf's builder named, as upb's. A file whose module the runtime does not find keeps protobuf's classes. Each file that imports a file that the runtime holds so, but no proto of Spark Connect, gets the runtime's classes too, as Delta Lake's commands.proto would of base.proto alone, of its module built before (of protobuf's symbol database) or after, so that its messages and those of the file take each other's as upb's of one class of each type. The files of other packages that such a file imports (a company's shared proto), and Google's (google/protobuf/duration.proto, google/rpc/status.proto), keep protobuf's classes, in their modules and of protobuf's lookups, as do the files that no such file imports, so that the modules of other libraries that import them (grpcio-status, google/longrunning/operations.proto) take their messages as they do without the drop-in. A module that protobuf's builder built before pyspark.sql.connect.proto is imported, as only a copy of Apache Spark's generated modules of the same protos can, gets the classes of the package (of a proto of Spark Connect) or the runtime's (of a file that imports them) once the package is imported, of the modules of the classes of protobuf's symbol database. Whether a file imports the protos is known of each file once, and message_factory.GetMessageClass of a type of the default pool that keeps protobuf's class looks it up once, so neither reads sys.modules nor costs more than a lookup of a dict afterwards; of a type of another pool, it costs the lookups of its file and pool too. The DESCRIPTOR of such a class is protobuf's descriptor of its type, the module's (_DELETEFROMTABLE), and protobuf's lookups of its type (message_factory.GetMessageClass, symbol_database, and the load of a pickle of a nested type) give the class. The relation of a DataFrame that such a message takes (df._plan.plan(session), as merge takes the plan of its source DataFrame) is a placeholder that holds the relation of the DataFrame (see "SparkSession.client" below), as in the plans of pyspark.ml: the core replaces it when the plan or the command reaches it, so the server receives the relation, as PySpark's plugins send it: also anywhere in the message of the extension of a relation or of a command, of a type that it does not know, as long as the message holds the placeholder as plan(session) gives it, or with the common that the plugin sets on it. "SparkSession.client" below lists the places where the core replaces it: under any relation, in any command that holds one, and in the extension of an expression. What differs of such a module:

    • a message field of such a module of a type of a file that keeps protobuf's classes (of a well-known type other than Any and Timestamp, such as Duration or Struct, or of a file of another package) gives a message of the runtime's own class of the type, of the module and qualified names of protobuf's (google.protobuf.duration_pb2.Duration), of protobuf's methods of a well-known type (ToTimedelta, the mapping of a Struct) and of upb's assignments, comparisons and membership of a Struct and a ListValue, where upb has one class of each type: isinstance(message.timeout, duration_pb2.Duration) is false, and protobuf's messages of other files take the runtime's message only through its bytes or a dict (their CopyFrom, constructors and containers raise upb's TypeError of a message of another class, as struct_pb2.Struct(fields=message.options.fields) does, where struct_pb2.Struct.FromString(message.options.SerializeToString()) reads it), and errors name the runtime's class, though the two are equal (==) of the same contents and the runtime's fields take protobuf's messages as they are. A message of one pickles as upb's, of protobuf's class, so it loads as a message of protobuf's class, where copy.copy gives one of the runtime's;
    • the class of the runtime of such a type pickles as protobuf's class of the type (pickle gives protobuf's class), as its messages do; cloudpickle pickles a class that is not of its module's name by value, which it cannot of such a class, so a function that refers to one does not pickle;
    • the _concrete_class of protobuf's descriptor of a type of the runtime's classes is, of upb, upb's SystemError: error return without exception set, as of a type that upb made no class of (upb gives only the classes that it made), and, of protobuf's pure-Python implementation, the runtime's class, which the runtime sets of a descriptor of no class, as protobuf's metaclass does, but never in place of protobuf's class of a descriptor (of a well-known type, or of a module built before), so protobuf's messages of other files make and take theirs; message_factory.GetMessageClass gives the runtime's class of either;
    • a file that declares extensions or extension ranges, or a message set, keeps protobuf's classes, as the runtime has no extensions, and so do the files that import it (as a file of custom options imports google/protobuf/descriptor.proto): their classes take the package's messages, and those of a plugin's module of the runtime's classes, only through bytes or dicts, where upb's take them as they are. Since upb's classes of such a file take only upb's messages of the files that it imports, a file that the runtime took over (of a plugin's package, or that imports one that it holds) that it imports gets protobuf's classes back, in its module, the names of the modules of the plugin's top-level package and protobuf's lookups, with the files that it imports (but a proto of Spark Connect and a file that imports one), whether the file builds before the plugin's or after (also of a module built after the runtime held its file of a lookup, as message_factory.GetMessageClass of a plugin's type of the default pool holds its files before their modules build): its messages are then of protobuf's classes, as without the drop-in, and the runtime's classes of its types are of the shadows of a file of another package (above). What the give-back does not reach keeps the runtime's class, which upb's classes of such a file refuse (TypeError, where a dict or the bytes are taken): a name bound outside the modules of that top-level package before (from gb.common_pb2 import Common in __main__), a message of the runtime's class made before the give-back, and the values of the plugin's message fields of the types, which are the runtime's messages;
    • the names that the runtime binds to its classes in place of protobuf's are the globals of the modules of the top-level package of the module that it takes over, so protobuf's class of a type that a module of another package or another object holds (an attribute, a closure) stays, as do the messages that it made before, which the runtime's fields take as they are. The globals of a module are read of its __dict__, so that a lazy module (importlib.util.LazyLoader) is not loaded.

    The other way, the messages of pyspark.sql.connect.proto take protobuf's messages of their types as they are, the MergeFrom of their maps takes the items of protobuf's maps, as upb's takes those of any mapping but a dict, and an Any of either packs and unpacks a message of either. A message of the runtime equals (==, on either side) a message of protobuf's of its type of protobuf's default pool of the same contents, as upb's messages of its one class of a type do: the runtime parses the bytes of protobuf's message to compare it, as a field of the runtime takes it, so in, remove and == of the containers find such messages too. Of protobuf's pure-Python implementation, whose == of its message reads the unknown fields of the other (_unknown_fields), a message of the runtime has them, of the bytes of the tag and of the value of each. A message of a type of another pool, of another class for upb too, is not equal, and CopyFrom, MergeFrom, the constructors and the containers refuse it with upb's errors of a message of another class, as they refuse an object that only has the DESCRIPTOR and the SerializeToString of a message. As upb's, == of two messages compares a float or a double field of its bits, so that a NaN equals a NaN of the same bits and -0.0 is not 0.0 (nor the default of a field without presence), and their unknown fields of their numbers, wire types and values, in the order of their tags, so that the order of fields of other tags and the encoding of a varint do not count. == of the containers and maps compares their values as Python's == does, as upb's list and Mapping comparisons do: a NaN value is not equal to itself, and a map is equal to a map of other key or value types of equal keys and values.

    google.protobuf.unknown_fields.UnknownFieldSet is the drop-in's wrapper of protobuf's class, once pyspark.sql.connect.proto is imported, which takes the messages of the package too, where protobuf's class refuses them ("Expected a message object"). The set of such a message is protobuf's set of a message of protobuf's google.protobuf.Empty that holds the unknown fields of the message, so it has the fields, values and order of upb's, and text_format.MessageToString(message, print_unknown_fields=True), which reads the attribute of the module, prints them as upb's. Of other arguments, the wrapper gives protobuf's set or raises protobuf's error. Its sets are of protobuf's class, as isinstance of the wrapper tells, but the wrapper is not that class (type(UnknownFieldSet(message)) is UnknownFieldSet is false), and a name bound to protobuf's class before the package is imported (from google.protobuf.unknown_fields import UnknownFieldSet) refuses the package's messages.

    A DataFrame of a plan of pyspark.sql.connect.plan (DataFrame(plan, session)), such as a plan of pyspark.ml, keeps the plan ID of the plan (plan._plan_id), which its columns refer to and its relation is sent with, as PySpark's DataFrame keeps its plan: the plans of the module take their IDs of the counter from which the core numbers the relations of its plans, one for the process, as PySpark numbers its plans of one counter, so a plan or a relation of any session is never numbered of the ID of another. The plan of a DataFrame of the core (df._plan) has the plan IDs of the DataFrame (_plan_id, _root_plan_id of the root of its WithRelations), and DataFrame(df._plan, session), also of a subclass of the DataFrame, is a DataFrame of the plan of df, in its session or another, which sends it of those IDs, as PySpark's is of the plan object of df: its columns and those of df refer to one plan and resolve against either, and a join of the two is a self-join, whose condition of their columns the server rejects as AMBIGUOUS_COLUMN_REFERENCE, as PySpark's. Its requests build the plan, as PySpark's do, so they pickle the functions of its UDFs, of the PicklingError of one that does not pickle, and raise the error of an option that UTF-8 cannot encode, at each request; the DataFrame is made. Of another plan, which the DataFrame keeps as its _plan, as PySpark's, the DataFrame is of the relation of plan.to_proto(session.client) that its first request builds (see "What a plan build does for a pickle" in dev/parity/known-differences.md), and of a plan whose build raises an Exception, each of its requests raises a new exception of the one of that build, of its class, its arguments, its state, and of copies of its cause and its context, as each of PySpark's requests builds the plan and raises an exception of its own: the DataFrame keeps the exception and its chain of no traceback, so that it keeps no frame of the build alive, and each exception of a request keeps the frames of that request alone. Another exception of the build, such as the KeyboardInterrupt of a Ctrl-C while it runs, or a SystemExit, is raised by the request, as PySpark's request raises it, and the next request builds the plan again. A pickle of the DataFrame, of PySpark's reduce of its _plan and its _session, of a Column of its subquery, of its table argument or of a DataFrame over it carries the plan, as PySpark's pickle carries its plan objects, and the unpickle builds it again, as PySpark's builds them when the unpickled object is used, of the error of that build, of the class, the arguments, the state and the chain that it gives then: the unpickle of the DataFrame raises it, as PySpark's, which builds the plan to read the columns of the new DataFrame, but for an AttributeError, of which it raises the NOT_ITERABLE of PySpark's __getattr__, and the requests of an unpickled table argument raise it, also on the server's Python worker, which builds the plan at each request, as PySpark's does. An unpickled Column of its subquery builds the plan at each request, of the session of the request, also on a thread that has no active session, as PySpark's Column builds it at each request of the client of the request, and a pickle of it carries the plan again. A build of a plan over it outside a request builds it of the session of the client that the build is given, as df._plan.plan(client) and df._plan.to_proto(client) of a DataFrame over it do, as PySpark's builds it of that client, and of the session of the DataFrame for an object that is not a client of the drop-in. Its to_plan(client), and that of a Column over it, raises the error of that build, where PySpark's gives the expression, as PySpark builds the plans of the subqueries of a Column with the relation of a plan, and as the Column that was pickled gives it. The pickle carries the plan and not that of an object over the DataFrame of the plan, so of such an object, as a DataFrame over it or a Column of its subquery, whose plan builds when it is unpickled or requested, the requests raise a RuntimeError that says so, where PySpark's work. The plan of a DataFrame of the core (df._plan) that such a plan holds, as a SubqueryAlias(df._plan, "s") does, pickles of the relation of the DataFrame as it was sent, of the functions of its UDFs, of the error of a plan that does not build and of the Observations of the plan, as a plan of pyspark.sql.connect.plan that has no session, as PySpark's plan object of a DataFrame pickles its fields: so a plain pickle of a Column of the subquery of such a plan, or of its table argument, pickles, and a UDF or a foreachBatch function that captures one unpickles on the server's Python worker, as PySpark's do. The plan that a pickle or a cloudpickle gives back pickles again, plainly too, of the functions of the drop-in's module by their names, as PySpark's plan objects do. The unpickled plan has copies of the Observations (observations), as PySpark's, which an execution of the client that is given them sets; an execution of a DataFrame of a plan over it on the client does not set them, where PySpark's does, as the core takes the Observations of the DataFrames whose plans a plan holds, and the unpickled plan holds the relation alone. The _plan of a DataFrame(plan, session) is plan itself, one object, as PySpark's is, which the DataFrame builds at its first request after it is set, as above.

    SparkSession.client¶

    spark.client is PySpark Connect's SparkConnectClient (of pyspark.sql.connect.client.core, which its package names too), with the public API of pyspark-client 4.3.0, over the Rust transport of the session. It sends the messages of pyspark.sql.connect.proto that it is given as they are, and reads their responses as PySpark's client does:

    • the analyses of a pb2.Plan (schema, explain_string, same_semantics, semantic_hash), the configuration requests of a ConfigRequest.Operation (config, which returns a ConfigResult), and the executions of a plan (to_table, to_table_as_iterator, to_pandas) or of a command (execute_command, execute_command_as_iterator), with the ExecutionInfo of the execution, the Observations that they are given set by their names, and the progress of the progress handlers (register_progress_handler), except for execute_command_as_iterator, as in PySpark. The observed metrics of an ExecutionInfo, of DataFrame.executionInfo and of the attrs of to_pandas are PySpark's PlanObservedMetrics: their metrics are the Expression.Literal messages that the server sent, of their repr, which pairs and flows convert as LiteralExpression._to_value does, raising its UNSUPPORTED_LITERAL when they are read;
    • the registrations of register_udf, register_udtf, register_java and register_data_source, which set their arguments in the fields of their commands, convert the return type and pickle the function in the order of PySpark's, of protobuf's errors for a value that a field does not take (an int is a bool, a bool is not an int), the artifacts of add_artifacts, copy_from_local_to_fs, cache_artifact and cache_artifacts, with the checks of PySpark's client rather than those of SparkSession.addArtifacts and copyFromLocalToFs, the tags, the interrupts, token, host, and the clients of newSession and clone. spark.udf.register, registerJavaFunction and registerJavaUDAF register through the client, as PySpark's do. The executions read their observations as PySpark's: of an object that is not a dict, such as None or an empty list, they set none, and of one that is not a container, such as None, they raise the TypeError of in when observed metrics arrive.

    SparkConnectClient(connection, user_id=None, ...) takes the arguments of PySpark's: it makes a client of a session of its own of the connection string or the ChannelBuilder connection, which is neither the default nor the active session. Its retry_policy, use_reattachable_execute, session_hooks, allow_arrow_batch_chunking, preferred_arrow_chunk_size, rpc_deadlines (pyspark.sql.connect.client.RpcDeadlines) and max_retry_exception_elapsed_time are those of the transport. A channel_options of a connection string raises NOT_IMPLEMENTED, as DefaultChannelBuilder does: the Rust transport opens the channel; as PySpark's, the client of a ChannelBuilder does not read it.

    The members of PySpark's client that its tests and some libraries read are there too. _builder is the channel builder of the connection, a DefaultChannelBuilder of the session's connection string for spark.client (of its endpoint, userAgent and parameters), and the one given for a session or a client made of one. _session_id and _user_id are attributes of the client, as PySpark's: the session ID and the user that the requests carry, which a set changes for them, of the error of protobuf for a value that the string field does not take when the next request is built, while the session's session_id stays, and so do the session ID and the user of the requests of the artifacts, as PySpark's ArtifactManager keeps those that the client was built with. The next response of another server-side session than the one that the client observed, or an INVALID_HANDLE.SESSION_CHANGED of the server, marks the session closed, as PySpark's sets _closed, of an open channel: its artifacts are sent, and it is open again once _closed is set to False. The sessions of the newSession and the clone of the client, and of those of the session, are of its _user_id, unless the connection has a user_id, and so are their artifacts. _server_session_id is the server-side session ID. _closed marks the session closed when it is set to True, so that its requests raise NO_ACTIVE_SESSION but those of its artifacts, which PySpark's ArtifactManager sends of a stub of its own, and opens it again when it is set to False; close() and stop() close the channel too, after which the requests raise grpc's ValueError of a closed channel once _closed is False (Cannot invoke RPC: Channel closed! of an execution, Cannot invoke RPC on closed channel! of the others, and of the artifacts, closed or not), and a newSession is of a channel of its own, as PySpark's. _use_reattachable_execute is whether the executions are reattachable, and _get_operation_statuses the statuses of the operations of the session, of a GetStatus RPC under the get_status deadline. The client and the conf of a session hold it as PySpark's hold its client: once the program dropped the session and the objects that hold it, such as its DataFrames, a kept conf or client raises NO_ACTIVE_SESSION, as PySpark's do once the __del__ of the session closed its client, and such a client keeps its attributes, as PySpark's: its server-side session ID, whether its executions are reattachable and its retry policies among them. The differences that remain are in dev/parity/known-differences.md ("Client session ID" and "The attributes of SparkConnectClient").

    The retry policies of get_retry_policies and set_retry_policies are PySpark's own (pyspark.sql.connect.client.retries, vendored from v4.3.0, which needs grpcio, grpcio-status and googleapis-common-protos). Policies of PySpark's classes DefaultPolicy and RetryPolicy themselves, of backoffs, a jitter and a delay of whole milliseconds, are retried by the transport, without Python: of the parameters of the first DefaultPolicy of the list, its retries, backoffs, jitter and the delay that the server asks for, as PySpark's Retrying retries with them, of the failures that DefaultPolicy.can_retry retries; a RetryPolicy retries none. It reads the parameters of the _retry_policies of the client as each call starts, so a change to a policy, or to the list, applies from the next call on. Any other policies, such as those of a subclass of a can_retry or a to_state of its own, are run by PySpark's Retrying, which the transport makes for each call, as PySpark's client makes one, and asks of each failed RPC, of grpcio's RpcError of it (_InactiveRpcError, or _MultiThreadedRendezvous of a stream of responses): Retrying calls the methods of the policies, and logs and warns as in PySpark, with the GIL, on the thread of the call, or on the delivery thread of the drop-in for a request that the transport sends in the background, and the transport waits the backoff that it gives. The client of a new session has the default policy, as PySpark's newSession and clone do. With disable_reattachable_execute, an execution has no reattach option and no deadline, is never released, and is sent again when it fails in a way that its policy retries, as PySpark's.

    The state of the client is the __dict__ of its attributes, as PySpark's: thread_local, _builder, _user_id, _retry_policies, _max_retry_exception_elapsed_time, _session_id, _closed, _rpc_deadlines, _use_reattachable_execute, _allow_arrow_batch_chunking, _preferred_arrow_chunk_size, _session_hooks, _server_session_id, _profiler_collector, _progress_handlers, _plan_compression_threshold, _plan_compression_algorithm, _release_session_on_exit, global_user_context_extensions and global_user_context_extensions_lock, in that order, of PySpark's values, which the clients of a session share, as PySpark's session has one client, and of which copy.copy of a client makes a dict of its own, of the same values. The transport reads them as each call starts, under the GIL, as PySpark's requests read them when they are built: a change of an attribute, of an assignment, of the object that it holds (client._session_hooks.append(hook)) or of vars(client), applies from the next call on, of the requests of the client of the call, or of the copy whose method made it. The methods read and set them as PySpark's: set_retry_policies sets _retry_policies, enable_reattachable_execute and disable_reattachable_execute set _use_reattachable_execute, register_progress_handler, remove_progress_handler and clear_progress_handlers change the list of _progress_handlers, which the registerProgressHandler, removeProgressHandler and clearProgressHandlers of the session call, spark.profile is a Profile of the _profiler_collector, which the profiles of the executions update, and close() sets _closed, as stop() and the drop of the last object of the session do. The responses set _server_session_id, the _closed of a server-side session change and the plan compression settings of their lookup, and a None of either setting makes the next plan look them up again. The exit of the interpreter reads _closed and _release_session_on_exit, as PySpark's _on_exit does. A client whose __init__ never ran raises the AttributeError of _closed, which PySpark's is_closed reads. The [DefaultPolicy()] of _retry_policies is made at the first read of the attribute, or at a copy.copy of the client, as its module imports grpcio, which the drop-in does not require. The client has none of the attributes of PySpark's that hold the objects of grpcio and of its Python compression of plans, _channel, _internal_stub, _artifact_manager, _release_futures and _zstd_module, which the native transport replaces (see dev/parity/known-differences.md, "The attributes of SparkConnectClient").

    The relation of a DataFrame's plan (df._plan.plan(client)) is a placeholder that holds the relation of the DataFrame, which the core builds at the call, as PySpark's plan builds the relation then: the call pickles the functions of its UDFs, warns of a window of no partitions and raises the errors of the build, and no request is sent. The core builds the relation, and Python has no messages of it, so the placeholder is one message, an extension whose value holds the relation encoded, which Python's protobuf does not parse, whatever the depth of the DataFrame, where PySpark's plan gives the messages of each level of the relation: so its fields are not those of the relation (WhichOneof("rel_type") is "extension"). The core replaces the placeholder by the relation that it holds when it sends a plan, a command or an analysis that holds it, also after the DataFrame is gone, and when the message is parsed again of its bytes, as PySpark's relation is sent. It replaces it wherever PySpark's plans and commands hold the relation of a DataFrame:

    • as the root relation of a plan, of an execution, of an analysis (schema, explain_string, same_semantics, semantic_hash) or of DataFrame(plan, session), and as the relation of the analyses of a relation (persist, unpersist, get_storage_level);
    • under it, as the input of any relation of Spark Connect, both sides of a join, a set operation or a zip and the other inputs of a GroupMap and a CoGroupMap included: those of pyspark.sql.connect.plan, such as SubqueryAlias(df._plan, "s") and the views of its SQL (SQL("SELECT * FROM v", views=[SubqueryAlias(df._plan, "v")]), aliases among the references of a WithRelations), those of pyspark.ml (an MlRelation's input, its model summary dataset and its fetched arguments), and those built with pyspark.sql.connect.proto;
    • as the relation of a command that holds one (a WriteOperation, WriteOperationV2, CreateDataFrameView, WriteStreamOperationStart, SqlCommand, CheckpointCommand or MergeIntoTableCommand, and the flow of a pipeline), and as the dataset of the ML commands (Fit, Evaluate, CreateSummary) and the arguments of a Fetch;
    • anywhere in the message of the extension of a relation or of a command, a plugin's message such as Delta Lake's MergeIntoTable (see the Delta Lake paragraph above), and of the extension of an expression of a relation or a command built in Python, such as a plugin's expression that holds the relation of a DataFrame.

    A placeholder whose common a plan or a plugin's message changes (plan.common.plan_id = ...), as PySpark's relation of plan(session) is changed, is replaced by the relation with that common merged into its own, as protobuf's MergeFrom sets the fields, so its plan ID is the one set, also in the message of an extension. The core does not walk the expressions of a plan built in Python: of each relation of the plan of a kind that holds expressions (a projection, a filter, a join, an aggregation, ...), it encodes the fields other than its inputs, and reads them for a placeholder only when the encoding holds the type URL of one; the relations that the core builds, such as that of a placeholder, are not encoded. It encodes and reads them, and the messages of extensions, only when the bytes that the client gives of the message (of plan(session), or of the plan, the command, the analysis or the request of a hook) hold a type URL of a placeholder that is not of one at the relations of the plan, which the core meets as it walks them: so a plan whose placeholders are all at its relations, or that holds none, costs a search of its bytes for the URL, and no encoding. The messages of extensions are read in a time of their length, however deep they nest.

    The observations of a DataFrame's plan (df._plan.observations) are the Observations of the DataFrame by their names, as PySpark's LogicalPlan.observations: those of observe on it and on its inputs, both sides of a join and of a set operation, in the order of PySpark's dict, but not a DataFrame observed by a name. Each is the object that observe was given, of its class and its attributes, which the plan holds, as PySpark's plan does, also once the program dropped it. A plan of pyspark.sql.connect.plan over it, such as SubqueryAlias(df._plan, "s"), has them too, and an execution of the client that is given them (client.to_table(plan, df._plan.observations)) sets them, as PySpark's DataFrame gives them to its client. An Observation that is not used, given for the name of metrics that the plan observes (client.to_table(plan, {"x": Observation("x")})), fails the execution with PySpark's AssertionError when those metrics arrive.

    As PySpark's, _name, _result and _error are the attributes of an Observation, which vars() shows and a program sets and deletes, and DataFrame.observe of an Observation gives what its _on gives, of a subclass too. Each build of a plan of the Observation, at each request of its DataFrames, reads its _name, as PySpark's CollectMetrics does: the plan is of str(_name), by which the metrics are set, so an Observation renamed after observe is observed by its new name, and a name whose __str__ raises, or a str that UTF-8 cannot encode, fails each request with that error until it is renamed again; observe does not read it. A deep copy of a DataFrame holds a copy of the Observation, of its name then. The get of an Observation raises its _error if it is not None, and is its _result otherwise: one dict, which the executions that set its metrics update in place, as it is then, so a dict read before an action has the metrics once the action ran, what is set on it stays, and a copy.copy of the Observation shares it. _result is None before the Observation is used, which observe checks, so an Observation whose _result a program sets to None is used again, and an execution of its metrics then raises PySpark's AssertionError. The executions convert the metrics when they set them, so a metric that does not convert, such as a timestamp of the year 1 that datetime cannot make local, raises the error of its conversion from the action, and none is set. The error of collecting them that the server reports is given to the _set_error of the Observation, which sets _error, the one exception that get raises. A reference cycle through the attributes, as through the result dict, is freed.

    The errors of the RPCs that PySpark's client leaves to grpc, those of clone (and SparkSession.cloneSession), of copy_from_local_to_fs (and SparkSession.copyFromLocalToFs) and of cache_artifacts, are grpcio's RpcError (_InactiveRpcError), as they are there: of the code, the details, the debug_error_string and the trailing metadata of the failed call, neither converted nor logged, where grpcio is installed. Without grpcio, whose classes they are of, they are converted as those of the other RPCs: they are the exception of their error class, such as UnknownException or UnsupportedOperationException.

    The clients of a session share their attributes, as PySpark's session has one client: an attribute that is set on spark.client is there the next time, and so are its lock, its _session_id and _user_id, and its global_user_context_extensions, the list attribute of PySpark's client, whose changes, and a list that it is set to, the requests of all the threads carry, while a list that it is no longer set to is not read. The thread_local of the client is PySpark's plain threading.local: its tags and user_context_extensions, a plain set and a plain list, exist once a thread uses them, and are the set and the list that the requests of the thread carry, as they change or are set, as the tags of SparkSession.addTag and getTags are; another attribute that a thread sets is its own. As PySpark's, an execution gives a thread of no tags an empty set of them, as the get_tags of its request does, and a failed request leaves inside_error_handling False, as its _handle_error does, but one that fails while the client converts another failure on the thread, which leaves it as it is. A thread of inheritable_thread_target shares the copies of the attributes of the thread that decorated its function, as PySpark's does. The transport reads them, on the calling thread, of the client that it calls, when a call starts its requests and when a finalizer sends its request (the release of the relation of a checkpointed DataFrame or of the cached objects of a model), as PySpark reads them when it builds each request, so a google.protobuf.Any of the lists that is changed in place is sent as it is; the next() of the iterator of an execution reads nothing of them, as its reattaches and releases copy the request of the execution, as PySpark's. The set of tags takes any object, as PySpark's: a tag that the string field of the tags of a request does not take, an object that is neither a str nor bytes, bytes that are not UTF-8 or a str that UTF-8 cannot encode, fails each execution, whose request carries the tags, with protobuf's error, before it is sent, while the analyses, the configurations and the interrupts are sent. The lists of the extensions take any object, as PySpark's: an extension that is not an Any (another message, or not a message), or an item that is not a pair, fails each call whose requests carry the extensions, those of executions, analyses, configurations and interrupts, with upb's TypeError (or the error of the unpacking of the item) before the request is sent, of the global extensions first, as PySpark's requests raise it when they are built, until it is removed, while the calls of the artifacts, cloneSession, stop and the release of a session at exit, whose requests carry none, are made. The error names the runtime's class of Any (pyspark.sql.connect.proto._rust.any_pb2.Any), where upb's names google.protobuf.any_pb2.Any. A copy.copy of the client has a copy of the attributes of its session, as PySpark's: the requests of its methods carry its own session ID, user and lists of extensions, also once the copy rebinds them and at once with its client on another thread, and those of the session, as those of a progress handler or of a finalizer that runs during a method of the copy, the attributes of the session's own client, after which the requests of the method, as those of the details of its failure, carry the copy's again, as they are then. The requests of the details of the failure of a call, of the configs of its stack trace and of the interrupt of a signal read the attributes of the client of the call when they are built, as PySpark's, also those of the iterator of an execution of a copy after a call of the session between two of its next(), and the error of their build, as of an extension that is not an Any, is the error of the call, of no request of the failure and of no interrupt, which goes with a failure that the call handles itself; the other requests of a call carry those of the start of the call (see "SparkConnectClient.thread_local" in dev/parity/known-differences.md). The official tests of the client (sql/tests/connect/client/test_client*.py) mock the gRPC stub and the reattachable iterator of PySpark's client, which the drop-in does not have; those of its retries (test_connect_retry.py) and of pyspark.errors.exceptions.connect.convert_exception (test_connect_errors_conversion.py) pass.

    Spark Declarative Pipelines CLI¶

    The CLI of Spark Declarative Pipelines (python -m pyspark.pipelines.cli init, run and dry-run) and its modules are Apache Spark's own, vendored from v4.3.0, but for those that run a pipeline over the client (spark_connect_pipeline and spark_connect_graph_element_registry), which build its commands in the Rust core, and Apache Spark's tests of them pass with this client. The CLI needs pyyaml, which pyspark-client-rust depends on, as pyspark-client does. The user context extensions of a client (spark.client.add_threadlocal_user_context_extension and add_global_user_context_extension), which the CLI adds for the analyses of a pipeline, are sent with the requests of executions, analyses, configurations, error details and interrupts, as PySpark sends them.

    How parity is verified¶

    Two independent gates in CI cover the two halves of a Spark Connect client - plan building and the transport/result path:

    • Golden-proto tests assert that the spark.connect protobuf plans this client builds (plans, expressions, and every SQL function) match the reference client byte-for-byte. This guards plan-building correctness.
    • The official Apache Spark Connect test suite runs the standard PySpark tests against this client, exercising the transport and Arrow result paths end-to-end against a real server. See the official-test-suite notes and the CI notes under dev/design/.

    Together the two gates cover what the client sends and what it does with what comes back.

    The parity ledger¶

    Coverage is tracked mechanically. scripts/gen_parity_ledger.py AST-parses the reference PySpark source and emits one row per public class, function, and method into a ledger (dev/parity/inventory.csv), so nothing is silently dropped. The current inventory covers roughly:

    Kind Count
    Methods ~1360
    Functions ~620
    Classes ~300

    Each row carries a status:

    • done / verified - mirrored by the client (and, where applicable, confirmed against the reference).
    • n/a - architecture-satisfied: the equivalent wire behavior is provided by the Rust core (spark-connect / -core / -proto), so a reference-private or generated symbol is not mirrored one-to-one.

    The ledger is a development tracking tool (kept under dev/parity/); the official test suite is the authoritative gate.

    What "drop-in" means¶

    From Python, pyspark-client-rust replaces the pyspark-client package: same import pyspark, same public API, same server - see Installation. Use it exactly like PySpark.

    The native Rust API mirrors the same surface, but for the APIs that need Python itself: the functions and classes of Python, which the server runs in Python, that udf, udtf and arrow_udtf of pyspark.sql.functions, udf.register, udtf.register, dataSource.register, Catalog.registerFunction and GroupedData.apply take, where the Rust API takes their commands as Python pickled them, as SparkSession::register_function, register_data_source and GroupedData::apply_in_pandas do, and has UDFs of its own in WebAssembly; pandas, of toPandas, pandas_api and plot, where the Rust API gives Arrow record batches (DataFrame::collect_record_batches); and Python's implicit session, of SparkSession.active and getActiveSession, where a Rust program passes its session. A SparkSession.Hook is a closure in the Rust API (SparkSession::set_execute_plan_hook), and SparkSession.client, which PySpark gives for tests, is the core crate's SparkConnectClient, which a session does not expose.

    Three differences come with being a different package with a native core. The distribution is pyspark-client-rust, so importlib.metadata.version("pyspark-client") raises PackageNotFoundError, where pyspark.__version__ is PySpark's. A traceback of an error that the server raises has the frames of the user's code only, without those of PySpark's client (collect, _to_table, _execute_and_fetch, ...), which the native core does not have. At run time, pyspark.sql.SparkSession and the functions of pyspark.sql.functions, PySpark's own, have PySpark's signatures and docstrings, and so do the functions of pyspark.sql.connect.functions, of which PySpark Connect's copy them too, while the methods of the native classes, such as DataFrame.select, have PySpark's parameters and docstrings, without its annotations. The docstrings of the modules of pyspark.sql and pyspark.errors, of their classes and functions and of the members of the classes, are PySpark's: the types of pyspark.sql.types, the classes and the members whose docstrings PySpark Connect's modules copy of PySpark's classic classes (the SparkSession, the registrations of UDFs, UDTFs and data sources, the Catalog, the RuntimeConf, the GroupedData and the PandasCogroupedOps, the MergeIntoWriter and its clauses, the Observation, the TableValuedFunction, the readers and the writers of DataFrames and of streams, and the StreamingQuery and its manager), and the others, of the modules of pyspark.sql.connect, such as the Builder and the Hook of the SparkSession, and the SparkConnectClient and the other classes of pyspark.sql.connect.client.core, as PySpark's source writes them. The native classes are built with them, of the source of PySpark at the tracked tag (scripts/gen_connect_docstrings.py), but for the __init__ of a native class, the slot of CPython, whose docstring is CPython's (see dev/parity/known-differences.md). The DataFrame, the Column, the WindowSpec, the TableArg, the DataFrameNaFunctions and the DataFrameStatFunctions of Spark Connect and their members have the docstrings of PySpark's classic classes, which inspect.getdoc and help() show of PySpark Connect's, whose own are None (see dev/parity/known-differences.md). The classic pyspark.sql.DataFrame, the public base of the native DataFrame, takes the docstrings of the native class, and PySpark's of its classic DataFrame where the two differ (python/pyspark/sql/_classic_docs_generated.py): of the aliases, such as groupby, whose docstring that help() shows of PySpark Connect's is that of the member that it names, and of the members that PySpark Connect's DataFrame wraps in a cache of functools, such as isEmpty, of which help() shows none. tests/golden/runtime_docstrings.json holds the digests of PySpark's docstrings of every module of pyspark.sql and pyspark.errors, which a test compares with the drop-in's: of the __doc__ of each object, or of the inspect.getdoc of one of no docstring of its own that inherits one, also of the members of the classes of class bodies, such as SparkSession.Builder. A default of an object, which the text signature of a native method cannot hold (inspect takes only literals there), shows as Ellipsis: the StorageLevel(True, True, False, True, 1) of DataFrame.persist, and the <no value> (pyspark._globals._NoValue) of the value of DataFrame.replace and DataFrameNaFunctions.replace and of the default of RuntimeConf.get. Their calls take PySpark's defaults. The __init__ of a native type of parameters, such as ArrayType, is a slot of its class, whose inspect.signature is CPython's (self, /, *args, **kwargs); that of the class shows the parameters, as PySpark's does.

    Type checkers and editors see PySpark's API and its docstrings, as with pyspark-client. The package is marked typed as PySpark's is (py.typed), and the modules whose classes are native or whose names are made at import time, such as pyspark.sql.connect.functions, pyspark.sql.dataframe, pyspark.sql.types, pyspark.sql.connect.datasource, the Avro and Protobuf functions of pyspark.sql.connect, the messages of pyspark.sql.connect.proto and pyspark.ml.stat, have stubs (.pyi) of PySpark 4.3.0's modules, with their annotations, their overloads and the attributes that their classes assign in their methods, which scripts/gen_type_stubs.py generates. The modules of pyspark.ml.connect of the native ML classes have stubs of their public classes, each a subclass of PySpark's class of its name of pyspark.ml, as at run time, of which type checkers see PySpark's API. So mypy and pyright type an application as they do with pyspark-client, which python/tests/test_type_stubs_offline.py checks with both, of each name of the stubbed modules and each member of their classes. The stubs keep the docstring that each of PySpark's objects has at run time (inspect.getdoc), of what pyspark.since adds and of what the modules of PySpark Connect copy of the classic ones (col.__doc__ = pysparkfuncs.col.__doc__), which the test compares with those of pyspark-client (tests/golden/stub_docstrings.json). The differences:

    • The stubs declare only the names that the drop-in's modules have at run time. A name that a module of PySpark imports, which mypy takes for an attribute of the module by default, is re-exported when the drop-in's module has it too, so that the PySparkTypeError of pyspark.sql.connect.functions.builtin, which pyright reports as a private import with pyspark-client, as mypy with --no-implicit-reexport does, is not reported. One that the drop-in's module does not have, such as pyspark.sql.connect.catalog.DataFrame, is an error, where mypy takes it with pyspark-client by default, as it fails at run time. So is the plumbing of PySpark's modules that the drop-in does not have, such as the py4j converters of pyspark.sql.types.
