    • A few names that PySpark's annotations need are declared, which the drop-in does not have at run time: DataFrame.rdd, which PySpark defines when not is_remote_only(), so that type checkers of pyspark-client see it too, and neither client has it (TYPE_ONLY of the test lists them with their reasons). pyspark.sql.types has DataTypeSingleton, the metaclass of the singleton types of the stubs, at run time too, of which the native classes are not. The SparkContext, RDD and the other names of classic PySpark that PySpark's pyspark module imports of pyspark.core when not is_remote_only(), which pyspark-client does not ship, the drop-in's module imports so too, in a block that does not run either: mypy takes them as Any and pyright reports their imports as of an unknown symbol, as with pyspark-client.
    • The classes that the drop-in implements in another way: the internal expressions and plans of PySpark Connect, which annotate private attributes (WindowSpec._orderSpec) and a few internal constructors, and which are Any.
    • The classes of pyspark.ml.connect are those of the drop-in, of PySpark's API of pyspark.ml (see "Native ML classes of the full pyspark.ml API"), where pyspark-client's modules of these names are its ML API on PyTorch, so a type checker types LogisticRegression of pyspark.ml.connect.classification as a subclass of that of pyspark.ml.classification with the drop-in, as at run time.
    • An editor shows the docstrings that the objects of PySpark Connect have at run time, which pyright does not see in PySpark's source, as its modules copy them there at run time: a hover over col of pyspark.sql.connect.functions or the builder of its SparkSession shows its documentation with the drop-in, and none with pyspark-client.
    • The names that only the drop-in has, such as Trigger of pyspark.sql.connect.streaming.readwriter and FrameBound of pyspark.sql.window, are typed with the drop-in and are errors with pyspark-client, as at run time.

    pyspark.sql.functions is PySpark's package, of its modules builtin and partitioning, of Apache Spark as they are. As with pyspark-client, each of its functions is PySpark's, which try_remote_functions (or try_partitioning_remote_functions) of pyspark.sql.utils wraps: on Spark Connect, a call runs the function of pyspark.sql.connect.functions (or of its partitioning) of the name, as that module has it at the call, so a patch of it is what the call runs; with PYSPARK_NO_NAMESPACE_SHARE set, it runs the function's own body, of Spark Classic, which fails in either client (col imports the SparkContext of pyspark, sum the module pyspark.sql.classic). So its __file__, __path__, what pkgutil reports, the __module__, __wrapped__, docstrings and signatures of the functions, their pickles and its names (dir(), __all__) are pyspark-client's, and type checkers read its source as with pyspark-client: mypy run on the files by path finds one module of it, and pyright resolves from pyspark.sql.functions import partitioning from source, where it reports F.partitioning as a private import, of PySpark's __all__, as with pyspark-client. A call costs the time of the wrapper too, as with pyspark-client, which is more than that of most functions of pyspark.sql.connect.functions, of the Rust core.

    The functions of pyspark.sql.connect.functions are the drop-in's, which build their columns in the Rust core: those that scripts/gen_fn_dispatch.py generates of PySpark Connect's functions (pyspark/sql/connect/functions/_generated.py), which run in the namespace of pyspark.sql.connect.functions.builtin, so that they call its lit, column and _to_col as it has them at the call, as PySpark Connect's do, and those that the module writes. As PySpark Connect's, they are of that module, and have the docstrings of PySpark's functions (but date_part, datepart and uuid, which PySpark Connect leaves without one) and their annotations; pyspark.sql.connect.functions.partitioning is PySpark Connect's, as it is. The differences: the frames of a traceback of a generated function are of _generated.py, where PySpark Connect's are of builtin.py; col is not wrapped by PySpark's _with_origin, so it has no __wrapped__ (the drop-in captures the call site of a Column in the Rust core); the module has none of the classes of PySpark Connect's expressions that its module imports (CaseWhen, ColumnReference, ...), nor np, and the private helpers of the Rust core are its own. Its annotations name PySpark's classic Column of pyspark.sql.column, as those of pyspark-client do, of which the Columns of a session are instances (see below). An import of a module of messages, such as from pyspark.sql.connect.proto import base_pb2, has no warning of pyright: its stub is PySpark's (base_pb2.pyi), and the module, which the drop-in's protobuf runtime makes at run time, has a file of the package as its source.

    The drop-in parses the protobufs that a pickled Column or DataFrame carries, and the requests that session hooks see, at any depth that PySpark's serializer reaches, where the parsers of protobuf and prost stop at 100 nested messages: its Rust client enables prost's no-recursion-limit feature for them. It decodes the responses of the server as PySpark's client does, whose deserializer is upb's FromString: of at most 100 levels of messages below a response, counted as upb and prost count them (a message, a map entry and a group are each a level, also a group of a field that the type does not know, while the other fields that the type does not know are not), whatever the features of prost. The client scans the bytes of each response for the levels of their messages, with a stack of its own, before prost decodes them; so does SparkSession.client of the messages of the responses that it parses (of schema, config, to_table, the results of commands and the statuses of operations). Prost then decodes them as before, and refuses some bytes that upb reads, which a server of Spark does not send: a field that the type knows of another wire type (which upb keeps as a field that the type does not know), a varint that does not end and a tag of 0. Of protobuf's pure-Python implementation, the limit is that of its decoder.SetRecursionLimit (100 of none), counted as upb counts it, where its decoder counts the groups of the fields that a type does not know from 0 in each message, and stops at the limit itself: so a response of such groups exactly 100 levels deep fails in PySpark, and one of a message at level 92 to 100 with 10 levels of such groups below it is read in PySpark, where the drop-in reads the first and refuses the second (a server, of proto3, sends no groups). So a column of a struct nested 31 levels deep is read, and of one nested 32 levels deep the schema, collect(), toPandas() and the observed metrics (of collect(), toPandas(), executionInfo and the Observation's get) fail, as in PySpark, without a crash at any depth (of a thread of a small stack too, see below). A response that is deeper, or that does not parse, raises PySpark's error of a response that gRPC's deserializer refuses, of each kind of call (an analysis, a configuration request, the status of operations, an execution and its reattachment): the SparkConnectGrpcException of the status INTERNAL, of the text of grpc's error of the call (_InactiveRpcError, or _MultiThreadedRendezvous of an execution) whose details are "Exception deserializing response!" (and whose debug_error_string is None), where the error of the Rust API is prost's error of its limit ([Internal error] failed to decode Protobuf message: DataType.kind: ...: recursion limit reached, of the fields that lead past the limit): the codec of the client tags its own failure, so a status of the server of the same text (of a server of tonic that fails to decode a request) is the server's error. Once upb's SetAllowOversizeProtos(True) is called, also before the drop-in's protobuf runtime is imported (a response past 100 levels asks upb for its limit), a response of up to 65535 levels is read, as upb reads it, decoded on a stack that grows for its levels, and a deeper one raises the same error, where upb's parser, which recurses on the stack, ends PySpark's process of a schema or a literal of 100,000 levels. As of the runtime's own parses, a call of a reference to the function taken before the runtime is installed that sets it back to False is not seen once a response found it set. Within the limit of 65535 levels, a response whose messages of the fields that their types have (which the decoded response keeps) nest more than 1024 levels deep raises the client's RecursionError ("the messages of a response may be nested at most 1024 levels deep"), which bounds the stack that the conversions, copies and drops of them take. PySpark converts the schemas and the literals of its responses in Python, up to Python's recursion limit: it raises its own RecursionError of the schema of DataFrame.schema (of its copy), of collect() and of toPandas(), and of the literals of the observed metrics, from a few hundred levels (about 650 of arrays, 370 of structs), so that these are read by the drop-in only from there to 1024 levels and fail in both past it; it reads the schemas that SparkSession.client's schema and to_table_as_iterator return up to about 1,000 nested types (2,000 levels of arrays, 3,000 of structs), and the messages that it keeps as they are, such as the relation of the SqlCommandResult of spark.sql, of 10,000 levels too, where the drop-in raises its RecursionError past 1024 levels. The waits of the client for the server, whose futures nest those of tonic, hyper, the deadlines and the retries (of frames of tens of KiB), and its decodes, copies and conversions of the responses, run on stacks with room for them, of a thread of a small stack (of threading.stack_size) too: a call on a thread of 64 KiB reads its response, as PySpark's does, and one on a thread of 256 KiB reads a response at that bound. So do the types that the calls return, and the types of as many levels that the user makes: they are made, compared, written, copied and lowered for the requests on stacks that grow with their levels, so that their ==, repr(), json(), jsonValue(), fromJson, simpleString(), treeString(), toNullable(), needConversion() and hash() give on a thread of 128 KiB what they give on the main thread. copy.deepcopy and pickling copy and pickle the __dict__s of the types as PySpark's do, of the levels of the frames of Python's copy and pickle: they raise their RecursionError from about 100 and 125 nested structs on the main thread and on a thread of 256 KiB, as PySpark's do. On a thread of 128 KiB, pickling overflows from there, as PySpark's does, and copy.deepcopy, whose copier of the native classes grows its stack (SPARK-62287, see below), raises its RecursionError where PySpark's overflows. On the main thread, json(), jsonValue() and fromJson raise the RecursionError of Python's json module of a JSON of more than about 1,000 levels (of about 330 nested structs), and the walks of the type objects raise theirs past Python's recursion limit of their levels, a level for each struct and each field: ==, treeString(), hash() and repr() from about 500 nested structs, and simpleString() and toNullable() from about 1,000, of more of a higher limit (sys.setrecursionlimit), as PySpark's walks take. On Python 3.11, PySpark's raise their RecursionError from about 100 nested structs (copy.deepcopy), 125 (pickling), 145 (==), 165 (hash() and repr()), 250 (json() and simpleString()) and 330 (treeString()), and its repr() overflows a thread of 256 KiB from about 150. The grpc-status-details-bin of a failure is read within the same limit, as upb reads it where PySpark's rpc_status.from_call does: a google.rpc.Status nested past it, as of 101 nested groups of a field that the type does not know, or of 100 in a detail, which is a level of its own, raises protobuf's DecodeError (Error parsing message with type 'google.rpc.Status': Wire format was corrupt) in both clients, whatever its code and message, as details that are not a google.rpc.Status do (see below). The server is trusted, as SECURITY.md sets out, so the limit is not a bound on the size of the responses.

    A type that holds itself, as a struct whose fields hold a field of it (an append to its fields, or add), an array or a map set to be its own element or value, or a field set to the type of the struct that holds it, has levels that no walk ends. As PySpark's methods, which walk the levels in Python, each use that reads them raises RecursionError, of the bound of the walks by Python's recursion limit: json(), jsonValue(), simpleString(), repr(), hash(), toNullable(), treeString() of more levels than the limit, and the uses that convert the type, such as createDataFrame, from_json, udf and the readers' schema. The others work as PySpark's: == of the type itself, needConversion() of a struct, the constructors and the assignments that keep it (StructField("x", s), ArrayType(s)), treeString(maxDepth) of the levels that it prints, and pickles and copies, which keep the cycle (of the class and the __dict__ of the type, as PySpark pickles it). A cast and DataFrame.to return their Column and DataFrame, as PySpark's do, whose repr and requests raise the error. Of an array or a map that holds itself, needConversion() raises it, and so does a StructType of a field of it, whose constructor reads it, as PySpark's does. The text of the error may differ from PySpark's (see "The message of the RecursionError of a type that holds itself" in dev/parity/known-differences.md).

    The error of grpcio of a response that the client fails to decode, of the details "Exception deserializing response!" and no debug_error_string, is also the one that PySpark's client sees elsewhere: the error that a retry policy is asked of (its details(), and its debug_error_string(), None), the error that the GRPC Error received log gives, the warning of a release that fails in the background (ReleaseExecute failed with exception: ...), and the error of a call that PySpark leaves to grpc (cloneSession(), the uploads of copyFromLocalToFs and of the caching of artifacts). As grpcio's, the debug_error_string() of such an error of a stream, _MultiThreadedRendezvous, waits forever for a debug string that nothing sets; a policy that the client asks of a stream that it reads in the background, as of the events of the streaming query listeners or of the removal of a model, and the log of such an error, run on a thread of their own (pyspark-apart), so that such a wait holds only its call, as in grpcio, and not the delivery thread of the client, which runs the Python of every other request in the background. A call that the client makes in the background awaits the log of its error (GRPC Error received, and Received ErrorInfo) before it goes on with it, so that the log precedes what follows, such as the warning that the listener bus ends with, as PySpark's handler thread logs the error before it warns. The query context loggers log grpcio's error of the call that failed as the exception that PySpark handles as it builds its exception, of its text: _InactiveRpcError of a call of one response, such as AnalyzePlan, and _MultiThreadedRendezvous of a stream, of the debug_error_string of grpcio 1.85, the name of the code and the details, where it was a _MultiThreadedRendezvous of either, of the debug_error_string of an older grpcio (UNKNOWN:Error received from peer {grpc_message:...}).

    The drop-in also sends a plan that PySpark's client cannot encode: from about 50 nested expressions, PySpark raises upb's DecodeError: Exceeded upb_DecodeOptions_MaxDepth before it sends the plan, where the drop-in sends it, and the server runs it within its own limits. The commands of DataFrameWriterV2 and of MergeIntoWriter are PySpark's, over the drop-in's message of each Column, which holds an expression of any depth, but PySpark's command of DataFrameWriterV2 extends its repeated field with the messages of the partitioning columns of partitionedBy, and its merge makes the messages of its command and of its actions of those of its conditions and assignments, which protobuf copies of their parse: one of more than 100 levels, such as a chain of about 50 +, raises that DecodeError, as PySpark's does, where of one of about 500 + or more PySpark's to_plan, which recurses, raises Python's RecursionError first (see "A deep Column in the commands of writeTo and mergeInto" in dev/parity/known-differences.md). The server parses a request of up to spark.connect.grpc.marshallerRecursionLimit nested messages (1024), so 508 nested abs of a select run and 509 fail, with the SparkConnectGrpcException of UNKNOWN (Application error processing RPC), and its analyzer resolves a plan in up to spark.sql.analyzer.maxIterations (100) iterations, so a chain of 98 + fails with its AnalysisException ("Max iterations (100) reached for batch Resolution"). A Column of more than 745 levels raises a RecursionError when it is pickled, of the drop-in's own message (an expression may be at most 745 levels deep). On Python 3.11, PySpark's pickler raises one from about 200 levels, under Python's default recursion limit, so a Column of about 200 to 745 levels pickles in the drop-in only. copy.copy and copy.deepcopy of a Column share its expression, of which a deep copy makes again only the levels above the UDFs whose functions it copies, so they copy a Column of any depth, as PySpark's copy.copy does; PySpark's copy.deepcopy raises a RecursionError from about 165 levels.

    The frame bounds of Window.rowsBetween and rangeBetween are converted when the plan is built, as in PySpark: the window keeps them in PySpark's WindowFrame as they are, so a bound of a row frame beyond an int, such as a start of -2**40 or of Window.unboundedFollowing, a bound of True, which protobuf's int field refuses, and an int beyond a long make a window that shows them as they are, as does the repr of a window expression of it (F.sum("x").over(w)), of which each request of a plan raises PySpark's error, of the start and then of the end (VALUE_NOT_BETWEEN, the TypeError of protobuf, Value out of range). The type of cast and try_cast is converted when the plan is built, as in PySpark: a StructField, the base DataType(), an abstract type such as AtomicType() and a Python subclass of them, also one nested in the type, make a Column that renders as the simpleString of the type, and whose plans raise PySpark's UNSUPPORTED_OPERATION.

    Column.alias and Column.name raise PySpark's errors when PySpark's ColumnAlias raises them. At the call, they raise the metadata of several names (ONLY_ALLOWED_FOR_SINGLE_COLUMN) and other keywords. When a request builds the plan, they raise: - a name that protobuf refuses (a TypeError, or the UnicodeEncodeError or UnicodeDecodeError of UTF-8); - metadata of one name that json.dumps refuses; - metadata of no names (CANNOT_PROVIDE_METADATA).

    So a Column that is not used raises none of these. The __repr__ of an alias of a name that is not a str (bytes too) raises the TypeError of PySpark's join of the names. A Column of such an alias copies and pickles, and its copy raises the same errors, also on a Python worker of the server. A str of lone surrogates, of a name or of another value of a Column (see below), shows escaped in the __repr__ of the Column (a\ud800b), where PySpark's shows it as it is: in upper case (\UD800) in the type of a cast, which the __repr__ upper-cases, and escaped twice in a list literal of Column.otherwise or getItem, which shows the repr of its strs.

    The literal of a bytearray is of the bytes that it holds when the Column is made, which its __repr__ shows as PySpark's (Column<'bytearray(b'xy')'>), so a change to the bytearray after lit is in neither the __repr__ nor the plan, where PySpark's literal keeps the bytearray and shows and sends what it holds at the time. The literals of the core are immutable bytes, so its plans, pickles and copies never share a mutable Python object.

    An Observation or a SparkSession.Hook, of the class or of a subclass (which a module defines in both clients), that PySpark pickled at protocol 0 or 1 raises a TypeError when the drop-in unpickles it, as CPython's copyreg._reconstructor cannot create the drop-in's, a class with a native base; protocols 2 and up, pickle's default, load in both clients, of the class or the subclass, and PySpark loads the drop-in's at every protocol. A hook of a subclass with __slots__ pickles at protocols 0 and 1 too, where PySpark's raises the TypeError of a class that defines __slots__ without __getstate__. A StorageLevel pickles at every protocol, and PySpark loads the drop-in's; one that PySpark pickled at 0 or 1 raises TypeError: object.__new__(StorageLevel) is not safe, use StorageLevel.__new__() when the drop-in unpickles it, and at the protocols 2 and up both clients load each other's.

    So it is for the data types. PySpark's are plain classes, which pickle as their class and their __dict__: at the protocols 2 and up the drop-in makes its type of the class, without arguments, and sets the attributes, so a pickled df.schema loads with its fields, names, metadata, collations, nullable flags and parameters, as PySpark's types of them. At protocol 0 or 1 the drop-in raises TypeError: object.__new__(DecimalType) is not safe, use DecimalType.__new__(). A type of parameters (a decimal, a string, a char, a varchar, a time, an interval of fields or a spatial type), an array, a map, a field and a struct pickle, copy and deep-copy as PySpark's plain class does, as copyreg.__newobj__(cls) and their __dict__, which holds their parameters (see below), also at the protocols 0 and 1, which the Python workers of the server load at every protocol: the pickle of a type of parameters is PySpark's own, byte for byte. The drop-in's other types pickle, copy and deep-copy as _parse_datatype_json_string of their JSON. So the copies of a struct, and of an array or a map of one, keep the metadata dicts of its fields as they are, which JSON does not (a tuple, a key that is not a str, the order of the keys), and are equal to it, as PySpark's pickle of the __dict__ is, and toNullable() makes each field of the dict of the field, as PySpark's metadata=field.metadata. A type of no parameters of an attribute that a user set pickles and copies as its class and PySpark's __dict__ of it, as a type of a Python subclass does (see below), also in the types that hold it. A shallow copy (copy.copy) of a struct, an array, a map or a field is of its class and the same attribute objects, as PySpark's copy of the __dict__, at any depth: a struct's copy shares its fields, names and _needConversion lists. copy.deepcopy of an object of a native class of the types itself is made by a copier of the extension, of no Python code of each object (SPARK-62287). pyspark.sql.types registers it in copy._deepcopy_dispatch, the dict of the copiers of copy.deepcopy by exact class, for the native classes themselves only: the 13 singletons, the types of parameters, StructField, ArrayType, MapType and StructType. No attribute of the classes is added, so their vars() and dir() are PySpark's, and an object of a subclass, which the dispatch does not hold, is copied of Python's protocol. The copier makes the copies that Python's copy.deepcopy makes of PySpark's plain classes, of copyreg.__newobj__ and _reconstruct, and the same entries of the memo and of its list of _keep_alive, in the same order, also of a memo that is not a dict and of one that holds an object already. It falls back to Python's protocol, as copy.deepcopy runs it past its dispatch, of an object of a native class patched over a method of the protocol, or of a class of its MRO patched so (__deepcopy__, __reduce_ex__, __reduce__, __getstate__, __setstate__, __new__, __getattribute__, __getattr__, __slotnames__, or the json and jsonValue that a singleton's reduce reads), of an entry of copyreg.dispatch_table for its class, of a pyspark.sql.types._parse_datatype_json_string rebound, of which a singleton's reduce is, or of such a hook in its own __dict__. It counts the frames of Python's copy as the levels of its recursion, of Python's limit up to Python 3.11, which takes the levels of C calls with Python's frames; from Python 3.12, which counts them apart, of the limit of C calls, so a type of more levels than Python's limit copies where Python's protocol raises its RecursionError. The class's own types of no parameters compare without the attributes that a user set, where PySpark's comparison of the __dict__s holds them: a VariantType() of an attribute equals VariantType(), where a type of parameters, an array, a map, a field, a struct and a type of a Python subclass compare them, of their __dict__s (see below).

    createDataFrame of a pandas DataFrame of rows under a struct converts it with PySpark's conversion where PyArrow is, also of a struct of a Python subclass that reads its fields of its own, and zips its columns with the fields, as that conversion does: the columns past the fields are dropped, and not converted, and of a struct of no fields the rows of a frame of columns too, as the table of the zip has no column and no row; of fewer columns than the fields, it raises the error of the conversion of a column that it zips with a field, if any, and then PyArrow's ArrowInvalid of the rename of its table to the names of the fields (tried to rename a table of 1 columns but only 2 names were provided). A frame of no columns is the table of pa.RecordBatch.from_pandas, of its rows: of a RangeIndex, of no column, so pd.DataFrame(index=range(2)) is two Row() under StructType([]); of another index, of a column of each level of the index, which the rename takes, so pd.DataFrame(index=[0]) is Row(a=0) under a long, and raises AXIS_LENGTH_MISMATCH under StructType([]), of one more column than the fields. Without PyArrow, the frame takes the row path, which zips the columns with the fields too, but raises AXIS_LENGTH_MISMATCH of a frame of fewer columns than the fields, and does not take the columns of an index (see "pandas DataFrames without PyArrow" in dev/parity/known-differences.md).

    PySpark's conversion of a pandas DataFrame, of a struct or without a schema, runs of the conversions and the types that the module of the session, pyspark.sql.connect.session, binds when it is imported, with the first session, as PySpark Connect's module binds them, in PySpark's order, and its conversion of each column imports to_arrow_type of the converter module of sys.modules when it is called, as PySpark's does. So a type name rebound in pyspark.sql.types or pyspark.sql.pandas.types before the first session, in that module, or in the converter module, changes the conversion as it changes PySpark's, of its values and errors, and a public name rebound after the first session, with the converter module removed, only the conversion of each column: a public BooleanType rebound to LongType so makes [1, 0, 2] under a long [1, 0, 1], of the bools of the column that the cast to the schema takes back to longs. A struct whose classes are patched converts with PySpark's conversion too, which reads the patch, and createDataFrame, of any data, checks its schema, of a DDL string as the server parses it, against the AtomicType and the StructType of that module before it reads any setting, as PySpark's does, of PySpark's TypeError of an isinstance of a name that is not a class, and of its NOT_EXPECTED_TYPE of a schema that they do not take. What differs, of a patched property that PySpark's __init__ assigns, and of the other modules of PySpark Connect, which bind the names with the session too, is in "pandas DataFrames under customized or patched types, or rebound type names" and "Type names that PySpark Connect's other modules and PySpark's types read" in dev/parity/known-differences.md.

    A StructType caches its names as it is made and as add adds a field, as PySpark's, so an in-place change of its fields list, such as a StructField appended, leaves names and fieldNames() as they were. createDataFrame takes local rows, and the struct values below the top level, apart by those names, as PySpark's conversion reads fieldNames(), and converts their values by position with the fields as they are: a row of another number of values than the names raises AXIS_LENGTH_MISMATCH, a dict is read by them, and a field past them has no value (IndexError); below the top level, a value past the fields and fewer names than fields raise IndexError too. A pandas DataFrame is named with toDF(*schema.fieldNames()), of which the server raises for another number of names than columns.

    createDataFrame under a struct whose fields hold an element that is not a StructField, as s.fields.append("x") gives, at any level (in a struct inside it changed in place, also of a struct, an array or a map that is itself an element of fields, and in the sqlType() of a UserDefinedType), is PySpark's, of any data; the width of a pandas DataFrame is zipped with the fields as of any struct (see above). It converts the data as PySpark's conversion does, of its errors: of local rows or a pyarrow Table, it reads the fields, of the AttributeError of the first attribute that an element lacks, and takes local rows apart by the cached names first, of AXIS_LENGTH_MISMATCH; of a pandas DataFrame of rows, it deduplicates the names of the fields first (_deduplicate_field_names), of the IndexError of an element past the cached names; and of an array of rows, it reads no field and checks the width of the array. It then writes the json() of the schema object as PySpark's does, of no rows too, of the jsonValue of each element of the fields of its structs in their order, so it raises the AttributeError of an element of none, and of an element that has one sends the converted data of that JSON, with the names of the struct of a pandas DataFrame (toDF). A field of such a struct writes the collations of the strings of its arrays and maps into its metadata (__COLLATIONS), and its type without them, as PySpark's StructField.jsonValue, which reads the attributes of the types in its order, so a type of a Python subclass of a property of its own, such as a dataType that makes a new struct at each read, is written of what each read gives.

    The other uses of such a struct read its elements as PySpark's StructType reads them, of each element as it is: of one that lacks the attribute that a use reads, its AttributeError, and of one that has it, the use of it. json() and jsonValue() write the jsonValue of each element, as above; simpleString() joins the simpleString() of each element; treeString() calls the _build_formatted_string of each element, as PySpark's treeString of its StringConcat; a lookup by name compares the name of each element, and a slice is StructType(...) of the elements, of the errors of its __init__; add appends the field and its name and then reads the needConversion() of each element; and toNullable() reads the name, the dataType and the metadata of each element, in that order. An assignment of fields takes any elements; pickles and copies keep them, as PySpark's pickle of the __dict__ of the struct (a shallow copy shares the list); and a StructField, an ArrayType and a MapType of such a struct keep it as it is. The plans of a UDF, of a map or group function and of transformWithState and transformWithStateInPandas send the type that PySpark's pyspark_types_to_proto_types reads of the elements when the request builds the plan, of each its name, dataType, nullable and metadata (of a sibling field of a type that it has no proto of, such as AtomicType(), its UNSUPPORTED_OPERATION), and each request raises the error of that. So do a cast and DataFrame.to, as PySpark's CastExpression and ToSchema keep the object and read it when the plan is built, of the struct as it is then, and the repr of the cast shows the simpleString() of the object in upper case as it is when it renders, of the error of that then, as PySpark's __repr__: the call reads nothing of the object. from_json, a reader's schema and catalog.createTable read the json() or the elements of the object at the call, as PySpark's. A struct whose fields hold the struct itself has levels that no walk ends, and its uses raise RecursionError as PySpark's do (see "A type that holds itself" above).

    The parameters of a type of parameters, a DecimalType, StringType, CharType, VarcharType, TimeType, DayTimeIntervalType, YearMonthIntervalType, GeometryType or GeographyType, are entries of its __dict__, as PySpark's __init__ sets them, of any value: t.scale = 3, or t.__dict__["scale"] = 3, makes a DecimalType(10, 2) a DecimalType(10, 3), as its repr, JSON, == and hash show, also in the struct, array or map that holds it, vars(t) holds them, and == compares the __dict__s, as PySpark's. Its methods read them where PySpark's read them, so a value that PySpark's methods fail of fails the same: %d of a str (%d format: a real number is required, not str), and the KeyError of a field that an interval does not have (DayTimeIntervalType().startField = 9); the plan of the type raises the error of protobuf's int field of a parameter that it refuses, as of a bool (Field spark.connect.DataType.Decimal.scale: Expected an int, got a boolean.) or an int out of its range (Value out of range). The constructors take any value, as PySpark's: DecimalType(10.0) is DecimalType(10,0), and the precision of DecimalType(np.int64(12)) is the NumPy int. A type of a Python subclass whose __init__ does not call that of its class has none of its parameters, and raises the AttributeError of the first that a method reads, as PySpark's. A set srid of a spatial type shows in its repr and simple string, and its JSON stays that of the _crs that its constructor set, of which it is equal to no type of that SRID, as PySpark's. The assertions of PySpark's constructors (field name 1 should be a string, elementType string should be an instance of ...) are made under python -O too, which skips PySpark's, whose types are then made. The parameters of an ArrayType, a MapType and a StructField are entries of the __dict__ of the type, as PySpark's __init__ sets them, of any value, which their methods read as PySpark's read self.<name>: of a property of a subclass, of a raw write of the __dict__, and of the instance's own over a class attribute (nullable = False). The constructors assert the types and the name as PySpark's do, and an assignment keeps any value: ArrayType(LongType()).elementType = "string" keeps the str, which its repr shows (ArrayType(string, True)), and its simpleString(), json() and needConversion() raise the AttributeError of the str, as PySpark's. A plan converts them as PySpark's pyspark_types_to_proto_types does: a value that is not a type raises its UNSUPPORTED_OPERATION, a flag that is not an int raises protobuf's TypeError of its bool field ('NoneType' object cannot be interpreted as an integer), and a name that is not a str protobuf's bad argument type for built-in operation of its string field. A struct's names hold the name as it is, and its treeString() raises the error of its replace, as PySpark's escape_meta_characters does ('int' object has no attribute 'replace'). The fields, names, _needConversion and _needSerializeAnyField of a StructType are entries of its __dict__ too, which its __init__ and add assign through setattr, as PySpark's, of a subclass's setters and their errors, and which its methods read as PySpark's: its iteration, length, items, simpleString(), repr, jsonValue() and treeString() of self.fields, of a property of a subclass too, and fieldNames() of list(self.names). The type objects that an ArrayType, a MapType, a StructField and a StructType are given, by their constructors, add and assignments, are kept as they are, as PySpark's keep them, so a UserDefinedType whose module() or sqlType() is not implemented, such as UserDefinedType() itself, makes them: their repr, simpleString, needConversion, ==, hash, copies, pickles, tree string, names, add and toNullable are PySpark's, of the objects. A use that reads the JSON of the type raises the NOT_IMPLEMENTED of its jsonValue() then, as PySpark's does: json(), the plans of a cast, DataFrame.to, from_json, a table, a Java UDF and a UDTF, a reader's schema and the relation of data of no rows. toInternal and fromInternal raise the errors of the type's own methods. A cast to one is made and shows its simpleString(), as PySpark's Column, and raises its error at each request. createDataFrame of local rows or of a pandas DataFrame of one, also of local rows of values of such a type (its __UDT__), whose schema it infers first, of the errors of PySpark's inference, such as the CANNOT_MERGE_TYPE of the type and the type of a value of another row and the CANNOT_INFER_TYPE_FOR_FIELD of a value of no type, then converts the data, as PySpark's converters do, of the errors of the sqlType() and the serialize of the type, and then raises the error of its jsonValue(), of the json() of the schema.

    The abstract classes of the types, DataType, AtomicType, NumericType, IntegralType, FractionalType, DatetimeType, AnyTimeType, SpatialType and AnsiIntervalType, make types of their own, as PySpark's plain classes do. AtomicType() has the typeName, simpleString and jsonValue of its class, atomic, converts no values, and is equal to a type of its class and __dict__ only. It pickles as its class and its __dict__: at the protocols 2 and up its pickle is PySpark's own, byte for byte, and PySpark loads the drop-in's at every protocol. The struct, array or map that holds it names it in its simpleString, JSON and tree string, and pickles of the types it is made of. It compares it, and names it in its simpleString and tree string, by the object that it holds, as PySpark's does, rather than by its JSON: so two types of the same JSON, of one class and different __dict__s or of two classes of one typeName, stay apart in the types that hold them, as UserDefinedTypes of other parameters do, and toNullable() keeps them as they are. The schema of a DataFrame and of its rows, as the server gives it, holds a new object of each user-defined type, as PySpark's UserDefinedType.fromJson makes it of its class, and so does fromJson: not the object that the client was given, nor of its attributes, and a class whose __init__ takes arguments raises its TypeError wherever PySpark converts that schema, as in PySpark: the schema, columns, dtypes, printSchema, the rows, the lookups that read the columns (df[0], dir(df), names of two underscores), the copies and the unpickles, the map functions and the numeric aggregations of grouped data. The cached schema of a DataFrame holds the objects that PySpark's _cached_schema holds, those of to or of a map function, else the new objects of the schema of the server: a set operation compares them as PySpark's does, by the __dict__ of their user-defined types, and a pickle carries them, which the unpickled DataFrame keeps as its cached schema. DataType.simpleString(t), the method of the base class, is t.typeName(), as PySpark's, which the types of a string of their own, such as bigint or struct<a:bigint>, override. IntegralType() is one instance, as PySpark's DataTypeSingleton makes it, and so is each Python subclass of it, of an instance of its own, and a Python subclass of the other classes, such as a UserDefinedType of parameters, takes the arguments of its __init__.

    Neither client has a proto or an Arrow type of such a type, also of one nested in another or in the sqlType() of a user-defined type. A plan of one raises the UNSUPPORTED_OPERATION of PySpark's pyspark_types_to_proto_types, as PySpark's plans do: a cast, DataFrame.to, a UDF that is not Arrow-optimized, a UDTF, and transformWithState and transformWithStateInPandas of an output type of one at their first request, and the registration of a UDTF or of a Java UDF, Catalog.createTable and the schema of a table or a materialized view of a pipeline at the call or the decorator. As the schema of createDataFrame, local rows of one raise UNSUPPORTED_DATA_TYPE_FOR_ARROW_CONVERSION once their values are converted, as PySpark's to_arrow_schema raises it after its converters run, so that the errors of the values, and of the serialize of a user-defined type, come first; and a pandas DataFrame of one as its only type raises UNSUPPORTED_DATA_TYPE_FOR_ARROW. Data of no rows and a NumPy array make a relation of the JSON of the schema, PySpark's json(), in which such a type is its own JSON, also nested; the server fails to parse it (PARSE_SYNTAX_ERROR), and so does a pyarrow Table of one as its only type. A reader's csv, json or xml of a DataFrame reads a schema of JSON as PySpark's Parse plan does when it is built, with StructType.fromJson, so each request of the DataFrame raises the CANNOT_PARSE_DATATYPE of the name of a type of its class only, and the UNSUPPORTED_DATA_TYPE of a StructField as a type. DataType() and a Python subclass of it that is not an AtomicType are not expected as a schema, as PySpark's isinstance reads them: their NOT_EXPECTED_TYPE names their class.

    PySpark's singleton types, from NullType and IntegerType to CalendarIntervalType and IntegralType, are classes of the metaclass DataTypeSingleton, which type(IntegerType) shows. The drop-in's are native classes, whose metaclass is type: PyO3 has no option of a metaclass of a class, and CPython refuses to assign the __class__ of a class of type after it is made. So an error that names the type of such a class, as the NOT_EXPECTED_TYPE of udf(f, IntegerType) (of the class, not of an instance), names type where PySpark's names DataTypeSingleton. pyspark.sql.types has PySpark's DataTypeSingleton, of which a class is made as PySpark's makes it, such as a subclass of a singleton type of __main__, which PySpark's cloudpickle sends by value of its metaclass: it loads, of one instance, as in PySpark. The constructors of the native classes return the one instance and raise the TypeError of DataTypeSingleton.__call__ for an argument, as PySpark's, and inspect.signature of the class shows no parameters. A copy, a deep copy and a pickle of the one instance are the one instance too, where PySpark's copy.copy, copy.deepcopy and pickle make another object of the class, as the note of its DataTypeSingleton says. A subclass of such a class of another metaclass, class X(IntegerType, metaclass=Meta), is made, where PySpark's raises the TypeError of a metaclass conflict.

    PySpark's concrete types are plain classes too, which a user subclasses, and so are the drop-in's, from NullType and StringType to ArrayType, StructField and StructType. A type of a Python subclass is of the core type of its class, which the plans and the conversions read, as PySpark's read it by isinstance, and the methods of its class are PySpark's, of the methods of the object: - typeName() is the name of the subclass (subinteger of class SubIntegerType(IntegerType)), of which simpleString(), jsonValue() and json() are, where the class has none of its own, and the type of the JSON of an array, a map and a struct; the repr of a class whose repr names its class names the subclass (SubBooleanType(), SubDayTimeIntervalType(0, 3)), and so does its hash, of the repr; - where a method of PySpark's calls another method of the object, or of a type that an array, a map or a struct holds, the drop-in's calls it too, so an override of a subclass is of their JSON, simple string, repr, tree string and conversions, also on the Python workers of the server, to which a UDF pickles its return type: a UDF that is not Arrow-optimized converts its results with the toInternal of the subclass, as PySpark's; - json() and jsonValue() read the attributes that PySpark's jsonValue reads as Python reads them: of a struct, its fields as it iterates itself (iter(self.fields), or an __iter__ of its own), of a field its name, dataType, nullable and metadata, and of an array and a map their types and flags, so a property of a subclass that overrides one is of the JSON, at any depth, and so is a patch of a native class (of StringType.isUTF8BinaryCollation patched to return False, a string is "string collate UTF8_BINARY", of the __COLLATIONS metadata of its field); createDataFrame of a pandas DataFrame, a pyarrow Table, local data or data of no rows sends that json() as the schema of its relation, as PySpark's, and converts the data with PySpark's conversion, which reads the attributes alike (see dev/parity/known-differences.md); - the conversion of the local rows of createDataFrame takes the type objects of the schema, as PySpark's converters hold them: its errors name the object (input for SubBooleanType() must not be None), of its repr, and a geometry or a geography converts with the toInternal of a subclass, whose result is converted as PyArrow converts it; - a type of a subclass is equal only to a type of its class or of a subclass of it, of the same parameters and of the same attributes of its __dict__, as PySpark's isinstance(other, self.__class__) and __dict__: StringType() == SubStringType() is false in both orders, as == calls the __eq__ of the subclass first; - its __init__ takes arguments of its own, which the __new__ of the class takes as object.__new__ does, and passes the parameters to the __init__ of the class, which sets them, as PySpark's, also of StringType, DecimalType, TimeType, the intervals and StructType, whose errors of arguments are those of __init__ (DecimalType.__init__() got an unexpected keyword argument 'foo'), but for the counts of too many positional arguments, which are PyO3's, without self, as for the other methods (see below): DecimalType.__init__() takes from 0 to 2 positional arguments but 3 were given, where PySpark's says "from 1 to 3" and "4"; - it pickles, copies and deep-copies, at the protocols 2 and up, as copyreg.__newobj__(cls) and PySpark's __dict__ of it, of its parameters (and of the _crs and _alg of a spatial type) and of the attributes that a user set, and of its __slots__, so each client loads the other's, and cloudpickle sends a class of __main__ by value: the pickle of a type of no types in it, such as a DecimalType of a subclass, is PySpark's own, byte for byte, and the types that a type holds pickle as the class's own do (see above); - a subclass of a singleton type has an instance of its own, as PySpark's DataTypeSingleton makes it, and takes no arguments; - the Column of a cast or try_cast to it renders its simpleString(), as PySpark's (CAST(id AS SUB)), and so does one to a user-defined type of its own simpleString() (CAST(id AS SCALE2)), also in an array, a map or a struct, while the plan is of the core type.

    A subclass of __slots__ of no __getstate__ of its own raises TypeError: a class that defines __slots__ without defining __getstate__ cannot be pickled at the protocols 0 and 1, as PySpark's, and pickles of its slots at the protocols 2 and up, as PySpark's.

    What differs: - Python calls the __init__ of a subclass of a singleton type at each call of the class, which returns the one instance, where PySpark's metaclass calls it on the first call, as the drop-in's DataTypeSingleton does of a class of it, such as of PySpark's cloudpickle of such a subclass of __main__ (see above). Copies and pickles of the instance are the instance, as for the class's own, where PySpark's copyreg.__newobj__ makes another object, and a subclass of another metaclass is made, where PySpark's raises its TypeError: metaclass conflict. This is inherent: PySpark's DataTypeSingleton.__call__ returns the one instance without calling __init__, and a native class is of the metaclass type, whose __call__ calls the __init__ each time the __new__ gives an instance of the class: the extension makes its classes with PyType_FromSpec, of the stable ABI of Python 3.9, which has no PyType_FromMetaclass to make a class of another metaclass (see dev/parity/known-differences.md). - A class of type classes of two branches of their hierarchy, such as class X(StringType, IntegerType) or class Y(ArrayType, MapType), raises TypeError: multiple bases have instance lay-out conflict when it is made, where PySpark makes the class (X() is StringType()). This is inherent: an object of such a class would be of the native layout of one branch, which the native methods of the other read as theirs, as CPython raises of bases of conflicting layouts. - createDataFrame of data sends the JSON of the core types of its schema, where PySpark sends the json() of the schema object, which names a type of a subclass by its typeName() ("subinteger") and which the server then fails to parse (PARSE_SYNTAX_ERROR), so sending it would only bring back that failure; of data of no rows it sends the json() of the object, as PySpark, and so it does of any data under a struct that holds a type that overrides a read of jsonValue (see above), or a struct of a names of its own, which PySpark's conversion reads, whose data PySpark's conversion converts, as PySpark's. The readers, from_json, from_xml, applyInPandasWithState and toDDL send the json() of the object, as PySpark's.

    The metadata of a field is the value that it was given, as PySpark's metadata or {} keeps it: a dict, or another value, such as a list or a str (see below). == compares it as Python compares dicts, as PySpark compares the __dict__s of its fields, also in a struct and in the types that hold it: (1, 2) is not [1, 2], nor {1: "a"} {"1": "a"}, nor 10**30 10**30 + 1. Its jsonValue() holds that dict, which json() writes with json.dumps, as PySpark's: an int of more than 64 bits as it is, a float as Python's repr writes it, of the shortest digits that read back as it and of an even last digit of a tie (1e-07, and 74334700308107.62 of 74334700308107.625), NaN and Infinity, and the keys sorted as sort_keys sorts them, of the ints and floats by their values ({10: "a", 9: "b"} is {"9":"b","10":"a"}). The core holds the metadata of the JSON of json.dumps of the dict, of its values as they are written, so a plan sends that text, as PySpark's pyspark_types_to_proto_types sends the metadata of a field, and so do the readers and createDataFrame in the JSON of the schema that they send, of any depth, also of the metadata of the fields of the sqlType() of a UserDefinedType, as PySpark's: of a schema that was given, or of one inferred of local rows, of the __UDT__ objects of their values, of their own jsonValue(). The server keeps the low 64 bits of such an int (10**30 becomes 5076944270305263616), and refuses a float that is not finite. The Arrow schema of createDataFrame of a pyarrow Table holds json.dumps of the metadata of each field, as PySpark's to_arrow_schema writes it; that of local rows, of a pandas DataFrame, of each of its conversions of a column, and of a numpy array holds it on the fields of the sqlType() of a UserDefinedType only, as PySpark's _deduplicate_field_names drops the metadata of the others, which names the fields of each struct of the schema (a_0, a_1), of the top level too for a pandas DataFrame, but not those of the sqlType(). A dict that json.dumps refuses, such as one of a set or of a key of a tuple, is kept, and only the uses that write its JSON raise the error of json.dumps, as PySpark's: json(), of its sorted keys too ('<' not supported between instances of 'str' and 'int'), each request of a plan of the type, such as of a cast, also of one made in the sqlType() that jsonValue() calls, and createDataFrame, after it converts the data, of the error of its Arrow schema first where that holds the metadata (keys must be str, int, float, bool or None, not tuple). mapInPandas, mapInArrow, applyInPandas and applyInArrow, after they read the columns of the DataFrame, and those of a cogroup raise PySpark's NOT_IMPLEMENTED of the return type of such a schema, of their UDF's check of its Arrow type. The JSON of a type writes each character of its names and strings that is not printable ASCII as the \u escape of json.dumps, as PySpark's json().

    Metadata that is not a dict, such as [1, 2], "abc" or 5, is kept as it is given, as PySpark's field keeps it, by the constructor (of false metadata, such as [] or 0, a new dict), an assignment and StructType.add, and so do its jsonValue(), json(), toNullable(), copies, pickles and fromJson. A plan sends json.dumps of it when it has a length, as PySpark's len(field.metadata) > 0, which the server refuses (ClassCastException: a JArray cannot be cast to a JObject), and each request of a plan of metadata of no length, such as an int, raises the TypeError of the length (object of type 'int' has no len()); createDataFrame and from_json send it in the JSON of the schema, which the server fails to parse. Its jsonValue() of a field of collated strings raises the TypeError of PySpark's {**self.metadata} ('list' object is not a mapping).

    A TableArg, of DataFrame.asTable(), pickles at every protocol, plainly and by cloudpickle, as PySpark's, of its user-set attributes, and copy.copy and copy.deepcopy copy it as PySpark's. Its pickle keeps the relation of the plan of its DataFrame, as a pickled DataFrame does (see "What a plan build does for a pickle" in dev/parity/known-differences.md), of the functions of its UDFs, and its partitioning, ordering and single partition; the unpickle sends no request, as PySpark's, and the table argument that it gives sends the same plan and subquery, of the same plan ID, whose functions its requests pickle. A plain pickle of one of a function that does not pickle by reference, such as a lambda, fails at it, as PySpark's. A plan that does not build pickles too, and the requests of the unpickled table argument raise its error, as PySpark's. The pickle names the session of its DataFrame by its ID, so it unpickles only where the session of that ID is active, as a pickled DataFrame does: it raises NO_ACTIVE_SESSION where none is, such as on another thread, and PySparkAssertionError where another one is, where PySpark's, whose plan holds no session, unpickles anywhere. On a Python worker of the server it unpickles as PySpark Connect's table argument of the relation, which needs no session. Its _subquery_expr is the entry of its __dict__, as PySpark's, an expression of pyspark.sql.connect.expressions, as a Column's _expr is, of PySpark's to_plan and repr, but not the SubqueryExpression of PySpark, whose fields of the subquery it does not have (see the Column, the WindowSpec and the TableArg below). _is_partitioned() is PySpark's.

    The __repr__ of a Column of a subquery names its plan by its plan ID, SubqueryExpression(plan=<plan 2>, type=scalar), where PySpark's shows the repr of its plan object, <pyspark.sql.connect.plan.Project object at 0x...>.

    DataFrame.inputFiles() returns a list of its paths, where PySpark returns protobuf's repeated field of the response, a RepeatedScalarContainer, which compares equal to the list.

    The keys of getMessageParameters() of a server error are in the server's order. PySpark's follow the protobuf map of the error details that it fetches: with protobuf's pure-Python implementation that is the server's order too, and with upb, protobuf's default, the order of its hash table, which changes from one process to the next. For its own errors, PySpark keeps the order of each call site.

    A signal ends the wait of a call for the server as it ends PySpark's. The KeyboardInterrupt of a handler, as of Ctrl-C, interrupts the execution in flight on the server, as the except KeyboardInterrupt of PySpark's execution does, and a second signal ends the wait without waiting for the interrupt; the exception of another handler ends the wait at once and is raised, without an interrupt. A signal while the failure of a call is converted, as during the request of the details of an error of the server or the read of its stack-trace settings, ends the wait at once, of no interrupt, and its exception has grpcio's error of the failure as its context, and the operation ID of the execution of the failure (_operation_id), as PySpark's _handle_error converts the failure in the except Exception clause of the call; a call that a handler of a log of the conversion makes meanwhile waits as any other, and a KeyboardInterrupt interrupts its execution, if it has one, never the converted one. The lookup of the plan compression settings of the first plan of a session, and the read of the stack-trace settings of an error, catch the exception of a handler that is an Exception, as the except Exception of PySpark's do: the lookup is then of no settings, as a failed one, and the call goes on and waits again.

    At interpreter exit, a session that is not stopped has its ML cache cleaned by one clean_cache request of the _cleanup_ml_cache of its client, as PySpark's _on_exit cleans it, so a program that replaces that method, as PySpark's tests do for a client of no server, replaces the cleanup. Its errors are ignored, but when the server is gone the exit waits on that request's retries, as PySpark's does, so the exit can take as long as those retries. A signal ends that wait, as it ends PySpark's, whose cleanup catches Exception only: the KeyboardInterrupt of Ctrl-C is raised out of the exit hook, which atexit reports before it runs the next one, and the session is then neither released nor closed. The exception of a handler that is an Exception ends the request that waits, which the exit hook goes on from, unless it arrives while the session looks the plan compression settings up for the clean_cache, as for its first plan: the lookup catches it, as PySpark's, and the clean_cache is sent, which waits again. A stopped session sends nothing at exit. PySpark 4.3.0 does not exit at all while a session other than its default one is left open to the end, such as one of newSession() or of SparkSession(connection=...): its SparkSession.__del__ closes the client in the teardown of the interpreter, whose grpcio Channel.close() waits forever, a bug of PySpark that the drop-in does not reproduce: it exits. Nor does a session that the program dropped send anything, of which no object is left but a conf or a client of it: neither the session nor a DataFrame, reader, writer, catalog, stream or ML object of it. PySpark's session object closes its client when it is freed, and the exit sends nothing for a closed client. The drop-in drops such a session, closes its client, and closes the connection of the process to its server once no session uses it, which the sessions of newSession() and cloneSession() share, as PySpark's channels share their subchannels, but a session of a token, whose connection is its own; so does the stop() of the last session of the connection, as grpcio's close of the channel of its client. Once its catalog or its streams is read, the session object of either client is in a reference cycle with its catalog, the catalog of its __dict__, which holds it as its _sparkSession, or with its StreamingQueryManager, its _sqm, which holds it as its _session, and which only the garbage collector frees, so that until a collection its exit still cleans its ML cache, as PySpark's. A client that no session object owns, of SparkConnectClient(...) or of its newSession() or clone(), lives until the exit, as PySpark's atexit registration keeps it, so its ML cache is cleaned at exit unless it is closed, even when the program dropped it. SPARK_CONNECT_RELEASE_SESSION_ON_EXIT is read when a session is made, as PySpark reads it: when it is true or 1, the session is then also released and closed at exit, after the cleanup.

    PySpark's client sends the ReleaseExecute requests of an execution through a ThreadPoolExecutor, which refuses new work once the interpreter has begun to shut down. The drop-in refuses them from the same point. An execution after it, such as a collect() in an atexit handler, raises RuntimeError: cannot schedule new futures after interpreter shutdown after its first response and sends no ReleaseExecute, as in PySpark. The exit's clean_cache ends the same way, and its error is ignored. Requests that are not executions, such as the AnalyzePlan of a schema, still work then.

    Once the exit has run the atexit hook of the drop-in's extension, after the threads that are not daemons have ended, a call that fails on another thread, a daemon thread, as the calls of a session that an earlier hook closed fail, raises nothing: the thread waits until the process ends, as CPython 3.14 holds such a thread once the interpreter finalizes, and the hook waits for the exceptions that such threads are building, for up to 5 seconds. Before 3.14, the finalization ends a thread that waits for the GIL with pthread_exit, which aborts the process when the thread runs the Python code of an exception, such as its first import of the exception classes, below the frames of the extension. PySpark's daemon thread raises its exception, until the finalization ends it.

    The TypeError for too many positional arguments counts them without self: where CPython says "takes from 2 to 4 positional arguments but 5 were given", the drop-in says "takes from 1 to 3 positional arguments but 4 were given". A method that takes no arguments gets "takes no arguments (1 given)", where CPython says "takes 1 positional argument but 2 were given" for DataFrame.distinct(1). A method that takes one argument gets the plural "positional arguments" even for a count of one: the drop-in says "takes 1 positional arguments but 3 were given" for DataFrame.limit(1, 2, 3), where CPython says "takes 2 positional arguments but 4 were given". These are the messages of PyO3 0.29.3, whose too_many_positional_arguments (src/impl_/extract_argument.rs) counts the parameters of the Rust function, of no self, and writes "positional arguments" of any count: matching CPython's would need a patched PyO3.

    A warning that the extension raises, such as the FutureWarning of registerTempTable, Catalog.registerFunction, createExternalTable, a dbName of the catalog, and getItem and getField of a Column, and the UserWarning of replace of a dict and a value, of GroupedData.apply, of type hints that applyInPandas and applyInArrow cannot read, of the arguments that createDataFrame ignores, of the configurations of the server, and of the listener bus of the streams, is attributed as PySpark's is: to the module of pyspark.sql.connect of PySpark's warnings.warn, of the registry of the warnings that the module showed, and to the line of that call, in the file of the drop-in's module of that name. So warnings.filterwarnings(..., module="pyspark") and the filters of a line match it, and the default filter shows it once, as PySpark's. The drop-in's module ends before that line, so the warning shows no source line, where PySpark's shows its call. The warning of a window of no partitions, WARN WindowExpression: No Partition Defined for Window operation! ..., is PySpark's: a UserWarning of pyspark.sql.connect.expressions, of its line 1126, which the default filter shows once, at each request that builds the plan of such a window, as PySpark warns as it builds the plan. A pickle of a DataFrame or a Column does not warn, as PySpark's builds no plan; but PySpark's unpickle of a DataFrame warns once, as it builds the plan to read the columns of the new DataFrame, where the drop-in's does not; and a DataFrame, a Column or a table argument that is unpickled sends the plan that was built for the pickle, of the functions of its UDFs pickled at each request (see the UDFs below), so its requests do not warn again, where PySpark's warn at each. Such a plan keeps the origin of the and of the conditions of a join of a list of Columns of that time too, where PySpark gives it the call site of each request that builds it, as it does in the drop-in otherwise. A copy of a DataFrame, in its session or another, builds its plan at each request, and warns then, as PySpark's. A request that PySpark builds in functools, as of isStreaming, a functools.cached_property, gives that and the call site of functools.py, as the drop-in's, whose isStreaming is one too.

    The FutureWarning of BisectingKMeansModel.computeCost is attributed as PySpark's is, to the line of its warnings.warn in pyspark/ml/clustering.py and to the module pyspark.ml.clustering, of its registry of shown warnings, so the filters of that module match it.

    The TypeError for a bad keyword argument of a method that takes no **options is PyO3's, which CPython words differently in two cases. For a key that is not a str, as df.limit(1, **{1: 2}), CPython says "keywords must be strings", where the drop-in says "DataFrame.limit() got an unexpected keyword argument '1'". For a method that takes no arguments, as df.count(foo=1), CPython says "DataFrame.count() got an unexpected keyword argument 'foo'", where the drop-in says "DataFrame.count() takes no keyword arguments". The methods that take **options, and the readers', writers' and SparkSession.builder.config's that take named options, word both as CPython does.

    A str that UTF-8 cannot encode, such as one with a lone surrogate, fails where PySpark builds the request that carries it, which is not always the call that takes it. The error is PySpark's UnicodeEncodeError with the same message, and no request is sent: - a reader's format, schema, option key or value, path or table name fails the DataFrame when its plan is built, at its first request, such as df.schema; - a DataFrameWriter's or a DataFrameWriterV2's format or provider, option, table property, partitioning, bucketing, sorting or clustering column, path or table name fails the write, such as save, saveAsTable or create; - a withColumns name, or an as-of join's how or direction, fails the DataFrame when its plan is built, at its first request, such as collect or columns. A direction of bytes that are not UTF-8 fails there with PySpark's UnicodeDecodeError, as protobuf's string field gives it; - the names of createDataFrame of a pandas DataFrame, of its column labels or of a list, and of a pyarrow Table, which PySpark applies with toDF, fail the DataFrame when its plan is built, at its first request. Those of other data, local rows or a NumPy array, which PySpark's conversion names its table by, fail the call, as PySpark's, which reads the settings of its conversions first, where the drop-in sends no request; - the names, SQL strings and literal strs of Columns fail each DataFrame whose plan has the Column when its plan is built, while the Column is made, renders, copies and pickles: those of F.col, F.expr, F.lit and the other functions, df[name], df.name, colRegex, metadataColumn, the field of getField, withField and dropFields, the function of call_function and call_udf, the type of cast and try_cast, and the column names and keywords of a UDF or UDTF call; - so do the arguments of the methods of a DataFrame, of its na and stat, and of grouped data that their plans set as they are given, such as the alias of alias, the names of withColumn(s)Renamed, withMetadata, toDF, drop, dropDuplicates, withWatermark, unpivot, describe, summary, crosstab, freqItems and the NA functions, a hint, the name of observe, the names of a using join, the name of zipWithIndex, the functions of agg of a dict, the output mode and the timeout of applyInPandasWithState, and the output mode, the time mode and the event-time column of transformWithStateInPandas and transformWithState, and the name of an Observation, which fails the DataFrames that it observes; - the schema of from_avro and to_avro, and the message name and the descriptor set of from_protobuf and to_protobuf, are literals, as PySpark's lit of them, which PySpark does not check otherwise: such a str fails each DataFrame of the Column, and a message name of another type, such as 5, bytes or None, is the server's error of the function; - a tag of addTag fails each execution that the thread starts, as its request is built after its plan, until removeTag removes it; a request without tags, such as that of df.schema, is sent. getTags gives the tag as it is; - a key or a value of SparkSession.builder.config, or an appName, fails getOrCreate; - a DataStreamWriter's fails the call that takes it, as PySpark sets that writer's request as it goes.

    Such a str is not one of the modes of explain, of the how of dropna, of the join types of join and lateralJoin, of the modes, directions and join types of nearestByJoin, nor a column of sum, avg, min and max of grouped data: they raise PySpark's VALUE_NOT_ALLOWED, UNSUPPORTED_JOIN_TYPE, the NEAREST_BY_JOIN errors and NOT_NUMERIC_COLUMNS, of the str as it is. So is the UNSUPPORTED_OPERATION of to and of a cast of such a str, or of another object whose str has a lone surrogate. nearestByJoin reads the lower() of its mode, direction and join type, as PySpark's, so an object without one raises its AttributeError, bytes as a join type the TypeError of replace, and bytes as a mode or a direction its error, of the bytes as they are. Such a str is no name of readStream.name, which raises INVALID_STREAMING_SOURCE_NAME of it, as PySpark's re.match(r"^[a-zA-Z0-9_]+$", name) does not match it; that pattern matches a name of a final newline, which the server refuses. A Column's attribute of such a name is the field of that name, as col[name], so hasattr of it is True, and a session's raises the AttributeError of object.__getattribute__, so hasattr of it is False, as in PySpark. explain builds the plan first, so the error of a plan that fails to build comes first.

    The commands that PySpark sends when they are called set their names as protobuf converts them, at the call: registerJavaFunction and registerJavaUDAF (the name, then the class), the register of spark.udf of a UDF and of spark.udtf, the name() of the class of spark.dataSource.register, interruptTag and interruptOperation take bytes of UTF-8, and raise protobuf's TypeError "bad argument type for built-in operation" of another value; interruptTag(None) and interruptOperation(None) raise the AssertionError of PySpark's request. The register of a plain function names the function of its UDF first, so a name that is not a str raises the TypeError of __name__, as PySpark's. A function registered of the name None is registered as fun_ and a random hex, as PySpark's client does, and register returns its UDF. SparkSession.builder.master of Spark Connect takes any value and does nothing, as PySpark Connect's.

    So it is for the other values that a plan sets as they are given, which protobuf converts when it builds the plan: a string field takes bytes of UTF-8, such as alias(b"q"), and refuses another object with the TypeError "bad argument type for built-in operation", such as the alias 5 or None, the names of withColumnRenamed(1, "z"), unpivot, describe(b"ab") (of its ints), freqItems(["a", 1]), the subset of na.replace, and a using join on ["a", 1]; an int field refuses an int beyond it with a ValueError, such as fillna(2**70), dropna(thresh=2**40) and the arguments of spark.range and spark.tvf.range after their int(), and a bool with a TypeError, such as dropna(thresh=True); a double field refuses a str, such as the support of freqItems. A join on a tuple, or on a list of conditions whose & fails, such as of a Column and an object that is neither a Column nor a literal, raises the AttributeError of to_plan or of &, which PySpark computes when it builds the plan, such as the PySparkAttributeError of the _expr of a Row; an exception of & of another class, such as a KeyError of an object's __getattr__, is raised by the join. Of several such values, the requests raise the error of the first in the order in which PySpark builds the plan: the input of a plan before the plan, but for the partitioning of a repartition, which comes first, and its count of partitions, which comes after its input, and the name of a hint or an observation before its expressions; the order of the other values of one plan can differ. As in PySpark, a DataFrame of such a plan raises at its requests, schema and collect() among them, and the call raises none. Of a join on a tuple, or on a list of conditions whose & fails, the properties read the Column of _schema, as PySpark's: their self._schema raises an AttributeError, after which Python calls the __getattr__ of the DataFrame, of that name. So schema gives a deep copy of the Column, columns, dtypes, the representation, dir(), the key completions and toArrow raise the NOT_ITERABLE of iterating it, and printSchema the TypeError of a call of a Column. The drop-in's DataFrame has no _schema itself, so df._schema is the Column of that name, where PySpark's is the schema.

    A DataFrame whose plan fails to build pickles, also by cloudpickle, and its unpickle raises the error, as PySpark's: its pickle keeps the objects of the plan, and its unpickle builds the plan to read the columns of the new DataFrame. Of an AttributeError, such as of a join on a tuple or on a list of conditions whose & fails, PySpark's read of the columns falls back from its _schema to the Column of __getattr__, whose fields is a Column, which is not iterable: so the unpickle, a copy.copy and a copy.deepcopy, which read the columns too, raise NOT_ITERABLE, in both. alias, a hint, dropDuplicates, observe, filter, sort, sortWithinPartitions and the repartitions of such a value keep the cached schema of the DataFrame that they are of, as PySpark copies it, so their schema is known without a request, and their other requests raise the error.

    The barrier of mapInPandas and mapInArrow is a protobuf bool, which their plan sets once it pickled the function, when it is built: an int is taken, as barrier=1, and another value, such as "x", None or a float, fails each request with the TypeError of protobuf, and not the call. The DataFrame keeps its schema, of the schema that it is given, as PySpark's _map_partitions caches it: a StructType as the object that it is, so schema gives a copy of it as it is then, and columns and dtypes its fields then, as do those of applyInPandas, applyInArrow and the cogroup's; the type of a DDL string is parsed at the call. The plan reads profile.id of its resource profile after that, so the profile is created on the server by the first request that builds the plan, such as collect, and not by the call nor by schema, as in PySpark; a profile without an id, such as a str, fails the requests with that AttributeError, and the id of a request whose flag fails is not read.

    The merge writer and its clauses are PySpark's (see below), which keep the condition and the conditions of the clauses as they are given: mergeInto(table, 5) and whenMatched(5) raise nothing, the clause's action raises the AttributeError of the to_plan that 5 lacks, and so does the merge of a condition of mergeInto. An action builds its assignments as PySpark's _build_merge_action, in the order of assignments.items(), in which the merge sends them, and raises their errors in that order, before those of the condition: a key that is not a str, bytes too, is the bare AssertionError of PySpark's expr, a value that is not a Column the AttributeError of its to_plan, and an object that is not a mapping the AttributeError of its items.

    A str that a later call unsets or replaces is no longer in the request, in the same cases as in PySpark. A stream reader's option of None leaves its option as it was, as PySpark's does. A batch reader's keeps it as None, which its load leaves out of its plan, and which the plans of its table and changes set, which fails their requests with protobuf's TypeError, as PySpark's do. An option key, a table property or a config key that is not a str fails at the same call as PySpark's, with the same error: an AssertionError at a reader's load of a path, and protobuf's TypeError where the request is built otherwise.

    The function of a UDF, of a pandas or Arrow UDF of any eval type, of a UDTF (called, of a table argument, in a lateral join) and of mapInPandas, mapInArrow, applyInPandas, applyInArrow, the cogroup's applyInPandas and applyInArrow, applyInPandasWithState, transformWithStateInPandas and transformWithState is pickled each time a request builds a plan that holds it, as PySpark's PythonUDF.to_plan and PythonUDTF.to_plan pickle it. So a value that the function reads, changed after the function was applied and before an action, is the value that the action runs with, and so for each later action. The output type of a UDF and of a map or group-map function is converted then too, of the type object that PySpark's PythonUDF keeps, as are the return type of a UDTF, of the object that its PythonUDTF keeps, the output schema of transformWithState and transformWithStateInPandas and the schema of DataFrame.to: a StructType changed in place after the call, as by add or by a field renamed, is the type that the request sends, and that the pickled command of a UDF carries, as it is then. A type that the conversion does not take, such as AtomicType() or a StructField as a type, fails the requests with its UNSUPPORTED_OPERATION, not the call, where the eval type of the UDF does not refuse it at the call, as the Arrow and pandas ones do with NOT_IMPLEMENTED. A function that does not pickle fails each request of the plan, from the first, such as df.schema, with PySpark's error, the PicklingError of CloudPickleSerializer.dumps, or UDTF_SERIALIZATION_ERROR of a UDTF, and no request is sent; the call that applies it does not fail. The functions are pickled in the order in which PySpark builds the plan, the initial state of transformWithState before its function and the arguments of a UDTF before its handler, and the first error of the build, of a pickling or of a value that the plan does not take, such as a str above, is raised. The return type of a UDTF is converted after its arguments and before its handler is pickled, as PySpark's PythonUDTF.to_plan converts it with pyspark_types_to_proto_types, of the PythonUDTF that the call made: of the return type object and the handler class of the UDTF at the call, a str as its UnparsedDataType, and of the __name__ of the class then, which UDTF_SERIALIZATION_ERROR names; so a returnType or a func set on the UDTF after the call is not that of the call. A value that is neither a DataType nor a str, such as 5, is kept by the UDTF as it is, and each request of a call of it raises UNSUPPORTED_OPERATION of data type 5, of the str of the value when the request is built, or the error of that str; so does a type that has no proto, such as AtomicType(), also one that a type changed after the call holds. spark.udf.register and spark.udtf.register are commands, which pickle the function when they are called, as PySpark's do; spark.udtf.register and spark.client.register_udtf set the name and convert the return type first, as it is then, and the latter reads the __name__ of the handler class before them, as PySpark's PythonUDTF does.
