A copy of a Column, a DataFrame, grouped data or a table argument holds the functions, which the requests of the copy pickle, as PySpark's: copy.copy shares them, and copy.deepcopy deep-copies each with the memo of the call, so a function, a lambda or a closure is the function itself, and a callable object, a bound method or a functools.partial is a copy, which the copies of one call share, as the two Columns of a UDF of copy.deepcopy([c1, c2]) do. copy.copy shares the output type of a UDF and the return type of a UDTF too, and copy.deepcopy copies them, as PySpark's copy of the plan does: a type changed in place after a deep copy is not that of the copy, nor one changed after a pickle that of the unpickled DataFrame. A request warns once of a window of no partitions, as PySpark's, in the order of the build, though the drop-in builds the plan of a request twice when it has such functions, as it pickles them between the two builds: a window that comes after a function in the build warns in its turn among the picklings, so a function before it that does not pickle raises the error of its pickling without the warning, and the error of the warning, of an "error" filter of the warnings, is raised before the functions after the window are pickled, as PySpark's.

    A pickled Column, DataFrame or table argument carries the functions of its UDFs, as PySpark's pickle carries those of its plan objects: the (func, returnType) of a UDF, the handler class, its name and the return type of a UDTF, and the schema of DataFrame.to, which the requests of the unpickled object convert and pickle when they build its plan, of the holes in place of their commands in the relation or the expression of the pickle, also on the server's Python worker, where PySpark's plan pickles them. So pickle.dumps of one of a UDF of a lambda raises the error of pickling the lambda, as PySpark's, and of a function that pickle pickles by reference, such as a function of a module or of __main__, pickles, of the function by its name, which the requests of the unpickled object pickle as it is then: a value that the function reads, changed after the pickle, is the value that they send, and a function that does not pickle then fails them with the PicklingError of CloudPickleSerializer.dumps. cloudpickle.dumps of a function that does not pickle by value raises the error of pickling it, such as the TypeError of a lock that it reads, as PySpark's, and CloudPickleSerializer.dumps its PicklingError. A plain pickle of a DataFrame fails at the first function that it does not pickle, and else at the function that gives back its session, as PySpark's pickles its plan and then its session. An output type of a UDF or a return type of a UDTF that the build of the plan does not convert, such as AtomicType() of a UDF without Arrow, or a StructType changed to hold it after the call, does not fail the pickle: as PySpark's, which keeps the type object, the requests of the unpickled Column and table argument raise its UNSUPPORTED_OPERATION, and so does the unpickle of a DataFrame, which builds its plan.

    A pickled Column of subqueries, of scalar(), exists(), isin of a DataFrame or a table argument, carries the relations of the plans that they refer to, of their plan IDs, as PySpark's carries the plan objects of its subqueries, which keep their _plan_id: a plan of the unpickled Column sends them in its WithRelations, as a plan of the Column does, also on the server's Python worker, where the Column is PySpark Connect's. Column._expr pickles as the expression of the unpickled Column, and TableArg._subquery_expr as that of a Column of its subquery, as PySpark's expressions pickle their fields. A pickled, cloudpickled or deep-copied Column keeps the origins of its expressions, of their call sites, as PySpark's unpickled expressions keep their origin, whether the capture of origins is on or not (is_debugging_enabled): so does a sort order or a CASE WHEN, which the unpickle makes again of its parts, and the sort orders of a window specification or of a table argument.

    A DataFrame or a Column holds the functions of its UDFs and the plans of the Python client as Python's garbage collector sees them, as it sees PySpark's plan objects: each part of a plan or of an expression that holds such an object, directly or below it, has a Python object, a SharedPart, which holds a reference to the SharedParts of the parts that it holds, one for each of its references to them, and reports the object of its part. The objects are the function of a UDF, a UDTF or a map or group-map function, the plan of a DataFrame(plan, session), which its relation keeps, also through the subqueries of the expressions of a plan, the plan that an unpickled Column of the subquery of a DataFrame of a plan that did not build keeps, to build it at its requests, the plan and the exception of a build that failed, which a plan keeps, and the Python Observations of observe. Each object that holds a reference to such a part holds a reference to its SharedPart and reports it, as each of PySpark's objects holds its plan objects: the plan object of a DataFrame (df._plan), which the DataFrame holds in its __dict__, and its copies, the plan object that a DataFrame makes of a _plan of the Python client or of the plan object of a DataFrame of another session, which the DataFrame reports with that _plan and keeps until the first read of another _plan or its __init__, a generator of toLocalIterator, which holds its DataFrame until it ends, as PySpark's generator holds self, and reads its plan at its first row, and the expressions of the client, the _expr of a Column, the _subquery_expr of a table argument and the entries of the lists of a window specification, which copy.copy and copy.deepcopy copy as they copy a Column, and which the __dict__ of the Column, the table argument and the window holds, as PySpark's hold their expressions, so the collector sees them through it, and a Column of an expression that it replaced holds the parts of the new one only. So a reference cycle through such an object and any of the objects that hold it, one or several, is collected, as PySpark collects it: through a bound method of an object that keeps a Column and a DataFrame of it, or a DataFrame and one derived from it; through a plan of the Python client whose attribute keeps both its DataFrame and a Column of its subquery, or a table argument of it. A function or a plan that a live object reaches is kept as it is, and pickled at the requests of that object. Those that hold their DataFrame report it too, and grouped data, their cogroups, na and stat hold their DataFrames, Columns and grouped data in their __dict__, and the writers of write and writeTo, the stream writer of writeStream and the merge writer of mergeInto the plans of their DataFrames, and the merge writer and its clauses their Columns and the messages of the actions, as PySpark's, which the collector sees, so a cycle through the DataFrame alone, such as df.back = df.groupBy("id"), is collected. The DataFrames of the plan of another, DataFrame(df._plan, session) and copy.copy(df), hold the plan object of df (_plan) and not df, as PySpark's do, and a copy.deepcopy(df) holds a deep copy of it, so df is freed at its last reference, its attributes with it. So is a DataFrame that keeps an object of its plan, such as its plan object (df.p = df._plan) or its stream writer (df.w = df.writeStream): the plan object of df._plan holds the plan of the core and its parts, in its native base, and no DataFrame, as PySpark's plan object holds none; its __dict__ holds the fields of PySpark's LogicalPlan, and its copies are of its class and its fields. A SharedPart reports nothing while a reference to its part is of a holder that the collector does not see, such as a request that holds a clone of a plan while it runs: so the collector frees no object that such a holder reaches, and a cycle through one is collected once the holder is gone. The objects of the native ML classes of pyspark.ml.connect report the parts of the plans of the DataFrames that they keep in Rust, as PySpark's summary holds its _summary_dataset and the plan of a DataFrame of a method of an ML object the object and the plans of its arguments: a model and its training summary those of the summary dataset (the training dataset, or its transform by a clustering model), a OneVsRestModel those of the binary training sets of the models of its classes, and a DataFrame of a method of an ML object or of an ML helper (of a summary, a model's gaussiansDF or describeTopics, ChiSquareTest.test, an FPGrowthModel's, PowerIterationClustering.assignClusters) those of the object's summary dataset and of the DataFrames of its arguments. So a cycle through a function of a UDF of the training DataFrame of a model that keeps the model, or a Python plan of that DataFrame that keeps it, is collected, and the model's object is deleted from the ML cache of the server, as PySpark's. They report the other Python objects that they hold, as PySpark's hold them as attributes: a model its training summary, its cached members (trees), its own defaults and its RemoteModelRef, of which a copy holds a reference of its own, and the one that it was made of while no transform, writer or call holds its object; an estimator, a transformer, a Pipeline, a PipelineModel, a OneVsRest and its model, and a validator and its model the values of their params and their attributes (stages, classifier, models, best model, metrics); a fitMultiple iterator its estimator, dataset and param maps. So a model and its summary in a cycle (model.summary.model = model) are collected, fit after fit, and the model's object is deleted from the ML cache of the server, as PySpark's. Nor does a SharedPart report anything while a call of the binding that may clone or drop its part runs without the GIL, on any thread: a call that builds a request of the plans of the objects that it is given, such as an action of a DataFrame or a fit of pyspark.ml.connect, clones and drops the parts of those plans, of what the call keeps alive anyway; the other calls, such as the waits of streaming queries, the calls of the configuration, the catalog and the session, and the messages that the Python client builds, such as the commands of the writers, of the starts of the stream writer and of the merges, whose DataFrames the binding replaces in them before the call, clone none. So the collector gets the same references of each object in each of its passes, and a cycle through parts that no call clones is collected whenever the collector runs, as PySpark's is, except while a listener of the Rust API of the session gets an event, which may clone any part. On a free-threaded build of CPython, the parts have no SharedPart, and a cycle through them is not collected (see "Garbage collection" in dev/parity/known-differences.md). An ArrayType, a MapType, a StructField and a StructType report the type objects, the flags and the metadata dict that they hold, and the lists of a struct, as PySpark's __dict__ holds them, so a cycle through a type that one holds, such as a DecimalType with an attribute that keeps the struct, is collected. The stream writer, PySpark's, keeps the functions of foreachBatch and foreach as their pickles, so a function that keeps its writer is freed with its last reference, and a copy or a pickle of the writer carries the pickle of the call. The registration of dataSource reports its session, and the builder of pyspark.sql.connect.session.SparkSession holds the dict of its options, its channel builder and the list of its hook factories in its __dict__, which its shallow copies share, as PySpark's plain objects hold them, so a session that keeps its dataSource, or an option, a channel builder or a hook factory that keeps its builder or a copy of it, is collected. The readers and the writers of read, readStream, write and writeTo, PySpark's classes, hold the dict of their options, whose keys and values may be any objects, such as a key that is not a str, and the writers the format, the save mode, the partitioning and clustering columns and the provider as they are, in their __dict__ (a writer in its _write), which their shallow copies share, as PySpark's copies share the dict of their options and the _write of a writer: so a cycle through one, such as a key of an option that keeps its reader, is collected, also with a copy of the reader, and freed with the last of the copies.

    A clause of mergeInto, PySpark's, builds the messages of its condition and assignments when it is added (_build_merge_action), which pickles the functions of their UDFs then and raises there, and the merge sends them as they were built then: so a value that a function reads, changed after the clause, is not that of the merge, also of a copy of the writer, and a window of no partitions of a clause warns once, at the clause.

    A UDF and a UDTF are of PySpark Connect's classes, UserDefinedFunction of pyspark.sql.connect.udf and UserDefinedTableFunction of pyspark.sql.connect.udtf, as in PySpark. The classic pyspark.sql.udf and pyspark.sql.udtf are PySpark's modules, of Apache Spark as they are, whose classes, which pyspark.sql.functions names too, need a SparkContext: so isinstance of a UDTF that udtf builds against the classic UserDefinedTableFunction is False, as in PySpark. udf gives a function in both clients, which is of neither class.

    So it is for the objects of a session: each is of PySpark Connect's class, of its module of pyspark.sql.connect, as repr, type(x).__module__ and pickle show it. The package pyspark.sql.connect.client is PySpark's, of Apache Spark as it is: of no __all__, it imports * of core and the getLogLevel of pyspark.sql.connect.logging. So its names, of dir() and of from pyspark.sql.connect.client import *, are PySpark's, those classes, getLogLevel and the modules core and retries, but for PySpark's modules artifact and reattach, which the transport replaces, and for retries until it is imported (by get_retry_policies, set_retry_policies, a read of _retry_policies or a copy.copy of a client, a retry_policy of SparkConnectClient, or an import of it), as it imports grpc: until then the package has no attribute retries, which PySpark's core imports. core defines PySpark's names, PYSPARK_ROOT, _is_pyspark_source and _import_zstandard_if_available too, and the private helpers of the drop-in.     The module that the class of each of the objects of a session above names is imported once the object is made, as PySpark imports it with the module that defines the class: the module of the session imports those of its DataFrames, its conf, its readers and its queries, and with them those of grouped data, merges, writers, columns, observations and table arguments, as PySpark's does, and catalog, tvf and dataSource import theirs when they make their objects, as PySpark's properties do, and a Column that a plain pickle gives back, of no session, imports its own, as PySpark's unpickle imports the module of its class.     The functions of pyspark.sql.connect.functions are of its modules (builtin and partitioning), and so are those of pyspark.sql.connect.avro.functions and pyspark.sql.connect.protobuf.functions: they are not those of pyspark.sql.functions, pyspark.sql.avro.functions and pyspark.sql.protobuf.functions, as in PySpark.     pyspark.sql.utils is PySpark's module, of Apache Spark as it is, of its names and its helpers: is_remote() and is_remote_only() are True, and its decorators, such as try_remote_functions and dispatch_df_method, run the functions of PySpark Connect's modules of their names, and the methods of the classes of Spark Connect, which an object of another class does not have (NOT_IMPLEMENTED).     pyspark.sql.SparkSession is PySpark's classic class, the module pyspark.sql.session of Apache Spark as it is, as in PySpark.     The classic classes of pyspark.sql.conf, pyspark.sql.tvf, pyspark.sql.datasource, pyspark.sql.merge, pyspark.sql.observation, pyspark.sql.catalog, pyspark.sql.readwriter, pyspark.sql.udf, pyspark.sql.udtf, pyspark.sql.group, pyspark.sql.pandas.group_ops, pyspark.sql.streaming.readwriter and pyspark.sql.streaming.query are PySpark's, of those modules of Apache Spark as they are, which pyspark.sql and pyspark.sql.streaming name as PySpark's do.     The classic Column, DataFrameNaFunctions, DataFrameStatFunctions, Window, WindowSpec, TableArg and TableValuedFunctionArgument are PySpark's classes, of their modules and MROs (pyspark.sql.column and pyspark.sql.table_arg of Apache Spark as they are), which pyspark.sql names as PySpark's does.     PySpark's classes are plain Python classes, which a user subclasses.     The builder of pyspark.sql.connect.session.SparkSession, SparkSession.Builder(), which SparkSession.builder makes at each access, keeps its state in its __dict__, as PySpark's: its _options, a dict of the to_str of each value that config is given, of any key (remote and appName call the config of the builder, of a subclass too, of spark.remote and spark.app.name), its _channel_builder and its _hook_factories, a list that _registerHook appends to. create and getOrCreate read them at the call, as PySpark's do: a channel builder and a spark.remote, or neither, raise SESSION_NEED_CONN_STR_OR_BUILDER, a spark.remote of None fails PySpark's assertion, and a str that UTF-8 cannot encode raises the UnicodeEncodeError of its channel.     The SparkSession of pyspark.sql.connect.session keeps its state in its __dict__, as PySpark's: its __init__, and the builder, newSession() and cloneSession() of the session that they make, set its _client, the SparkConnectClient of the session that spark.client gives, its _session_id, the _session_id of the client, and its release_session_on_close, True. streams keeps its StreamingQueryManager as its _sqm, unless the session has one, and catalog and version are functools.cached_propertys of PySpark's functions, whose first access keeps the value in the __dict__, which the next ones read: version asks the server once.     The catalog, the table-valued functions, the data source registration and the runtime configuration of a session keep their state in their __dict__, as PySpark's: their __init__ sets the object that it is given, whatever it is, as their _sparkSession (sparkSession of a registration, _client of a configuration), and their methods read that attribute where PySpark's read it, through a property, a __getattr__ or an assignment of a subclass too.     So do the streaming queries, spark.streams and its listener bus, of PySpark's state: a StreamingQuery of its _session, _query_id, _run_id and _name, which id, runId and name give back and its methods read at each request, as _session.client; the StreamingQueryManager of its _session and its _sqlb, the StreamingQueryListenerBus(self) of its __init__, to which addListener and removeListener add and remove the listeners, as self._sqlb.append(listener) and self._sqlb.remove(listener); and the bus, of its _sqm, its _listener_bus list, its _execution_thread and its _lock.     The grouped data, the cogrouped data and the null and statistics functions of a DataFrame keep their state in their __dict__ too, as PySpark's: the _df, _group_type, _grouping_cols, _pivot_col, _pivot_values and _grouping_sets of GroupedData, the grouped data _gd1 and _gd2 of PandasCogroupedOps, and the df of DataFrameNaFunctions and DataFrameStatFunctions, which their __init__ sets of PySpark's arguments.     The DataFrame of pyspark.sql.connect.dataframe keeps its state in its __dict__, as PySpark Connect's: its __init__, of DataFrame(plan, session), sets _plan and _session as they are given, of PySpark's checks (MISSING_VALID_PLAN, NO_ACTIVE_SESSION), and then _support_repr_html, _cached_schema, _cached_schema_serialized and _execution_info, which its methods read and set where PySpark's do: its methods read _plan and _session at each call, those that make a plan give _session to their DataFrame as it is, which the requests resolve, _schema requests the schema while _cached_schema is None, of one request for the first reads of several threads at once, where each of PySpark's threads sends its own, and keeps it with its pickle, which schema loads, the methods that keep the schema of their input share their _cached_schema, the actions set _execution_info, which executionInfo gives, as the callbacks of the writers set it, and _repr_html_ sets _support_repr_html. isStreaming is PySpark's functools.cached_property, of the __dict__. df._plan is one object, the plan object of the drop-in (_DataFramePlan), which DataFrame(df._plan, session) and copy.copy(df) keep, and of which the methods make their DataFrames with the GIL held, of the plan IDs that the plans take when they are made, in their order, as PySpark's.     The DataFrameReader and the DataStreamReader of pyspark.sql.connect are PySpark Connect's classes, adapted from the source of PySpark 4.3.0 (pyspark/sql/connect/readwriter.py and pyspark/sql/connect/streaming/readwriter.py), whose code is PySpark's but for the plans of their reads, which are the Rust core's: the state of a reader is its __dict__, of the _client, _format, _schema and _options (and _source_name) of its __init__, which keeps any object, and its setters, its format methods, _set_opts, its errors, its copies and its pickles are PySpark's.     The Column, the WindowSpec and the TableArg of pyspark.sql.connect keep their state in their __dict__ too, as PySpark's: the _expr of a Column, the _partitionSpec, _orderSpec and _frame of a window specification and the _subquery_expr of a table argument, which their __init__ sets of PySpark's arguments and their methods read at the call. What differs is of the expressions of the core: a Column that a method makes is a value of the expressions at the call, so the window expression of over is of the lists and the frame of the window then, and a later change of them is not of it, where PySpark's WindowExpression holds the window and builds its message of it at each request, and a Column made of a Column of an Expression of PySpark's classes holds its message as its to_plan gave it then. The expressions that the drop-in makes, the _expr of a Column, the entries of the lists of a window and the _subquery_expr of a table argument, are of the client's class _ColumnExpression of pyspark.sql.connect.expressions, of PySpark's to_plan and repr, and not of PySpark's classes of the module (ColumnReference, SortOrder, SubqueryExpression and the others), whose fields they do not have, as the expressions are the core's (see dev/parity/known-differences.md).

    A pickle of PySpark, which makes an object of its class without arguments and then sets its __dict__, loads in the drop-in: a reader, a catalog and a registration, of their __dict__; and the drop-in's pickles of them load in PySpark, of their classes, as PySpark's own do: where the session of their ID is active. The other native classes cannot be subclassed (TypeError: type '...' is not an acceptable base type), and do not construct (TypeError: cannot create '...' instances): their objects are made of the state of the Rust core, a plan, an expression or a query, which the arguments of PySpark's constructors, the Python objects of its client, do not give, as the drop-in does not have them, so a subclass would have no __init__ to call.

    A copy.copy or copy.deepcopy of a DataFrame requests its schema as PySpark's does, one request for the copy, and one more for the DataFrame in a deep copy when it has no schema yet, so a stopped session fails the copy with SparkConnectException, as PySpark's does. One difference remains: PySpark's DataFrame.__getattr__ answers getattr(df, "__deepcopy__") and getattr(df, "__copy__") by requesting the schema, to read the columns, and raises ATTRIBUTE_NOT_SUPPORTED, so hasattr(df, "__copy__") is false; the drop-in defines both itself, which it finds without a request. An unpickled DataFrame requests its schema once, at the unpickle, which raises the error of the request or of its conversion, as PySpark's pickle looks the __setstate__ of the new DataFrame up through its __getattr__. Its cached schema is then that of the pickled DataFrame, as the state of PySpark's pickle sets it, of the objects that it carries: of none, its schema requests it again. So does the unpickle of an object that PySpark pickles with its DataFrame: a writer, of its callback, grouped data, na and stat; a stream writer, which PySpark's pickles of the plan of the DataFrame, requests nothing.

    A copy, a deep copy or an unpickle of a DataFrame keeps the schema that it requests apart from the cached schema of the state, as PySpark's keeps it as the _cached_schema_serialized that its schema returns. So the two differ when the schema on the server changed since the DataFrame cached it, such as that of a view that was replaced, and the copy gives, as PySpark's, the schema that it requested for schema, printSchema, toArrow, the set operations of equal cached schemas (c.union(c).schema) and the numeric aggregations of its grouped data (sum() of no columns), and the cached schema for columns, dtypes, repr, the column lookups and the methods that copy the cached schema, such as filter. A copy of such a copy keeps the schema that its own reconstruction requests, and the cached schema, which a pickle of it carries too. Of a DataFrame that had no cached schema, both give the schema of the server, which the next read of the copy requests again.

    to caches its schema whatever it is, as the object that it is, as PySpark's, so of a DataType that is not a struct, such as LongType(), schema gives a copy of it without a request, while its plan is sent, which the server rejects; and of a StructType changed in place after the call, schema, columns and the plan that the requests send are of the type as it is then. The methods that copy the cached schema of their input in PySpark copy that of to too: alias, filter and where, limit, sort, orderBy and sortWithinPartitions, hint, repartition, repartitionByRange, repartitionById and coalesce, distinct and dropDuplicates, sample, randomSplit and observe, as do the set operations of equal ones.

    The other objects of a session above keep the attributes that are set on them through copies and pickles, as PySpark's copy and pickle their __dict__: a shallow copy shares them, and a deep copy, a pickle and cloudpickle copy them, where they copy the object at all, and where PySpark's fail, the drop-in's fail with the same errors. Their __dict__ holds only those attributes, where PySpark's holds the state of the object too.

    A table name that is neither a str nor bytes, and bytes that are not UTF-8, give a DataFrame of read.table, readStream.table and changes, whose plan raises protobuf's TypeError or the decode error when it is built, as PySpark's do. A reader's format that is not a str is kept as the object it is, which _format gives back, and a copy or a pickle of the reader keeps it. The Connect builder's remote keeps a value that is not a str as PySpark's does, and create rejects it as an invalid Spark Connect URL, as PySpark's create does for its str(). An explicit None, given as remote(None), remote(location=None) or config("spark.remote", None), is kept as None: create and getOrCreate fail PySpark's assertion, unless a session exists, which getOrCreate returns. A later remote replaces it. A remote() without an argument is sc://localhost, as in PySpark's Connect builder. The locks are PySpark's: getOrCreate holds SparkSession._lock from its lookup to the options that it sets, create to make its session the default one, and stop() throughout, and the builder holds SparkSession.Builder._lock in config, remote, appName, channelBuilder and _registerHook, and while it sets the options of a session. Each is a class attribute, an RLock, read at each call, so a thread of the program that holds one holds off the calls of the other threads that take it, and a signal ends the wait for it. As PySpark's, getOrCreate sets the options of a session that it creates twice, in create and again.

    The client honors the HTTP proxy of the environment (grpc_proxy, https_proxy, http_proxy, no_grpc_proxy, no_proxy) with grpcio's rules, see HTTP Proxies, of a loopback host too, unless no_proxy lists it, with one difference: a proxy address with an invalid host or port is the error of the creation of a session of a host that the proxy applies to, where grpcio makes the session and fails its requests, but for a port past 65535, which it takes modulo 65536 and connects to (a proxy of port 99999 is reached at 34463): the error names the variable at once, rather than requests that fail later or a connection to a port that was not given. A host that no_proxy lists connects directly whatever that address, as in grpcio, which resolves it only for a host that it proxies. As grpcio, the client logs a proxy variable that is not a proxy, and a roots file that cannot be read, as errors on stderr (see Errors on stderr).

    The endpoint of a channel builder is read as grpcio's channel of PySpark's toChannel reads its target: bytes as they are, up to a NUL, as gRPC's core reads a C string, else the encode("utf8") of the object; a URI of the scheme of a resolver of gRPC, dns, ipv4, ipv6, unix, unix-abstract, xds or google-c2p, of any case, else dns:/// and the endpoint; of its authority and its path percent-decoded, of their bytes (loc%61lhost:15002 is localhost:15002, and [fe80::1%25eth0]:15002 of the zone eth0), and of its query and its fragment dropped, of the characters that gRPC takes there (127.0.0.1:15002?x=1#y is 127.0.0.1:15002). The name of dns: is the path, a host and a port as gRPC's resolver splits them, of the port 443 without one, 80 and 443 of http and https, of a sign and of spaces around it, and of 16 bits (localhost:99999 is the port 34463), resolved as c-ares resolves it, of the name of each error of the resolver (errors resolving localhost:abc: [field:hostname lookup error:Failed to parse port in name: localhost:abc], Unparsable name, host must not be empty in name, Misformatted domain name); of an authority that is not an IP address and a port, each call fails of gRPC's INTERNAL (Illegal status code from resolver; original status: INVALID_ARGUMENT: Cannot parse authority: ...). Of an authority of an IP address and a port (dns://127.0.0.1:53/spark.example.com:15002), the name is asked of that DNS server alone, as gRPC's resolver has c-ares ask it: for each family at once, of IPv6 too where the system has an IPv6 loopback, the hosts file and DNS are looked up in the order of /etc/nsswitch.conf, the hosts file of the family of an entry, localhost and the names under it are the loopback, which DNS is not asked for, and DNS is asked for the A or the AAAA records of the name, over UDP, of recursion desired and no EDNS, and again over TCP of a truncated answer, of the search domains and ndots of /etc/resolv.conf (on macOS, as c-ares reads them there, of the resolvers of no domain, reachable and of a name server of the system's configuration of DNS, their search lists joined, and /etc/resolv.conf only of no configuration to read; on Windows, of the search list of the registry, the SearchList and the Domain of TCP/IP, the SearchList and the PrimaryDNSSuffix of the policies of its DNS client, and the SearchList, the Domain and the DhcpDomain of each interface), LOCALDOMAIN and RES_OPTIONS (the name first of ndots dots at least, alone of a last .), until an answer has an address: of NXDOMAIN or of no data, the next name is asked, of SERVFAIL, REFUSED or NOTIMP the query is sent again, and of its attempts (3, of attempts) the timeout of the first is that of timeout (2 s, 5 s at most) and that of each next one twice the previous one but a random part of a half at most. The addresses of both families are sorted as gRPC's resolver sorts them (RFC 6724) and tried of each family in turn, as gRPC's pick_first interleaves them; of none, each call fails of c-ares's error of the family that ended last (errors resolving spark.example.com:15002: [field:hostname lookup error:address lookup failed for spark.example.com:15002: Domain name not found], DNS server returned answer with no data, DNS server returned general failure, DNS server refused query, Timeout while contacting DNS servers, Could not contact DNS servers), and a resolution that runs for 120 s, gRPC's timeout of it, or whose TCP connection fails to connect, is cancelled (DNS query cancelled). The addresses of ipv4: and ipv6:, separated by ,, each an IP address of the family, of a zone of IPv6, and a port of 0 to 65535, are tried in turn as those of a host are, and the Unix sockets of unix: and unix-abstract:, of their paths or of their abstract names, of the bytes that their paths decode to (unix:/tmp/%FF.sock); of none, each call fails of UNAVAILABLE (empty address list). A target of google-c2p is its name of DNS, as gRPC's resolver of it on a machine that is not of Google's cloud, and each call of a target of xds fails of UNAVAILABLE, of no bootstrap of xDS in the environment of grpcio's text (Failed to create XdsClient: Environment variables GRPC_XDS_BOOTSTRAP or GRPC_XDS_BOOTSTRAP_CONFIG not defined). Of each target, the channel takes gRPC's names, and not the host of its connection string, which stands for the target only: gRPC's default authority of the target, its path of no first /, percent-encoded as an authority (127.0.0.1:1,127.0.0.1:2, tmp%2Fa.sock), is the :authority of the calls where HTTP can carry it, and of its host up to a % the name that TLS verifies (::1 of [::1%25lo], tmp of tmp%2Fa.sock), as gRPC checks it twice. Once the handshake is done, as its HostNameCertificateVerifier: a name of DNS of the certificate matches it of either case and of a wildcard of its first label alone (*.localhost of xyz.localhost, of x*y.localhost too), an IP address of the certificate as its text (::1, which 0:0:0:0:0:0:0:1 is not), and of a certificate of neither, its common name; of none, each attempt fails of gRPC's Custom verification check failed with error: UNAUTHENTICATED: Hostname Verification Check failed., and nothing is sent. Then of the host of each call, as its SslCheckCallHost, of a wildcard of a rest of two labels at least (*.spark.example): of a certificate that is of the name but not of the host of the calls (*.localhost of xyz.localhost), each call fails of UNAUTHENTICATED (call host does not match SSL server name), which is not retried. Whether a token goes in plaintext is of the host of the authority (localhost) too. The proxy of the environment is chosen of the host of the path, of any target but one of unix:, which gRPC does not proxy, and its CONNECT names the host:port of the path, of the port 443 without one ([127.0.0.1:1,127.0.0.1:2]:443, name:443 of unix-abstract:name), up to a NUL, as gRPC's argument of it is a C string (localhost of localhost%00:15002). A Unix socket is not of gRPC's local credentials of TCP, so a channel of them fails each call to it of Endpoint is neither UDS or TCP loopback address.; a socket of no file fails of gRPC's text of it (FAILED_PRECONDITION: unix:/tmp/a.sock: connect failed: addr: unix:/tmp/a.sock error: No such file or directory). A target that gRPC does not take fails each call of its UNKNOWN (Failed to create channel to '127.0.0.1:15002?':the target uri is not valid: dns:///127.0.0.1:15002?), which PySpark's retry policies do not retry, as they retry UNAVAILABLE. The connection string of the channel takes the host and the port of the target, and nothing else of the endpoint.

    As grpcio's, a channel connects to the addresses of its host, or of its proxy, as the system's resolver gives them, within the connect deadline, but those of localhost and of the names under it, the loopback of both families, as c-ares, grpcio's resolver, gives them (::1, then 127.0.0.1): to the first, then to each next one 250 ms after the previous one, or at once once the previous one failed, while none is done, each with a connect deadline of 20 s of its own, which its TCP connection, the tunnel of the proxy, the TLS handshake and the HTTP/2 settings that the server sends first share. The first to complete is the connection, and the others are dropped. The connection is the process's, of one address, as a subchannel of gRPC's global subchannel pool, which the sessions of the same proxy, TLS and keepalive, and of no token, whose channels resolve the target to that address, share: the channel of each session resolves the name of its target of its own and takes at once a connection of its addresses that another session made, the first of them in their order, as grpcio's pick_first over the subchannels of the pool, else connects to its addresses in turn, so a session of newSession(), cloneSession() or the clone of a client, and any other session of the same connection string, uses the connection of its session while its name resolves to its address, and connects to the addresses that its resolution gives otherwise, while each reads the variables of the proxy again, as grpcio's channel of a new client does; a session of a token has a connection of its own, also of newSession() and cloneSession(), as grpcio's channels of the credentials of a token, of each session, share none. The window of a connection is the largest of HTTP/2, so that the results that a session holds open and does not read on, as of toLocalIterator(), do not hold the calls of the other sessions of the connection, nor its other calls, as gRPC's connections, whose windows grow with those of their streams; the window of each stream, of 2 MiB, bounds what it holds unread. A child of os.fork() makes the threads, the runtime and the channels of the client again, as grpcio's fork support does, so that its sessions, and those of its parent, run queries there, and leaves those of its parent to the parent: a session of the parent that the child frees or stops leaves the connection of its channel to the parent, which reads it on, and an execution of the parent that the child drops is not released. The child closes the sockets of the connections of its parent and the epoll instances of its parent's runtime as the fork returns, as grpcio's fork support closes the descriptors of the event engine of a parent, so that a connection of the parent ends on the server with the parent; the eventfd of its parent's runtime stays, an eventfd of the child's own, which an object of the parent writes to when it wakes a task of that runtime, one descriptor of each generation (see dev/parity/known-differences.md). The client writes the lines of gRPC's core to stderr without the lock of Rust's stderr, and the state that its threads keep is of each generation or read without a lock, so that a lock that a thread of the parent held at the fork does not hold the child. A fork while another thread makes a call can leave the child waiting, as grpcio's fork support asks that no other thread makes a call then, and so can a fork while the client makes a call in the background (a release of an execution, the removal of a cached relation or of a model, the events of the streaming query listeners), which holds the lock of the hooks, the user context extensions, the plan compression or the last execution of its session for the moment that it reads them, and so can a fork while a thread of the client releases a Python object without the GIL, as of a retry policy, whose release PyO3 defers under a lock of its own, which the next call of the child into the client waits for. The tags and the user context extensions of the other threads of the parent stay in the child, which never forgets them, as those threads end in the parent only: a leak bounded by the threads of the parent. A message over the size limit of a call, 128 MiB, as PySpark's channel sets grpc.max_receive_message_length and grpc.max_send_message_length, fails the call with grpcio's RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED, of the details of gRPC's core: Stream removed (CLIENT: Received message larger than max (<length> vs. <limit>)) of a response, and CLIENT: Sent message larger than max (<length> vs. <limit>) of a request, which is not sent. So does the metadata of a response, its headers or its trailers, past gRPC's limits of the metadata that a call receives, of HPACK's sizes of its entries: at 16 KiB (grpc.absolute_max_metadata_size), Stream removed (received metadata size exceeds hard limit (value length <length> vs. 16384)) of an entry too large alone, or (<size> vs. 16384); <entries>, at once, also of metadata far larger, where the drop-in failed with INTERNAL (transport error) or retried an UNAVAILABLE of the end of its connection; and past 8 KiB (grpc.max_metadata_size), at random, the more often the larger it is, of soft limit, of the entries as gRPC's core lists them. The connection advertises the hard limit as the size of a header list that it takes, as grpcio's channel does, so that Spark's server, of grpc-java, resets the stream of a call whose metadata would reach it, as an error whose message has a few hundred CJK characters gives, of grpcio's INTERNAL, Stream removed (RST_STREAM (Received RST_STREAM with error code 1)), where the drop-in raised the hard limit of the metadata that it received; a stream that a server resets fails so of the code of the reason, as gRPC's core gives it. Past the soft limit, the call still has the details of the server's error, as grpcio's call does, and its failure is PySpark's ValueError of grpc_status, Code in Status proto (StatusCode.INTERNAL) doesn't match status code (StatusCode.RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED), where it was the SparkConnectGrpcException of RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED: as PySpark reads the google.rpc.Status of each failure with rpc_status.from_call, the failure of a status that its details do not match, of their code or of their message, is that ValueError, raised as grpcio's error of the call is handled (its __context__), of no log of the error where the DefaultPolicy or the iterator of a reattachable execution reads it, and after GRPC Error received where _handle_rpc_error does (see dev/parity/known-differences.md), of no context where the DefaultPolicy reads it before a retry, as PySpark's Retrying does between its attempts. So is the failure of a grpc-status-details-bin that gRPC's core does not decode as base64: as gRPC's core, the client fails the call INTERNAL of the details Stream removed (Error parsing 'grpc-status-details-bin' metadata: illegal base64 encoding), whatever the code of the server, and gives it the status details empty, a status of the code OK (Code in Status proto (StatusCode.OK) doesn't match status code (StatusCode.INTERNAL)). Of details of base64 that upb's parser, PySpark's protobuf, does not read as a google.rpc.Status, as of one nested past its limit (see above), of a string that is not UTF-8 (String field had bad UTF-8) or of a tag of more than five bytes, the failure is protobuf's DecodeError, as PySpark's rpc_status.from_call raises it, while a field that the type knows of another wire type is read as one that it does not know, as upb reads it; and so is the failure of a status whose first ErrorInfo, which _handle_rpc_error unpacks, or first RetryInfo, which the DefaultPolicy unpacks, upb does not read (Error parsing message with type 'google.rpc.ErrorInfo': Wire format was corrupt), where PySpark unpacks it. The first detail of a type is the first that protobuf's Any.Is tells of it, of a type URL of a /, of any host, and the metadata of an ErrorInfo holds the entries that upb sets in its map, not those of a field that the entry type does not know or of a key or a value of another wire type. As PySpark's convert_exception, the classes and the messageParameters of the metadata are parsed with Python's json.loads, of the values of the JSON as they are, and its error is the failure, as the JSONDecodeError of JSON that does not parse, of grpcio's error as its context (or as that of the StopIteration of json that it suppresses); the errorClass of the metadata, also empty, is the condition. The delay of a RetryInfo is PySpark's, the ToMilliseconds() of its retry_delay, signed and unbounded and 0 of one of none, which the log gives as it is, and so is the wait before a retry, of Python's numbers, which a negative delay never lengthens. A negative wait, and one of decades, are slept of Python's time.sleep, as PySpark's Retrying sleeps them, which raises at once, of no context, of one that it cannot hold: its ValueError of a negative one (sleep length must be non-negative), its OverflowError of one past its nanoseconds of 64 bits (timestamp out of range for platform time_t from Python 3.11 on), or the system's OSError of one that the clock cannot hold. A signal that comes while it sleeps ends the wait as one that comes during any other wait of the call: its KeyboardInterrupt interrupts the execution in flight. A request sent in the background sleeps it on a thread of Python of its own, and fails of its error, which is logged as PySpark's sender logs it (ReleaseExecute failed with exception: ...). A policy that PySpark's Retrying decides, as a subclass of DefaultPolicy, is asked of a failure as grpcio's error of the call is handled, as PySpark's AttemptManager asks it, so that what its can_retry raises has that error as its context. A channel that cannot connect fails its calls with UNAVAILABLE, which the retry policy retries, of the last error of its addresses: of a plaintext server that accepts the connection and never answers, grpcio's failed to connect to all addresses; last error: UNKNOWN: ipv4:127.0.0.1:15002: connection attempt timed out before receiving SETTINGS frame. Once it failed, it fails the later calls at once, of that error, while each of its addresses connects again in the background with gRPC's backoff, 1 s and then 1.6 times longer each time up to 120 s, with a jitter of 20 %, each attempt with the longer of its backoff and 20 s as its deadline, once for each address of the process, whatever the sessions that wait for it, until a connection succeeds, which the next call takes. The channel of each session, of a new session or a clone too, resolves the name of its target of its own before its first call, as the resolver of each channel of grpcio does, whose calls fail of the error of it, at once, while it tries it again in the background a backoff after its end, as gRPC's resolver does, and connects once it resolves; and it resolves the name again of its own, in the background, as its connection ends, or as a GOAWAY of the server ends it for new calls, which then connect again while the calls in flight go on, and each time its addresses failed again, no sooner than 30 s after the start of its last resolution, as gRPC's resolver resolves a name again no sooner (grpc.dns_min_time_between_resolutions_ms), of whose addresses it connects meanwhile: a resolution that fails after one that succeeded leaves it the addresses of that one, and a resolution that comes while it is connected makes it take at once the first connection of the new addresses that the process has, its own too, or, of none, close its own once no other session uses it and connect to the new addresses at its next call, as gRPC's pick_first drops its connection and goes idle then. Once no call came for 30 min to a channel that failed, or once its session is stopped, it stops connecting, as grpcio's channel goes idle, or shuts down, and the next call connects. A connection that ends is made again by the next call. The Python client logs the failures of the connections that gRPC's core logs, on stderr (see Errors on stderr), and its CONNECT request of a proxy has grpcio's User-Agent: grpc-httpcli/0.0. What stays different, such as the keepalive pings, which are hyper's, is in dev/parity/known-differences.md.

    The user agent, <user_agent> spark/4.3.0 os/<os> python/<version> of the version of Python, as PySpark's ChannelBuilder.userAgent makes it, is sent as PySpark sends it: as the client type of the requests that PySpark builds with it, and not as a header. The user-agent header is the client library's own, tonic/<version> here and grpcio's in PySpark, such as grpc-python/1.85.1 grpc-c/58.0.0 (linux; chttp2). A user-agent parameter of the connection string is checked as metadata and not sent, as grpcio replaces it with its own. So a user agent may hold any character, a control character included. As PySpark's userAgent, which its requests read when they are built, a user agent longer than 2048 characters after URL escaping, or a SPARK_CONNECT_USER_AGENT that is not UTF-8, is the error of each request that carries it, which is not sent: SparkConnectException, or the UnicodeEncodeError of urllib.parse.quote. The session is made all the same, its stop() logs the error as that of its release, and the requests that PySpark builds without the user agent, of the artifacts and of CloneSession, are sent. As urllib.parse.urlsplit does, a tab, CR or LF anywhere in the connection string is removed. SPARK_CONNECT_USER_AGENT is read as each call of the client starts, so that a change of os.environ applies to the next call, as PySpark's requests read the variable when each is built, and a request that the transport builds during a call, such as a reattachment or a release in the background, has the user agent of the last call that started.

    A gRPC failure whose status carries no error of the server, such as of an unreachable server or of a proxy, is the SparkConnectGrpcException of its code whose message is the text of grpcio's error of the call, as PySpark's _handle_rpc_error raises it: <_InactiveRpcError of RPC that terminated with:, or <_MultiThreadedRendezvous for ExecutePlan and ReattachExecute, and the lines of its code (status = StatusCode.UNAVAILABLE), its details and its debug_error_string, as grpcio 1.85 gives them. The message of a call past its deadline begins with that text, as PySpark's f"{error}: RPC deadline exceeded. ...", and that of a status of a google.rpc.Status without an ErrorInfo is the message of the status. The details are the message of a status that the server sent, and the debug_error_string is the name of the code and the details, as in grpcio. Those of a failure that the client detects itself are those of gRPC's C core: of a channel that cannot connect, failed to connect to all addresses; last error: UNKNOWN: ipv4:127.0.0.1:1: Failed to connect to remote host: Connection refused, of the last error of its addresses, such as a TLS handshake of BoringSSL's error or a proxy that refuses the tunnel (HTTP proxy returned response code 403), of a name that does not resolve, errors resolving ..., and of a call that the end of its connection fails, Stream removed (Socket closed), or Stream removed (sendmsg: Broken pipe (32)) of a write of it that fails. The errors of copyFromLocalToFs and cloneSession, which PySpark leaves to grpc, are grpcio's RpcError, as there (see SparkSession.client).

    As PySpark's Retrying, a call whose retries of a failure that a policy retries are spent warns [RETRIES_EXCEEDED] The maximum number of retries has been exceeded. before it raises the failure: a UserWarning of the location of PySpark's warnings.warn in pyspark.sql.connect.client.retries, and of that module, which its filters and the actions of a location take as PySpark's, and which a filter of "error" raises in place of the failure. So do the lookup of the plan compression of the first plan of a session, whose failure is caught, the read of the conf of the server's stack trace as an error of the server is raised, which is retried as PySpark's, and the release of the session of stop(). A policy retries the failures that its can_retry takes, as PySpark's Retrying asks it, also a subclass of RetryPolicy that is not a DefaultPolicy, and warns when its retries are spent; policies none of which takes a failure, such as PySpark's own RetryPolicy, retry nothing, and do not warn. The requests that the client sends in the background, which no caller waits for, warn too, and their failures are logged as PySpark's senders log them (ReleaseExecute failed with exception: ...), from the delivery thread of the drop-in (see dev/parity/known-differences.md). stop() logs the failure of the release of its session to the logger of Spark Connect (pyspark.sql.connect.logging), which is disabled unless SPARK_CONNECT_LOG_LEVEL is set, as PySpark's: session.stop(): Session could not be released. Error: $<error>.

    With SPARK_CONNECT_LOG_LEVEL, or a level of the logger of pyspark.sql.connect.logging, the client logs to the logger of Spark Connect what PySpark's client logs, of the same levels, messages and order, through the logger's methods, as PySpark's: the deadlines of a new client (Spark Connect RPC deadlines: ...); the settings of the plan compression that the first plan of a session reads (Plan compression threshold: ..., or the error of a failed read as the argument of a text of no placeholder for it, as PySpark's) and the compression of a plan (Plan compression: original_size= ..., of the frame of python-zstandard's compress, and Plan compression not effective. Using original plan.); the plan of each execution and analysis, as the method of PySpark's client that it stands for logs it (Executing plan ..., Execute command for command ..., Execute command as iterator for command ..., Schema for plan: ..., Explain (mode=...) for plan ..., Execute and ExecuteAndFetch), of the text of SparkConnectClient._proto_to_string of the drop-in's protobuf runtime; the request of each execution and each response as the execution reads it (ExecuteAndFetchAsIterator. Request: ... and Response received: ..., Received the schema., Received the SQL command result., Received metric batch., Received observed metric batch., Received arrow batch ... and Assembling arrow batch ...); the deadline of a stream that the execution reattaches (Deadline exceeded on stream for operation ...) and the interrupt of an execution by a KeyboardInterrupt (Interrupt request received for operation=...), after which nothing more of the execution is logged of its responses, as PySpark's reads nothing more of them (PySpark's release of the execution, which it sends as it interrupts it, may fail on the server, which it logs, ReleaseExecute failed with exception: ..., where the drop-in releases it once the server ended it); each retry and the end of the retries of a call (Given up on retrying. error: ...); each gRPC failure that it converts (GRPC Error received, of grpcio's RpcError, Received ErrorInfo: ... of an error of the server, and Disabling plan compression for the session due to CONNECT_INVALID_PLAN.CANNOT_PARSE error.); and of SparkSession.client, its resources and resource profiles (Fetching the resources, Creating the ResourceProfile) and a delete or a cleanup of the ML cache that it skips while one is in flight on its thread (Skipping re-entrant ML cache ...). The exception of a handler or a filter of the logger at a record fails the call there, as PySpark's: before the request of an execution when the record precedes it, and of a KeyboardInterrupt at a response, of toLocalIterator too, with the interrupt of the execution, as PySpark's except KeyboardInterrupt, but at a record of the conversion of the failure of the execution (GRPC Error received, Received ErrorInfo: ...), which PySpark's except Exception writes, of no interrupt; the exception of a record that the client writes as it handles a failure, of the conversion of a failure or of the failed lookup of the plan compression, has the failure as its context, as PySpark's. A call of the client that fails while the client converts another failure on the thread, as a call of a handler of such a record, raises its error as grpc gives it, grpcio's RpcError of the call, of no records, no request of the details of the error or of its stack-trace settings, and no _operation_id, as PySpark's _handle_error raises the error of a request as it is while it handles another (inside_error_handling); the failure of a call of the client of another session is converted. A call of a closed client, as of a stopped session, writes the records that come before the request, and then raises NO_ACTIVE_SESSION, as PySpark's client, which reads its _stub, of that error, only to send. The level of the logger is read once per call, as the call starts: a record of a level that the logger does not write is not made, and nothing is formatted for it, so a level that a progress handler enables during a call applies from the next call on, while one that it disables applies at once, as the logger's methods filter the records. With SPARK_CONNECT_DEBUG_CLIENT_CALL_STACK, the requests of the executions, the analyses and the configurations carry the call stack of the user's code, as PySpark's (see dev/parity/known-differences.md for both).

    For TLS roots, including GRPC_DEFAULT_SSL_ROOTS_FILE_PATH, see TLS Root Certificates.

    Native NaiveBayes¶

    The Rust ml::NaiveBayes and ml::NaiveBayesModel train and predict through Spark Connect's cached-model protocol. The Rust-backed Python API exports NaiveBayes from pyspark.ml.connect and pyspark.ml.connect.classification; fit returns the typed NaiveBayesModel, whose transform produces DataFrame predictions.

    All nine PySpark constructor parameters and named setters are supported: featuresCol, labelCol, predictionCol, probabilityCol, rawPredictionCol, smoothing, modelType, thresholds and weightCol. Rust exposes corresponding builder setters and getters. Unset parameters are omitted so the server uses its defaults. The Python constructor is keyword-only and converts values with PySpark's parameter converters.

    All four model types are covered by live tests: multinomial, bernoulli, complement and gaussian. Multinomial/complement inputs must be nonnegative; Bernoulli inputs must be binary; Gaussian inputs may contain continuous negative values. Tests also cover multiclass dense/sparse inputs, probability/raw score columns, smoothing and instance weights, threshold decisions, suppressed vector outputs, independent fits, session isolation and lazy model lifetime. A golden fixture captures all nine parameters for each model type from official PySpark.

    The Rust NaiveBayesModel has PySpark 4.3.0's model members: pi, theta and sigma (a 0 by 0 matrix for multinomial, complement and Bernoulli models), numClasses, numFeatures, predict, predictRaw and predictProbability of one vector, and NaiveBayesModel::load. NaiveBayes::load reads a saved estimator with one ML Read, as PySpark's NaiveBayes.load does. The estimator's fit returns this typed model. PySpark 4.3.0 gives the model no training summary. The Rust param accessors are generated from PySpark's param table: features_col and set_features_col replace the former feature_col and set_feature_col.

    In Python, NaiveBayes and its NaiveBayesModel follow the full API of pyspark.ml (see "Native ML classes of the full pyspark.ml API"): the Params API, pi, theta, sigma, numClasses, numFeatures, predict, predictRaw and predictProbability of a vector, and save and load. A NaiveBayes model has no training summary, as in PySpark. The vendored pyspark.ml.classification API continues to use its existing implementation.

    Native BisectingKMeans¶

    The Rust ml::BisectingKMeans and ml::BisectingKMeansModel support training and DataFrame cluster assignment through Spark Connect's cached-model protocol. Python exports the native estimator from pyspark.ml.connect and pyspark.ml.connect.clustering; fit returns its BisectingKMeansModel.

    All eight constructor parameters and named setters are supported: featuresCol, predictionCol, maxIter, seed, k, minDivisibleClusterSize, distanceMeasure and weightCol. Rust also provides parameter getters. The keyword-only Python constructor matches official PySpark's order/defaults and parameter conversions. Unset parameters are omitted so the server uses its defaults, including k=4.

    Live tests cover hierarchical splitting, absolute/fractional minimum divisible cluster sizes (which may produce fewer than k leaves), instance weights, Euclidean/cosine distance, sparse/dense inputs, seeded reproducibility, independent fits, session isolation and lazy model lifetime. Golden fixtures compare all eight parameters at six signed seed/integer-width boundaries with official PySpark.

    The Rust model's members, summaries and persistence are in "Native clustering models (Rust API)". In Python, BisectingKMeans, its BisectingKMeansModel and BisectingKMeansSummary follow the full API of pyspark.ml (see "Native ML classes of the full pyspark.ml API"): the Params API, clusterCenters(), computeCost(dataset), numFeatures, predict of a vector, the training summary (k, clusterSizes, trainingCost, numIter, predictions and cluster, of the transform of the training dataset, as PySpark's), and save and load. The vendored pyspark.ml.clustering API is unchanged.

    Native GeneralizedLinearRegression¶

    spark_connect::ml::GeneralizedLinearRegression fits Spark's JVM estimator and returns a cached GeneralizedLinearRegressionModel. Python exposes it from pyspark.ml.connect and pyspark.ml.connect.regression; fit returns its GeneralizedLinearRegressionModel.

    All 16 PySpark constructor parameters and named setters are available: labelCol, featuresCol, predictionCol, family, link, fitIntercept, maxIter, tol, regParam, weightCol, solver, linkPredictionCol, variancePower, linkPower, offsetCol and aggregationDepth. Rust provides builder setters and getters for each parameter. Unset optional parameters stay unset: Spark chooses the family default link, or the Tweedie link power from variancePower.

    Fitting accepts ML vector features and numeric labels. Transform produces predictions and, when requested, the linear predictor in linkPredictionCol. Instance weights, offsets and L2 regularization are handled by Spark. The same numerical tests run against official PySpark and the native Python client, covering all five families (Gaussian, binomial, Poisson, Gamma and Tweedie), default and explicit links, dense/sparse features, weights, offsets, independent fitted models, lazy model ownership and rejected inputs. Official PySpark golden fixtures verify the parameter encoding, and live Rust tests exercise fitting and prediction directly.

    The Rust model's members and persistence are in "Native regression models (Rust API)". In Python, GeneralizedLinearRegression, its GeneralizedLinearRegressionModel, GeneralizedLinearRegressionSummary and GeneralizedLinearRegressionTrainingSummary follow the full API of pyspark.ml (see "Native ML classes of the full pyspark.ml API"): the Params API, coefficients, intercept, numFeatures, predict of a vector, the training summary (of the training dataset, as PySpark's, with residuals(residualsType) and the statistics of the coefficients), evaluate, and save and load. As PySpark's over Spark Connect, residuals sends its positional arguments only: residuals(residualsType="pearson") gives the deviance residuals. As with PySpark, the repr of a model whose link is not set fails on the server.

    Native GaussianMixture¶

    Rust ml::GaussianMixture and ml::GaussianMixtureModel train Gaussian mixture models and produce cluster assignments and component probabilities through Spark Connect's cached-model protocol. Python exports the native estimator from pyspark.ml.connect and pyspark.ml.connect.clustering; fit returns its GaussianMixtureModel.

    All nine constructor parameters and named setters are supported: featuresCol, predictionCol, k, probabilityCol, tol, maxIter, seed, aggregationDepth and weightCol. Rust also provides corresponding getters. The keyword-only Python constructor matches official PySpark's order/defaults and conversions. Unset values are omitted so the server uses its defaults.

    Live tests cover one- and two-dimensional mixtures, two/three components, normalized probabilities and assignments, dense/sparse vectors, weights, seeded reproducibility, independent fits, disabled probability output, server validation, session isolation and lazy model lifetime. Golden fixtures compare all nine parameters at six signed seed/integer-width boundaries with official PySpark.

    The Rust model's members, summaries and persistence are in "Native clustering models (Rust API)". In Python, GaussianMixture, its GaussianMixtureModel and GaussianMixtureSummary follow the full API of pyspark.ml (see "Native ML classes of the full pyspark.ml API"): the Params API, weights, gaussiansDF, gaussians (a MultivariateGaussian of each collected row of gaussiansDF, as PySpark builds them), numFeatures, predict and predictProbability of a vector, the training summary (probability, probabilityCol, logLikelihood and the members of a clustering summary, of the transform of the training dataset, as PySpark's), and save and load. The vendored pyspark.ml.clustering API is unchanged.

    Native FMClassifier¶

    spark_connect::ml::FMClassifier and pyspark.ml.connect.classification.FMClassifier fit Spark's binary factorization machine classifier. The 17 parameters of PySpark's constructor have Rust builder setters and getters and named Python setters; weightCol, a param of the class that PySpark's constructor does not take and that PySpark has no setter of, has a Rust getter only. Fitted models produce prediction, probability and raw-prediction columns, with configurable thresholds. Both the gd and adamW solvers run on the Spark server.

    The Rust FMClassificationModel has factors, linear, intercept, predict, predictRaw and predictProbability of one vector, numClasses, numFeatures, and load. FMClassifier::load reads a saved estimator with one ML Read, as PySpark's FMClassifier.load does. Its summary() is the binary training summary of a fitted model, and has_summary() says whether one exists; summary() fails for a loaded model, as PySpark does. evaluate(&df) returns the binary summary of a DataFrame, with the ROC, PR and threshold DataFrames.

    In Python, FMClassifier, its FMClassificationModel and their summaries follow the full API of pyspark.ml (see "Native ML classes of the full pyspark.ml API"): the Params API, factors, linear, intercept, numClasses, numFeatures, predict, predictRaw and predictProbability of a vector, summary() (the training summary: objectiveHistory, totalIterations, roc, pr and the other curves), evaluate, and save and load.

    Native MultilayerPerceptronClassifier¶

    spark_connect::ml::MultilayerPerceptronClassifier and its native Python binding support the 14 params of PySpark's class: the 13 of its keyword-only constructor, and thresholds, which the constructor does not take, as PySpark's does not, and which setThresholds (set_thresholds in Rust) sets. Configure layers before fitting; input and output sizes must match the feature dimension and number of classes. initialWeights accepts dense or sparse ML vectors: Rust uses MlVector (a Vec<f64> or array also converts to a dense vector), and Python accepts the values supported by PySpark's TypeConverters.toVector. Layer arrays and vector parameters are encoded in Rust and checked against fixtures captured with official PySpark.

    The fitted model produces multiclass prediction, probability and raw-prediction columns. Both l-bfgs and gd are supported. As in PySpark, fitting an empty DataFrame can return a model; the native client preserves that server behavior.

    The Rust MultilayerPerceptronClassificationModel has weights, predict, predictRaw and predictProbability of one vector, numClasses, numFeatures, and load. MultilayerPerceptronClassifier::load reads a saved estimator with one ML Read, as PySpark's MultilayerPerceptronClassifier.load does. Its summary() is the multiclass training summary of a fitted model, and has_summary() says whether one exists. summary() fails for a loaded model, as PySpark does. evaluate(&df) returns the summary of a DataFrame. The param accessors are generated from PySpark's param table.

    In Python, MultilayerPerceptronClassifier, its MultilayerPerceptronClassificationModel and their summaries follow the full API of pyspark.ml (see "Native ML classes of the full pyspark.ml API"): the Params API, weights, numClasses, numFeatures, predict, predictRaw and predictProbability of a vector, summary() (the training summary: objectiveHistory and totalIterations), evaluate, and save and load.

    Native OneVsRest¶

    spark_connect::ml::OneVsRest and pyspark.ml.connect.classification.OneVsRest implement the multiclass reduction in Rust. Supply a native binary classifier, for example OneVsRest::new().set_classifier(LinearSVC::new()) in Rust or OneVsRest(classifier=LinearSVC()) in Python. The Classifier enum accepts all eight native binary classifiers: LogisticRegression, LinearSVC, DecisionTreeClassifier, RandomForestClassifier, GBTClassifier, NaiveBayes, FMClassifier and MultilayerPerceptronClassifier. Perceptron base classifiers need a two-unit output layer.

    In Python, the classifier can be any other object, as in PySpark. The native fit is that of a classifier of exactly a native class. Any other classifier, such as PySpark's own classes of pyspark.ml.classification, or a Python subclass of them or of a native class, is fit by PySpark's own OneVsRest._fit over the estimator's params, which runs the classifier's own fit and _fit for each class (classifier.fit(trainingDataset, paramMap)), overrides of a subclass too. Its model is PySpark's OneVsRestModel of the classifier's own models. A None classifier is fit there too, and fails at its first use. So is a dataset that is not a Spark DataFrame: a pandas DataFrame raises the KeyError of PySpark's pandas aggregate of its max label.

    All seven PySpark constructor parameters have named setters. Rust exposes scalar parameter getters and the configured classifier. Fits clone the base classifier for each class and run at most parallelism fits at once. Python observes mutations to the supplied classifier made before fit, while fitting leaves that classifier unchanged. Instance weights are forwarded to each class's fit when the base classifier is a HasWeightCol of PySpark, which every native classifier is but the perceptron (FMClassifier included). For the perceptron, Python warns weightCol is ignored, as it is not supported by <uid> now., with the classifier's uid, as PySpark does, and fits without the weights. The warning is attributed as PySpark's is, to the line of its warnings.warn in pyspark/ml/classification.py and to the module pyspark.ml.classification, whose filters match it.

    A fit runs the steps of PySpark's OneVsRest._fit, in its order and of its requests, in Rust and in Python, but for the reads of each class's model that PySpark's makes after its fit (numClasses of a LogisticRegression model), which the Rust API makes when they are asked for:

    • Classes: the fit reads the max label first, as dataset.agg({labelCol: "max"}).head()["max(" + labelCol + ")"]: the max of the label column as named, which the server resolves (a name with a dot names a field of a struct), of the column's own type. The classes are int(max) + 1, of Python's int, so the max of a string column is its greatest string (labels "10", "9" and "0" fit ten classes, and the rows of "10" are of no class), a fraction is truncated (a max of -0.5 fits one class), and a null (of no rows), a NaN, an infinity or a string that is no integer raises the TypeError, ValueError or OverflowError of int. Python takes PySpark's own Python values and int. Rust converts the max as int does, of the same messages, but of the ASCII digits of a string only, where Python's int takes the decimal digits of other scripts too.
    • Dataset: the label, features and weight columns (dataset.select(labelCol, featuresCol, weightCol)) are cached while the classes fit when the input is not persisted, as PySpark's _cache_spark_dataset(..., StorageLevel.MEMORY_AND_DISK) caches them: persisted, and unpersisted after the fits and the reads of their models; or, with SPARKML_TEMP_DFS_PATH set, saved under that path, read back, and deleted after by the ML helper (handleOverwrite). The binary label of each class is 1.0 where the label column of that dataset (multiclassLabeled[labelCol]) is the class, and the training summary of each class's model is of its binary training set.
    • Pool: then PySpark makes its list of the tasks of the classes and its pool of min(parallelism, classes) threads, so that a parallelism below one, a null one, and a max label below zero raise their errors after the max label is read and the dataset is cached: ValueError: Number of processes must be at least 1, or the TypeError of min of a None. This client fits at most 2147483647 classes: a count beyond raises a MemoryError in Python (and an error in Rust), where PySpark allocates its list of that many tasks ([trainSingleClass] * numClasses, of 8 bytes a class, 16 GiB of 2^31 classes, before any fit), and beyond 2^63 the OverflowError of that list, as PySpark's.
    • Params: the params are read where PySpark reads them, with no check of their own: an unset classifier raises PySpark's KeyError of the param, a null labelCol the AttributeError of F.col(None), a null featuresCol the TypeError of the select (after the max query), a null predictionCol is sent to the classifier's fits, which the server rejects, and a null rawPredictionCol fits, as PySpark's does. In Python, parallelism is an int of any size, which PySpark compares and never sends, and which saves and loads as it is.

    The model aggregates the positive-class raw scores of its submodels into rawPrediction and chooses the first highest score for prediction, ignoring NaN/null scores as PySpark does; it produces no probability column. In Python, transform is PySpark's own OneVsRestModel._transform, and the Rust transform is a port of it, with the same DataFrame operations and so the same requests. Each submodel transforms the data with its own featuresCol and rawPredictionCol, not the model's, so a model whose featuresCol names no column still transforms, and a model rawPredictionCol that the submodels do not produce fails with UNRESOLVED_COLUMN when the transform is evaluated, as PySpark's does. The scores are accumulated in a mbc$acc<uuid> column. When the input is not persisted, the transform persists it at MEMORY_AND_DISK while it builds the composition and unpersists it then, as PySpark's _cache_spark_dataset does. With SPARKML_TEMP_DFS_PATH set, a copy of the input is written there and read back, and the copy is deleted before the transform returns: as in PySpark, the returned DataFrame then fails with PATH_NOT_FOUND when it is evaluated. Temporary columns and fit-created caches are cleaned up, and transformed DataFrames retain the binary models for lazy evaluation. Models must transform data in their fitting session.

    Rust OneVsRest::fit returns the typed OneVsRestModel. Its models() gives each binary submodel as its typed model, one SubModel variant per native classifier, and classifier() gives the classifier. Single-vector prediction is on the submodels. OneVsRestModel::new(session, models) constructs a model of the given models, of a new uid and no set params or classifier, as PySpark's OneVsRestModel(models), and its set_classifier sets the classifier.

    Persistence is of PySpark's Python format, in Rust and in Python, as PySpark's OneVsRestWriter, OneVsRestModelWriter and their readers write it: OneVsRest::write().save(path) and OneVsRest::load(session, path), and the same for OneVsRestModel. The metadata of the estimator has its params without the classifier, which is saved at classifier, in the order of the maps of the Python object that it saves, as PySpark's extractJsonParams writes them (of the constructor's defaults of a OneVsRest of its constructor, of the names of a load or a copy); the metadata of the model also has numClasses, and each submodel is saved at model_<index> by its own writer. What each of PySpark and this client saves, the other loads, with the same uids, params and predictions, and the metadata files equal PySpark's apart from their timestamps and uids (of a subclass, of its class, see "Subclasses" above). As PySpark's readers, the loads read metadata of any class, of PySpark's Python format and of the JVM's class names, which classic PySpark's JavaMLWriter (and Scala's writer) saves of a OneVsRest of a Java classifier, in the same layout, also as a stage of a pipeline. A load of a model loads its classifier and each of its models once, of PySpark's requests. A CrossValidator or a TrainValidationSplit over a OneVsRest saves and loads with it, and a param map of the validator sets the params of the classifier, as PySpark's copy does. In Rust, a param map of a model sets the params of its models (apply_param_map), as PySpark's OneVsRestModel.copy(extra) copies each model with it.

    In Python, the OneVsRest estimator has the Params API of pyspark.ml (its classifier param included), setClassifier, fitMultiple and clear, and its fit returns the typed OneVsRestModel, which has classifier, the other params, transform, and a uid of its own, as PySpark's. OneVsRestModel(models) constructs a model of the given models, of a new uid and the defaults of the class, with no set params and no classifier, as PySpark's constructor does, and its models attribute can be assigned. Its models are the typed submodels, each with the uid of the classifier, the binary label of its class as labelCol, the classifier's params, and its training summary. They are made from the fitted submodels without a request, each of the typed model class of its classifier. A LogisticRegression submodel is a typed LogisticRegressionModel, with its training summary; a DecisionTreeClassification submodel is a typed DecisionTreeClassificationModel; a RandomForestClassification submodel is a typed RandomForestClassificationModel with its training summary (binary, as its class's fit is); and a GBTClassification submodel is a typed GBTClassificationModel, with the trees of its ensemble. The per-class fits send the requests of PySpark's, and PySpark's client also reads the post-fit requests of each submodel: numClasses of a LogisticRegression submodel, and numClasses and trees of a RandomForestClassification one, and trees of a GBTClassification one. PySpark's makes the reads of a class right after its fit, on the thread of its pool that fits it (its try_remote_fit), so they come between the fits of the classes; this client's core fits the classes, in the threads of its own pool, and then the Python client makes the reads of each class, in their order, once all the fits are done: the requests are the same, of another order. Their order would need a Python call of each class's fit on the threads of the core's pool, for no other difference.

    As PySpark's, the write() of a OneVsRest or a OneVsRestModel gives its writer, whose save raises the KeyError of the unset classifier param of one of no classifier (a OneVsRestModel of its constructor too), and then PySpark's ValueError of validateParams of a classifier or a submodel that is not writable (OneVsRest write will fail because it contains ... which is not writable.), before any request.

    Native AFTSurvivalRegression, IsotonicRegression and FMRegressor¶

    All eight regression estimators in PySpark 4.3.0 now have native Rust fit/transform implementations. The three additions are available under spark_connect::ml and pyspark.ml.connect.regression, and are re-exported from pyspark.ml.connect.
