They fit Spark's JVM estimators and retain the returned server-cached models for as long as a model clone or transformed DataFrame needs them.

    • AFTSurvivalRegression fits a Weibull accelerated failure time model. It accepts positive survival times and a censor column (1 for observed events, 0 for censored observations), and can produce a vector of requested quantiles. All 11 constructor parameters have Rust and Python setters, including quantileProbabilities, quantilesCol, and the aggregation/block-size settings.
    • IsotonicRegression fits increasing or decreasing univariate regression. It supports scalar features or a selected vector component (featureIndex), instance weights, interpolation between fitted boundaries, and clamping beyond the training range. All six constructor parameters have setters.
    • FMRegressor fits factorization machines with pairwise interactions and optional intercept and linear terms, using either adamW or gd. All 14 constructor parameters have setters, including factor size, initialization, mini-batch fraction, regularization and seed.

    Unset parameters use Spark's defaults. The Python wrappers preserve PySpark's keyword-only constructor signatures and type conversion, and take ML vector features, as pyspark.ml's do.

    The Rust models' members and persistence are in "Native regression models (Rust API)". In Python, the three estimators and their models follow the full API of pyspark.ml (see "Native ML classes of the full pyspark.ml API"): the Params API, numFeatures and predict, coefficients, intercept, scale and predictQuantiles of a survival model, boundaries and predictions of an isotonic model, whose predict takes a single feature value, intercept, linear and factors of an FM model, and save and load.

    Parameter goldens come from official PySpark 4.3.0. Native Rust and Python tests exercise censoring and quantiles, weighted isotonic interpolation, both FM solvers, validation errors and cached-model lifetimes. The Python behavior tests also run unchanged against the official client.

    Native LDA and PowerIterationClustering¶

    The native Rust and Python APIs now implement all five clustering algorithms in PySpark 4.3.0: KMeans, BisectingKMeans, GaussianMixture, LDA and PowerIterationClustering. The Rust models' members and persistence are in "Native clustering models (Rust API)".

    Rust ml::LDA supports online and EM training and cached-model DataFrame transforms. Python exposes LDA through pyspark.ml.connect and pyspark.ml.connect.clustering; fit returns a DistributedLDAModel of the em optimizer and a LocalLDAModel of the online one, as PySpark's. All 14 constructor parameters have Python setters and Rust builder setters/getters, including document/topic concentrations, learning settings, checkpoint settings, seed and topic-distribution output column. Both optimizers return a vector of topic probabilities per document; empty documents produce zero vectors. As with pyspark.ml, an array feature column is sent as it is, and the JVM rejects one whose elements may be null, such as the arrays of createDataFrame.

    ml::PowerIterationClustering::assign_clusters and Python assignClusters build Spark Connect's powerIterationClusteringAssignClusters helper relation. This algorithm has no fit/transform API or fitted model. It accepts source/destination vertex columns and optional nonnegative edge weights, with k, maxIter and initMode settings, and returns a Spark DataFrame with id: bigint and cluster: int. Parameter values are captured when the relation is built. Omitting weightCol uses unweighted edges; an explicit null remains distinct from an omitted parameter and is validated by Spark. The lazy relation retains any cached models used by its input DataFrame.

    Both APIs require Spark DataFrames and reject pandas inputs. Reference-derived golden fixtures and live tests cover both LDA optimizers, dense/sparse inputs, graph initialization modes, weighted/unweighted graphs, custom columns, setters, validation and model lifetime. The Rust models' members and persistence are in "Native clustering models (Rust API)". In Python, LDA, LDAModel, LocalLDAModel and DistributedLDAModel follow the full API of pyspark.ml (see "Native ML classes of the full pyspark.ml API"): the Params API, the members of an LDA model (describeTopics, topicsMatrix, logLikelihood, logPerplexity, vocabSize, isDistributed and estimatedDocConcentration; describeTopics sends its positional arguments only, as PySpark's over Spark Connect, so describeTopics(maxTermsPerTopic=1) describes the server's 10 terms), toLocal (the same LocalLDAModel on every call, as PySpark's, of the constructor of its class), trainingLogLikelihood, logPrior and getCheckpointFiles of a distributed model, and save and load. PowerIterationClustering has its uid and the Params API, and saves and loads as PySpark's does: as the params of the server's PowerIterationClusteringWrapper. LDAModel is the base class of the two LDA models, as PySpark's, with the members of an LDA model but no persistence of its own: an LDA model is an instance of it, LDAModel.load is an AttributeError, and a copy or a load of a model is of the model's class. The vendored pyspark.ml.clustering API is unchanged.

    Native feature stages¶

    All 38 standard pyspark.ml.feature estimators and transformers have native Rust execution and Python bindings. Rust exposes them as spark_connect::ml::<Name>, with typed builder setters/getters. Python exports them from pyspark.ml.connect and pyspark.ml.connect.feature, with standard pyspark.ml.feature keyword-only constructor signatures and named mutating setters. The vendored pyspark.ml.feature module is unchanged.

    Area Stages
    Text and categorical features CountVectorizer, HashingTF, IDF, Word2Vec, Tokenizer, RegexTokenizer, NGram, StopWordsRemover, StringIndexer, IndexToString, OneHotEncoder, TargetEncoder, FeatureHasher
    Scaling and numeric transforms StandardScaler, MaxAbsScaler, MinMaxScaler, RobustScaler, Normalizer, Binarizer, Bucketizer, QuantileDiscretizer, Imputer, DCT, PolynomialExpansion, ElementwiseProduct
    Assembly and selection VectorAssembler, VectorIndexer, VectorSizeHint, VectorSlicer, Interaction, PCA, ChiSqSelector, UnivariateFeatureSelector, VarianceThresholdSelector, RFormula, SQLTransformer
    Locality-sensitive hashing BucketedRandomProjectionLSH, MinHashLSH

    The 18 transformers build lazy ML relations, and the 20 estimators fit through Spark Connect's ML command. In Python each is a typed class with PySpark's Params API (params, getParam, the getters and setters, copy, extractParamMap), write/save/read/load; the estimators also have fitMultiple. A transformer's transform, and an estimator's fit, apply a non-empty param map to a copy as PySpark does. HashingTF.indexOf fails at PySpark's JVM import, as the official one does over Spark Connect. The outputCol default of a loaded stage is the one of the object that its load makes (<a new uid>__output), not of the saved uid, as PySpark's load keeps the defaults of that object when it takes the saved uid (_resetUid). A fit returns one of 19 typed model classes, with PySpark's members: getters and setters, attributes such as mean, vocabulary, pc and categoryMaps, DataFrames such as surrogateDF, approxNearestNeighbors, approxSimilarityJoin, getVectors and findSynonyms, and the class methods CountVectorizerModel.from_vocabulary and StringIndexerModel.from_labels and from_arrays_of_labels. Over Spark Connect, PySpark's relation decorator (try_remote_attribute_relation) drops every keyword argument of approxNearestNeighbors, approxSimilarityJoin, getVectors and findSynonyms, and ours does too, as PySpark's behavior over Spark Connect: a distCol given by keyword is ignored (the column stays distCol), and a call whose positional arguments are not all given, or of more (getVectors(1)), fails at the server, as official's does. The class methods take their arguments in PySpark's order: an empty vocabulary raises the ValueError of from_vocabulary (of its len) first; then the model of the class's constructor gets the object that the ML helper builds of its uid and of list(...) of the labels of any iterable (a str, a set, the keys of a dict), sent as PySpark's serialize sends them ((list(labels), ArrayType(StringType())) of from_labels, whose literal asserts each string), after the assertion of an active session; and then the setters of the given params, whose TypeConverters raise their TypeError after the request. QuantileDiscretizer's fit returns a Bucketizer of a new uid, with its splits and its input and output columns, of its keyword constructor, as PySpark's _create_model does, and of the defaults of the discretizer that _copyValues gives it (an outputCol of the discretizer's uid). Transformed DataFrames retain their fitted models and upstream input plans. The Rust wrapper of the discretizer's splits is named QuantileDiscretizerModel. Training-only parameters are omitted from model transforms. Multi-column parameters, nested Bucketizer splits, vector-valued ElementwiseProduct weights, NaN imputation values, and UnivariateFeatureSelector's setter-only parameters are supported. RegexTokenizer's gaps, which has no converter in PySpark, keeps its Python value, which a transform and a save send as its literal (of LiteralExpression._from_value, as PySpark's serialize_param), so that a load has it; a value of no literal raises its error at either.

    StandardScaler and MaxAbsScaler exports now refer to the native implementations. This changes the previous deprecated pyspark.ml.connect scaler behavior: pandas frames and array-valued feature columns are rejected; transforms return Spark DataFrames containing vectors. Mean centering is opt-in through withMean=True. Convert pandas data with spark.createDataFrame and array columns with array_to_vector before fitting.

    This covers constructor parameters, named setters, fit and DataFrame transform, and StopWordsRemover.loadDefaultStopWords, which reads the server's default stop words through Spark Connect's ML helper, as PySpark does (Rust StopWordsRemover::load_default_stop_words). As PySpark's constructor does, StopWordsRemover() fetches its default locale and then its English stop words of the active session, once each, so without one it raises PySpark's bare AssertionError. They are the defaults of PySpark's own __init__ (_setDefault), which the native stage takes, so a load sends its Read and then these two requests, and a copy sends none, as PySpark's. The Rust StopWordsRemover::load sends its Read alone, where PySpark's load also fetches these two defaults, in the constructor of the loaded stage: only the requests differ, since a Rust stage, new or loaded, reads an unset locale or stop_words as None or empty and the server applies its defaults.

    In Rust, each fitted model also has the members of its PySpark model class, which it reads of the server-side model as PySpark does: for example CountVectorizerModel::vocabulary, PCAModel::pc, the scaler statistics, VectorIndexerModel::category_maps, the LSH models' approx_nearest_neighbors and approx_similarity_join, and Word2VecModel::get_vectors and find_synonyms. CountVectorizerModel::from_vocabulary and StringIndexerModel::from_labels and from_arrays_of_labels build a model of given values, and QuantileDiscretizer::fit_bucketizer gives the Bucketizer of the fitted splits, as PySpark's fit does. Every stage and model saves with write() and loads with load(session, path), as PySpark's save and load. Pipeline/tuning execution is separate from this native surface. Existing vendored helper classes remain available.

    Tests compare all 38 stages with official PySpark 4.3.0 parameter literals and Spark results, including 10 multi-column/parameter variants, both constructor and setter configuration, model lifetime, invalid inputs and pandas rejection. Schema checks compare class identifiers, nullability and ML metadata; Connect omits the backing SQL schema of JVM UDTs on the wire. Run scripts/capture_ml_features_golden.py with the official client to regenerate the fixture. scripts/capture_feature_models_golden.py captures the members of every fitted model, the class methods, and the save and load of every stage, which e2e_feature_models.rs compares. Native live tests run in CI; set FEATURE_REFERENCE=1 explicitly to run their reference mode. They also check that every feature object that this client makes (a stage, its copy and load, a fit and the copy and load of its model, the models of the class methods) is of the class of its module, and that the Pipeline of pyspark.ml fits, saves and loads the stages.

    Native FPGrowth and PrefixSpan¶

    Rust ml::FPGrowth fits Spark's frequent-itemset model and returns a typed FPGrowthModel. The model exposes lazy freq_itemsets() and association_rules() DataFrames and transform() predictions. The Python classes are exported from pyspark.ml.connect and pyspark.ml.connect.fpm; fitted models expose freqItemsets and associationRules as properties. All five estimator constructor parameters have named setters; model setters cover the item column, prediction column and minimum confidence. Parameters for prediction are captured when its lazy plan is built. The itemset/rule properties fetch the fitted server model, matching standard PySpark's Connect attribute relations.

    The Python FPGrowth and FPGrowthModel follow pyspark.ml.fpm in full: the Params API (params, getParam, extractParamMap, explainParams, copy, set, clear, Param objects and getters), setParams and fitMultiple of the estimator, and write, save, read and load of both. A fitted model copies its estimator's defaults, as PySpark's _copyValues does. PrefixSpan has the same Params API, and no persistence, as Spark has none.

    Rust ml::PrefixSpan::find_frequent_sequential_patterns and Python PrefixSpan.findFrequentSequentialPatterns mine arrays of itemsets directly through the Connect helper, with all four constructor parameters and named setters. They return sequence/frequency DataFrames and have no fit/transform model.

    These APIs accept Spark DataFrames and reject pandas frames before requests; a dataset that is not a DataFrame is sent as PySpark sends it (see the argument rules above). Lazy model tables retain their cached model; PrefixSpan retains its input plan, including any upstream fitted models. A fitted model's transform of a DataFrame of another session is lazy and fails with CONNECT_ML.CACHE_INVALID when the result is analyzed or collected, as PySpark's does. In Rust, FPGrowth and FPGrowthModel save with write() and load with load(session, path), as PySpark's save and load. The vendored pyspark.ml.fpm module is unchanged.

    Spark 4.3.0's Connect helper currently rejects Long argument literals: PrefixSpan's maxLocalProjDBSize works within the signed 32-bit range, including its default 32,000,000; larger values raise the same LONG not supported server error as official PySpark. The client preserves PySpark's integer widths and explicit null parameters. Tests cover this limitation, typed string/integer itemsets, association rules, held-out predictions, model setters, sequence-length limits, invalid data, and model lifetime.

    Native ALS recommendation¶

    ml::ALS / ml::ALSModel in Rust and pyspark.ml.connect.recommendation.ALS / ALSModel in the Rust-backed Python package run Spark's standard ALS over Connect. The estimator exposes all 18 parameters, including explicit/implicit feedback, nonnegative factors, regularization, block counts, storage levels and seed. Python mirrors the 17 official constructor keywords; use setPredictionCol for an alternate prediction column, and setNumBlocks to set both block counts.

    The fitted model supports transform, rank, user/item factor tables, recommendations for all users/items, and recommendations for a subset of users/items. Rust uses user_factors, item_factors, and recommend_for_*; Python exposes userFactors, itemFactors, and recommendFor*. Model setters control transform parameters, including coldStartStrategy="drop" to remove predictions for unknown IDs. Factor and recommendation relations access the fitted server model, matching PySpark Connect. Lazy results retain their cached model and any models referenced by the subset DataFrame until evaluation completes.

    The Python ALS and ALSModel follow pyspark.ml.recommendation in full: the Params API, getters and setters, setParams, setNumBlocks, fitMultiple, copy, and write, save, read and load. A model's coldStartStrategy, userCol, itemCol and blockSize come from its estimator, as _copyValues gives them. The rank of a model is read from the server on each access, as PySpark's _call_java("rank") does, so a load sends no request for it. PySpark's fit also fetches it once, or twice when rank is set, as its _copyValues probes hasParam("rank") of the new model and so reads that property; the Python and Rust fits do not, so only the requests differ.

    Fit, transform and subset recommendation inputs must be Spark DataFrames; pandas DataFrames are rejected before conversion or RPC, and a subset's other values are sent for the server to reject, as PySpark sends them. Model operations must use the fit session for transforms: another session's DataFrame fails with CONNECT_ML.CACHE_INVALID when the transform is analyzed or collected, as PySpark's does. The subset recommendations accept another session's DataFrame and run, as PySpark's do. Official PySpark 4.3.0 fixtures cover explicit and implicit ratings, predictions, cold starts, factors, and all four recommendation methods, with live Rust and native Python tests. In Rust, ALS and ALSModel save with write() and load with load(session, path), and a loaded model reads its rank of the server, as PySpark's does. Live tests also check that the CrossValidator and TrainValidationSplit of pyspark.ml fit, save and load an ALS with a native RegressionEvaluator.

    Native ML evaluators¶

    Rust and the Rust-backed pyspark.ml.connect.evaluation exports provide all six standard Spark ML evaluators: regression, binary classification, multiclass classification, multilabel classification, clustering and ranking. Their constructor parameters and named setters follow pyspark.ml.evaluation in PySpark 4.3.0. Rust also provides typed parameter getters. Metric direction is available as is_larger_better() / isLargerBetter() without contacting Spark. pyspark.ml.connect.evaluation also exports PySpark's own pyspark.ml.evaluation.Evaluator, the base class of the six, so that it has the names of pyspark.ml.evaluation (as pyspark.ml.connect.tuning exports PySpark's ParamGridBuilder), while the Evaluator, Estimator, Transformer and Model of the pyspark.ml.connect package stay the deprecated classes of pyspark.ml.connect.base, as in PySpark's deprecated package.

    The three evaluators of upstream's deprecated pyspark.ml.connect.evaluation (regression, binary and multiclass classification), which the native classes of this module replace, use standard Spark ML defaults: BinaryClassificationEvaluator takes rawPredictionCol="rawPrediction", and MulticlassClassificationEvaluator defaults to metricName="f1" and requires keyword arguments. Use rawPredictionCol / setRawPredictionCol instead of the deprecated probabilityCol / setProbabilityCol binary-evaluator argument.

    Evaluation sends Spark DataFrames and their column names/types directly to Spark. A pandas DataFrame raises the AttributeError of its own sparkSession, as pyspark.ml does, before serialization or RPC; a model's summary evaluate raises the TypeError of pyspark.ml instead. Prepare double prediction columns, vector scores, and arrays of doubles where required by the corresponding standard evaluator. Clustering features may be vectors or arrays of doubles. Evaluators no longer silently cast columns, convert array scores to vectors, or normalize field-name casing.

    Official PySpark fixtures and live Rust/Python tests cover every supported metric, weights, regression through the origin, binary score bins, multiclass log-loss clipping and beta, per-label metrics, both silhouette distances, ranking cutoffs, invalid inputs, and metric direction. In Rust, each evaluator saves with write() and loads with load(session, path), as PySpark's, and set_uid sets its uid, as PySpark's _resetUid does. In Python, each evaluator has PySpark's Params API, its getters and setters, setParams, and write/save/read/load, which keep its uid and params. evaluate(dataset, params) with a non-empty param map evaluates a copy of the evaluator, as PySpark's Evaluator.evaluate does, and leaves the evaluator as it was. scripts/capture_evaluation_api_cases_golden.py captures these cases from official PySpark.

    scripts/capture_fpm_recommendation_evaluation_golden.py captures the members of the FPGrowth and ALS models and of the evaluators, and their save and load, of the configurations of these fixtures, which e2e_fpm_recommendation_evaluation.rs compares.

    Native ML statistics¶

    Rust's ml::{ChiSquareTest, Correlation, KolmogorovSmirnovTest, Summarizer, SummaryBuilder, MultivariateGaussian} and the native Python classes in pyspark.ml.stat cover the standard ML statistics APIs from PySpark 4.3.0. The Python module is a Rust-backed shim, replacing its vendored implementation, except MultivariateGaussian, which stays PySpark's plain Python class. pyspark.ml.connect adds no statistics module; its summarizer is the one of PySpark's deprecated package, unchanged.

    The module has PySpark's names and no __all__, as PySpark's, so from pyspark.ml.stat import * imports the same names (the names of its imports too, such as Column and JavaWrapper). ChiSquareTest, Correlation, KolmogorovSmirnovTest and Summarizer construct as PySpark's plain classes: of no arguments (ChiSquareTest() takes no arguments) unless a subclass has an __init__ of its own, of attributes and weak references, and pickled and copied as plain objects. SummaryBuilder is a JavaWrapper of PySpark's __init__(self, jSummaryBuilder), which keeps no Java object over Spark Connect: its summary reads the _metrics of its attributes, which Summarizer.metrics sets with a _java_obj of None, so SummaryBuilder(None).summary(col) raises PySpark's AttributeError, and a subclass that sets _metrics summarizes of them. The classes have PySpark's docstrings, and their static methods (and SummaryBuilder.summary) are functions of the module of PySpark's signatures, annotations and docstrings (Summarizer.mean.__module__ is pyspark.ml.stat, and its docstring has the .. versionadded:: 2.4.0 of PySpark's since), over the native methods, so inspect and help give PySpark's and a call binds as PySpark's does (SummaryBuilder.summary() takes from 2 to 3 positional arguments but 4 were given); Summarizer._check_param and _get_single_metric are PySpark's code over Spark Connect. The annotations name PySpark's classic Column of pyspark.sql.column, as PySpark's do, of which the Columns of a session are instances, and the classes are subclasses of the native classes, which their MROs name after them (ChiSquareTest, ChiSquareTest, object), where PySpark's are plain classes.

    The statistical tests return lazy Spark DataFrames. Chi-square supports both the vector result and one row per feature (flatten=True); correlation supports Pearson and Spearman; the KS test supports Spark's normal distribution, with optional mean and standard deviation. Spark validates column types and statistical arguments. As with pyspark.ml.stat, the dataset and the other arguments are serialized as the arguments of a helper call, in that order, before any RPC: a pandas DataFrame raises PySpark's UNSUPPORTED_DATA_TYPE, and another argument that cannot be a literal raises the error of its conversion. A literal of another type than the helper takes, such as a dataset that is not a DataFrame or a column name that is not a str, is sent as PySpark sends it, and the server rejects it when the result is analyzed or collected (tests/golden/statistics_arguments.json records these cases of official PySpark). A (value, DataType) pair is sent as the literal of that type, as PySpark sends it, and raises the AssertionError of PySpark's LiteralExpression(value, dataType) of a value or type that a literal does not take. The KS test converts its parameters with float() first, as PySpark does. Results retain any fitted models used by their input plans.

    Summarizer.metrics(...).summary(features, weight) builds a SQL aggregate that works in select and grouped aggregations. All ten metrics are supported: mean, sum, variance, std, count, numNonZeros, max, min, normL1, and normL2. Each also has a static convenience method; Rust uses snake_case (num_non_zeros, norm_l1, norm_l2). Omitted weights default to 1.0. Inputs are ML vector Columns, including sparse vectors. Metric names are case sensitive; numNonZeros uses a capital Z. An empty metric selection returns an empty struct.

    MultivariateGaussian is a mutable mean/covariance holder, with no computation or extra validation. Rust uses MlVector and MlMatrix (dense or sparse storage, the matrix type of the native ML params). Python keeps PySpark's plain class, whose instances hold the original vector and matrix objects and pickle as PySpark's. Python results materialize vectors and matrices through the existing ML UDT conversion.

    Reference protobuf fixtures and live Rust/Python tests cover every metric with and without weights, grouped summaries, chi-square flattening, both correlation methods, KS parameters, constant-feature NaNs, invalid inputs, and lazy model ownership. Python tests additionally compare sparse and dense inputs. Native classes are asserted explicitly; the tests also run separately with official PySpark as the reference.
